# THE ADDERWOOD CHRONICLES

## Part One: The World That Wasn't

---

# Chapter One: The Knock

The rain hadn't stopped for three days.

Finch Adderwood sat cross-legged on the living room floor with a colouring book spread open between her knees, the wax crayons scattered around her like fallen soldiers. She was eight years old and she had already decided that purple was the superior colour for drawing horses, and no one in the entire world was going to convince her otherwise.

Her mother, June, was in the kitchen. Finch could hear the clatter of pots and the low hum of a song she didn't know the name of. The kitchen light spilled through the doorway in a warm rectangle that cut across the carpet, and Finch kept glancing at it the way she always did when her mother was cooking — half-expecting her to appear in the doorway with a wooden spoon and a look of exaggerated seriousness, asking Finch to come taste something.

But tonight June didn't appear. Tonight the kitchen sounds kept going, steady and unbroken, and Finch went back to her horse. It was going to be purple with a green mane. She had already decided this.

The knock came at 6:47 in the evening.

It wasn't the kind of knock you heard every day. It wasn't the postman or the neighbour from down the street or one of Mum's friends popping in unannounced. It was the kind of knock that had weight behind it. Two sharp raps on the front door, followed by a pause, followed by a third. Like someone had rehearsed it.

June turned off the stove. The song stopped.

Finch looked up from her colouring book. Something had changed in the quality of the air, the way it does when someone in the house goes very still. She heard her mother's footsteps cross the kitchen floor — slower than usual, heavier — and the creak of the hallway floorboard just before the front door.

Through the gap beneath the colouring book, Finch could see her mother's feet stop. Could see the hem of her blue dress. Could see the way she stood there for a long moment without reaching for the handle.

"Mrs. Adderwood?" said a voice from the other side of the door. A man's voice. Deep and measured and careful in the way that voices are careful when they've been trained to deliver bad news.

June opened the door.

Finch couldn't see the man's face from where she was sitting, but she could see his shoes. Black leather boots, polished to a dull shine, wet from the rain. She could see the bottom edge of a dark blue uniform, and she could see the way her mother's hand tightened on the doorframe.

"Mrs. Adderwood, my name is Sergeant Paul O'Brien. I'm from the New South Wales Police Force. May I come in?"

What followed was a conversation that Finch would replay in her mind ten thousand times over the next eight years. She would rearrange the words and try different inflections and search for hidden meanings the way you search for shapes in clouds, but the shape of the conversation never changed. It was always the same. It was always the worst thing she had ever heard.

Sergeant O'Brien sat at their kitchen table. June made him a cup of tea because that was what you did, even when the world was falling apart, you made a cup of tea. Finch sat in the doorway between the kitchen and the hallway with her colouring book still clutched to her chest, and she watched the policeman's mouth move.

The truck had left the road at high speed on the Pacific Highway, about forty minutes north of Sydney. It had crossed the median strip and struck a tree — a massive ironbark — at an angle that wrapped the chassis around the trunk like tinfoil. The engine had been pushed into the cabin. There was almost nothing left of the cab.

June's face was very white. She held the teacup in both hands but she wasn't drinking from it.

The strangest thing — and Sergeant O'Brien used that word, *strange*, which was the wrong word for what he was about to say, the completely and utterly wrong word — was that there were no remains at the scene.

No remains.

The truck was destroyed. The driver's side was compressed to a third of its original width. There was blood on the steering column and on the shattered glass of the windshield. But there was no body. No body in the cab, no body on the road, no body in the grass verge or the tree line. The paramedics had arrived within minutes. The police had searched a two-kilometre radius. The dogs had picked up nothing.

Caleb Adderwood had simply ceased to exist.

"His work ID was still in the glove compartment," Sergeant O'Brien said. "His wallet was in his back pocket. The wedding ring—" He stopped. Looked down at his tea. "The wedding ring was on the dashboard."

June made a sound that Finch had never heard before. It was a very small sound, almost nothing, but it went through Finch like a nail.

The policeman left eventually. The rain kept going. June sat at the kitchen table for a long time, and Finch sat in the doorway, and neither of them moved. Eventually Finch got up and went to her mother and put her arms around her waist because that was as high as she could reach, and June put her hand on the back of Finch's head and held her there.

That was the night Finch Adderwood's father died.

Except he hadn't.

---

Eight years is a long time when you're young.

Eight years took Finch from primary school to high school, from a small body to a tall one, from childhood to whatever awkward thing she was now — sixteen, long-limbed, with a shock of lavender hair that she'd dyed herself in the bathroom using a kit she'd bought from the chemist. Her mother hated it. Finch loved it. It was the colour of the flowers in the garden that Caleb had planted the spring before he disappeared, and some part of her — the part she didn't like to examine too closely — kept it that way on purpose.

She was tall. Taller than most of the girls in her year, taller than half the boys, with the kind of gangly frame that suggested she might still have some growing left to do. Her face was angular, her jaw defined, her eyes a pale grey-blue that people sometimes called silver when the light hit them right. She had her father's eyes, her mother used to say, before she stopped saying much of anything at all.

June Adderwood had not handled the years well. She had handled them the way some people handle grief — by becoming smaller. She still went to work, still paid the bills, still made dinner most nights, but the light behind her eyes had gone out and Finch had never seen it come back on. They lived in the same house in Brookvale, on the same street, in the same room Finch had always slept in. Caleb's things were still in the garage. His boots by the back door. His flannel jacket hanging on the hook in the hallway. June couldn't move them. Finch couldn't ask her to.

School was school. Finch was not unpopular, exactly, but she was not the centre of anything either. She had a small orbit of friends — Priya, who was obsessed with marine biology; Ollie, who played guitar badly and enthusiastically; and Tomoko, who was the only person Finch had ever met who read more books than she did. They ate lunch together in the library most days, and they talked about normal things, and Finch was normal.

Mostly.

Sometimes she woke up at three in the morning with a feeling she couldn't name. A sense of something unfinished, like a sentence that cut off mid-word. She would lie in bed and stare at the ceiling and feel the shape of her father's absence like a missing tooth, her tongue going back to it again and again.

She dreamed about him. Not often, but enough. In the dreams he was always just out of reach — standing at the end of a long hallway, or sitting in the cab of his truck with the window up, or walking away from her across a field that went on forever. She would call out to him and he would turn, and she would see his face, and then she would wake up.

The morning everything changed started like any other.

Finch's alarm went off at 6:30. She hit snooze. It went off again at 6:39. She hit snooze again. The third time, she actually opened her eyes.

The ceiling was wrong.

Not wrong in a dramatic way. Not cracked or water-stained or on fire. It was simply a different ceiling. It was flat and white and featureless, and it was very close to her face, much closer than the ceiling in her bedroom, which was a standard two-point-four metres above her bed. This ceiling was maybe a metre away. She could have reached up and touched it.

Finch blinked. She was not in her bed. She was lying on something hard and cold, and the sky above her was pale and grey and enormous, and the ceiling she had been looking at was not a ceiling at all.

It was the underside of a highway overpass.

She sat up. Her head throbbed. Her mouth was dry and tasted like copper. She was lying on asphalt — a road surface that was cracked and buckled, with weeds pushing through the gaps. There were lane markings beneath her, faded yellow lines, and she was lying directly across one of them.

To her left, the highway stretched into the distance, empty. To her right, the same. There were no cars. There were no trucks. There was no sound at all except the wind, which moved through the empty air with a low, mournful quality, as though it had been blowing like this for a very long time and expected to go on blowing forever.

Finch got to her feet.

She was wearing what she'd gone to bed in — an oversized t-shirt with a faded print of a band she no longer listened to, cotton pyjama shorts, and no shoes. Her feet were cold on the asphalt. Her lavender hair was tangled and matted on one side from sleep.

The overpass she was beneath spanned a two-lane road that ran perpendicular to the highway. On either side of the road, the land was overgrown — long grass, scrubby bushes, the skeletal remains of a fence. In the distance, she could see buildings. Or what was left of them.

Finch turned in a slow circle. The highway was littered with abandoned vehicles — cars, trucks, a bus — all of them stopped at random angles, some with their doors open, some with their windows shattered. None of them looked recently abandoned. Rust had settled on the exposed metal. Vegetation had begun to reclaim the tyres. One car, a white sedan, had a tree growing through its engine bay, the trunk pushing up through the hood like a fist through paper.

"What the fuck," Finch whispered.

She said it quietly, almost conversationally, as though she were commenting on the weather. Because what else do you say when you go to sleep in your bedroom in Brookvale and wake up on an empty highway with the world apparently ended?

Her first thought was that she was dreaming. Her second thought was that she was hallucinating. Her third thought — the one that came in cold and clear and stayed — was that something had happened. Something enormous and terrible and completely beyond her understanding.

She looked at the sky. It was overcast, the clouds low and grey, and there was a faint smell in the air that she couldn't immediately identify. It took her a moment. It was the smell of decay. Not the sharp, fresh smell of something recently dead, but the deep, pervasive odour of organic matter breaking down over a long period of time.

Everything smelled like that. The grass, the trees, the air itself.

Finch began to walk.

She chose a direction — toward the buildings she could see in the distance — because staying in one place felt wrong, instinctively wrong, the way standing still feels wrong when you hear a noise in the dark. Her bare feet hurt on the rough asphalt. She picked her way between the abandoned cars, glancing into them as she passed. A child's car seat in the back of a minivan. A phone on the dashboard of a sedan, its screen cracked. A pair of sunglasses hanging from the rear-view mirror of a ute.

She found a pair of thongs — flip-flops, if you were from anywhere else — in the footwell of one of the cars. They were a size too big, but they were better than nothing. She slipped them on and kept walking.

The buildings resolved themselves as she got closer. A service station with its pumps ripped out and its windows dark. A fast-food restaurant with its sign hanging by one bolt. A motel — the kind of low, sprawling, single-storey affair that catered to truck drivers — with every one of its doors hanging open like rows of empty mouths.

There was no one in any of them.

Finch stood in the car park of the service station and looked at the devastation around her and tried to make her brain work properly. The world hadn't just ended. It had ended a long time ago. Months, maybe years. The vegetation growth alone told her that. The decay. The way nature was slowly, patiently dismantling everything that humans had built.

She didn't cry. She wanted to. She could feel the crying sitting in her chest like a stone, but it wouldn't come, and she was almost grateful for that, because if she started she didn't think she would be able to stop.

Instead, she looked for supplies.

The service station was mostly stripped. The shelves behind the counter had been emptied long ago — no food, no water, no batteries, nothing. But in a storeroom at the back, behind a door that had been kicked in, she found a backpack. It was a grey hiking pack, dusty but functional, with both straps intact. She also found a water bottle — stainless steel, reusable — and a first aid kit that had been half-used but still contained bandages, antiseptic, and a packet of paracetamol.

She filled the water bottle from a tap that, impossibly, still worked — she turned the handle and water came out, brown at first and then clear — and she put everything in the backpack and slung it over her shoulders.

The motel yielded more. A hoodie — dark blue, too large, which meant it was perfect. A pair of trainers that were close enough to her size. A kitchen knife from what had apparently been someone's room, still sitting on the nightstand next to a stack of mouldering paperback books. She took the knife. She took a torch that still worked, the beam flickering but present. She took a tin of baked beans and a can opener from a miniature kitchen in one of the rooms, and she sat on the edge of a bed that smelled like mildew and ate the beans cold with the back of a spoon and tried not to think about where all the people had gone.

She had been walking for about three hours when she heard the sound.

It was a groan. Low and guttural and sustained, like someone in terrible pain. It came from somewhere ahead of her, beyond the shell of a building that might have been a warehouse. Finch stopped. Her hand went to the knife at her belt — she'd fashioned a belt from a strip of fabric and looped it through the handle.

The groan came again. And then another one joined it. And another.

Finch moved toward the sound, slowly, keeping close to the wall of the warehouse. She reached the corner and looked around it and saw them.

There were three of them.

They had been people once. That much was obvious. They had the same basic shape — two arms, two legs, upright posture. But whatever they were now, they were not people any more. Their skin was a greyish colour, pulled tight over their bones in some places and hanging loose in others. Their clothes — what was left of them — were torn and stained with substances Finch didn't want to identify. Their eyes were open but they didn't seem to see. They stood in the middle of the road, swaying slightly, making those low, continuous groaning sounds.

Finch's stomach turned. Her heart rate doubled. Every instinct in her body was screaming at her to run, but she didn't run. She stood very still and watched them.

One of them turned its head. Not quickly — nothing about them was quick. It turned its head the way a clock turns, slow and mechanical, and its milky eyes found the corner of the building where Finch was standing, and it groaned louder, and it began to move toward her.

Finch ran.

She ran harder than she had ever run in her life, the backpack bouncing against her spine, the thongs slapping against her heels, the knife banging against her hip. Behind her, she could hear them — not running, they didn't seem capable of running, but shuffling, dragging themselves forward with a horrible, relentless persistence.

She ran until she couldn't hear them any more. She ran until her lungs burned and her legs ached and the buildings had given way to open country. Then she stopped, bent double, hands on her knees, and gasped for air.

And that was when she heard the voice.

"Hey! Hey, don't move! Stay right where you are!"

A man. Young — younger than she expected. He was standing on a rise about fifty metres ahead of her, backlit by the grey sky. He had something in his hands. A weapon of some kind. He was watching her with the kind of intense focus that comes from years of having to decide very quickly whether someone was a threat.

Finch raised her hands slowly. "I'm not—" she started. Her voice came out cracked and dry. "I'm not one of them."

The man didn't lower his weapon. "How long you been out here?"

"I don't know. I just — I woke up on the highway. I don't know what happened. I don't know what any of this is."

The man stared at her for a long moment. Then he lowered the weapon — a crossbow, she realised, a compact, modern-looking thing — and took a few steps closer. He was maybe twenty-five, lean and sun-darkened, with close-cropped hair and a scar that ran from his left ear to his jaw.

"You don't know," he repeated. Not a question.

"No."

He looked at her for another long moment, and then he did something strange. He laughed. It wasn't a cruel laugh, or a hysterical one. It was the laugh of a man who had seen enough strange things in the world that one more didn't even register on his scale any more.

"Alright," he said. "Alright, come with me. My name's Liam. And you, mate, have got one hell of a story ahead of you."

---

# Chapter Two: Hollows

They walked east, away from the open highway and into the tangled remains of suburbia. Liam moved with the easy, practised efficiency of someone who had been navigating this landscape for a long time — stepping over debris without looking down, ducking beneath hanging wires, taking turns at intersections that all looked the same to Finch but apparently weren't.

He carried the crossbow across his chest and a machete at his hip, and he walked with a slight limp that favoured his right leg. He didn't explain the limp. He didn't explain much of anything at first. He just walked, and Finch walked beside him, and the silence between them was thick with everything she needed to know.

Eventually, as they picked their way through what had once been a residential street — houses with their doors hanging open, gardens gone to seed, a playground with rusted swings — Liam began to talk.

"What year do you think it is?" he asked.

Finch blinked. "What?"

"Humour me."

"Twenty twenty-four. It's — it's June. Early June."

Liam nodded slowly. "It's twenty thirty-one."

The number hit her like cold water. "That's — no. That's seven years. You're telling me I've been asleep for seven years?"

"Not asleep. Not exactly. But yeah, you've missed a bit." He glanced at her sideways. "What's the last thing you remember?"

Finch thought about it. The last thing. The very last thing before waking up on the highway. She had been in bed. It had been night. Her alarm was set for six-thirty. She had pulled the covers up and closed her eyes and—

"That's it," she said. "I went to sleep. I went to sleep in my bed in Brookvale, and I woke up on the highway."

Liam stopped walking. He looked at her with an expression she couldn't read — something between disbelief and a very cautious, very careful kind of hope.

"Brookvale," he said. "Brookvale, Sydney."

"Yes."

"That's — Finch, that's two hundred kilometres from where I found you."

"I don't know how. I don't know any of this. I just—"

"Okay." He held up a hand. "Okay. We'll figure it out. But right now I need you to listen to me very carefully, because we're about to go through an area where they tend to congregate, and if you make a sound, they will find us."

"Who is they?"

Liam looked at her. "You saw them. The ones on the road. The groaning ones."

"Yeah."

"They're called Hollows. Nobody knows exactly what to call them, but that's what most people have settled on, because that's what they are. Hollow. There's nothing left inside."

He told her as they walked. He told her in short, clipped sentences, the way you'd relay information to someone who needed it fast and couldn't afford to be gentle.

It had started seven years ago. A virus. Not a natural one — it had been engineered, or at least it had originated in a laboratory. Qwantum Labs. The name meant nothing to Finch, but Liam said it like a curse word. A biotech company, massive, well-funded, with a campus on the outskirts of Sydney. Nobody knew exactly what they were working on. The official story — the one that had been in the news before the news stopped — was that it was a respiratory illness. An accident. A containment breach.

But that wasn't what it actually was.

"It wasn't a bug," Liam said. "Not the way you think of a bug. It didn't just make you sick. It got into your brain. Rewired you. Turned you into something that wasn't you any more. Something that didn't think, didn't speak, didn't do anything except hunger."

"Hunger for what?"

"Anything. Everything. Flesh, mostly. They don't eat in the normal sense — they tear, they rip, they consume. But the hunger isn't really about food. It's about — I don't know. Spreading. Destruction. There's no logic to it. No pattern. They just want."

"And it spread?"

"Like wildfire. Within a month, Sydney was gone. Within three months, the east coast was gone. Within a year—" He stopped. Swallowed. "Within a year, there was nobody left who wasn't either a Hollow or hiding from them."

They were passing through a park now, or what had been a park. The grass was waist-high and the trees had grown wild, their branches tangling together overhead to form a canopy that blocked out the grey light. Finch could hear birds — actual birds, alive and singing — and the sound was so incongruous with everything else that it made her chest ache.

"How are you still alive?" she asked.

Liam smiled. It was a thin smile, the kind that doesn't reach the eyes. "Luck, mostly. And a healthy respect for the fact that Hollows are stupid. They can't open doors. They can't climb. They can't reason. They're attracted to sound and movement, but they can't plan. They're like — imagine a toddler with the strength of a grown man and the moral compass of a blender."

"And they were people."

"Yeah." The smile vanished. "They were people."

They crossed the park and entered another street, and Liam led her to a building that had been a church. The doors were barricaded from the inside with welded steel, and there was a peephole at head height. Liam knocked — a specific pattern, three taps and a pause and two more — and the door opened a crack. An eye appeared. Then the door opened wider, and Liam gestured for Finch to go inside.

The church had been converted into a waystation. Pews pushed against the walls, replaced by sleeping mats and storage crates. A water collection system made from gutters and tarps. A small kitchen area with a gas stove and a stack of propane canisters. It was cramped and dim and it smelled like camphor and dust, and it was the safest place Finch had seen since waking up.

There were no other people there. Just a space that Liam used, apparently, when he was ranging too far from home to make it back before dark.

"Home?" Finch asked.

"I'll get to that. Sit down. Eat something." He tossed her a packet of something — dried fruit, the preservative-heavy kind that would outlast the apocalypse — and poured her a cup of water from a jerrycan. "You need to understand what's out there before I take you anywhere."

He sat across from her, the crossbow propped against the wall beside him, and he talked.

The Hollows were the obvious threat. They roamed in groups — packs, almost — ranging from a handful to dozens. They were drawn to light, to sound, to the scent of living flesh. During the day they were sluggish, almost torpid, standing still and groaning in that aimless way. At night they moved faster. Not fast, not by any normal standard, but fast enough that you didn't want to be caught in the open.

"There are worse things than Hollows," Liam said.

"What could be worse than that?"

"People."

He told her about the groups. The survivors. They had formed in the early days, banding together for safety, and most of them had been what you'd expect — scared, desperate, trying to help each other. But seven years was a long time, and seven years of fear and scarcity did things to people. Some groups had become territorial. Aggressive. They fought over resources, over territory, over control.

"The two big ones around here are the Junkyard and the Black Vultures," Liam said. "The Junkyard is — well, you'll see. They're mostly decent. The Black Vultures are not."

He didn't elaborate. He just shook his head in a way that suggested he'd seen things he didn't want to describe.

"And then there's Avarice."

He said the name differently. With a weight that wasn't there for the other groups.

"What's Avarice?" Finch asked.

Liam looked at her for a long moment. Something flickered across his face — she couldn't tell if it was fear or anger or both.

"Avarice is the reason nobody sleeps at night," he said. "They're — look, most groups, even the bad ones, they steal because they have to. They fight because they have to. Avarice is different. They kill because they want to. They steal because they enjoy it. They're not surviving — they're playing. And they're good at it."

"How many of them?"

"Nobody knows. They don't stay in one place. They move like a storm — they show up somewhere, tear it apart, and move on. They've got this symbol — an 'A' with a circle around it, burned into everything they touch. Walls, doors, cars, people."

Finch felt a chill that had nothing to do with the temperature. "And they're out there now?"

"They're always out there."

Liam stood up and began packing gear into a bag. He moved with the same practised efficiency as before — checking blades, topping off water containers, testing the tension on his crossbow.

"I'm going to take you somewhere safe," he said. "There's a group — the Junkyard I mentioned. They've got walls, they've got people, they've got food. I've traded with them before. They'll take you in."

"What about you?"

"I'll be around. I range too far to stay anywhere permanent. But I can get you there, and I can vouch for you, and that's the best I can do."

It wasn't much. It wasn't a home, or a family, or a world that made sense. But it was something, and right now something was all Finch needed.

They left the church as the light was beginning to fade. Liam moved quickly now, with an urgency that hadn't been there before, and Finch kept pace without being told twice. The Hollows would be more active soon. They needed to reach the Junkyard before dark.

They moved through streets that were dark and silent and vast, the buildings looming on either side like cliffs. Once, Finch saw a shape moving in the shadows between two houses — a Hollow, shambling aimlessly, its groan a low drone beneath the wind. Liam held up a fist and Finch stopped. They waited. The Hollow moved on.

They moved again.

The city fell away gradually, replaced by industrial sprawl — warehouses, freight yards, a railyard with trains sitting motionless on their tracks like metal carcasses. The Junkyard was ahead. Finch could see it — a sprawling compound of corrugated iron and chain-link and concrete, lit from within by the warm glow of fires and lanterns. It looked, from a distance, like something from a Mad Max movie. Up close, it looked like hope.

"We're going to approach the main gate," Liam said. "There'll be guards. They'll be watching us from about a hundred metres back. Don't make any sudden moves. Don't touch anything. And whatever you do, don't run."

"Who are the guards?"

"John and his wife. Good people. Tough, but good."

They walked toward the gate. Finch could see it now — a heavy iron affair, welded from truck parts, flanked by two towers made of stacked shipping containers. A light shone from the top of the left tower, and she could see a silhouette behind it, watching them approach.

"Hold it right there!" A voice, deep and steady, carried across the distance. "Stop where you are and identify yourselves!"

Liam raised his hands. "It's Liam! Liam Cross! I've got a girl with me — she's a survivor, she's not a threat!"

There was a pause. Then the voice again: "Drop your weapons. Both of you. On the ground. Step back."

Liam looked at Finch. "Do as he says."

She pulled the kitchen knife from her belt and set it on the ground. Liam lowered his crossbow and set it beside the machete he'd unstrapped from his hip. They both stepped back.

The gate opened a crack, and a man stepped through. He was in his fifties, broad-shouldered, with a grey beard and eyes that missed nothing. He had a shotgun cradled in his arms, not pointed at them, but ready. Behind him, a woman appeared — shorter, younger, with dark hair pulled back in a braid and the same watchful eyes.

"Liam," he said. "Marta."

---

# Chapter Three: The Interview

"John," Liam said. "Marta. This is Finch. She woke up on the highway this morning with nothing. She's not infected. She's not a Vulture. She's just a kid who needs help."

John looked at Finch. He looked at her for a long time, the way you'd look at an animal that had wandered into your yard and you weren't sure if it was friendly. His eyes moved over her — the lavender hair, the oversized hoodie, the thongs that were a size too big, the backpack that she clutched with both hands as though it were the only solid thing in a dissolving world. She could feel him cataloguing her, the way a buyer catalogues goods at auction, measuring her weight against some internal scale of usefulness and danger.

"Inside," he said finally. "She can leave her stuff at the gate. We'll need to check her."

"I'm not leaving my bag," Finch said, and she was surprised by how steady her voice came out. It didn't feel steady. It felt like a wire pulled taut, vibrating with the frequency of exhaustion and fear and the particular, defiant pride of someone who had nothing left to lose except the things she was holding.

John looked at her again. Then, very slightly, he smiled.

"Alright, then. Keep your bag. But you're going to have a conversation with Mara before you go any further."

He stepped aside. Marta unbarred the gate — a heavy iron thing, welded from truck parts and reinforced with chains that clinked like wind chimes as they fell away. The gate swung inward with a sound that was part metal, part protest, and Finch Adderwood walked into the Junkyard.

The Junkyard was larger than it had appeared from the outside.

Beyond the gate, the compound opened up into a sprawling, chaotic, surprisingly organised labyrinth of structures. Shipping containers stacked two and three high, repurposed into dwellings, their corrugated walls painted with names and symbols in colours that had faded in the sun. Tents and tarp shelters stitched together with wire and hope occupied the spaces between, their guy-lines anchored to anything heavy enough to hold — engine blocks, concrete chunks, a shopping trolley filled with stones. Vehicles — cars, trucks, buses — stripped of their useful parts and reassembled into walls, towers, and what appeared to be a communal kitchen. A windmill turned slowly on the roof of the tallest structure, its blades creaking in the wind, powering something Finch couldn't see.

The air smelled of woodsmoke and metal and the faint, acrid tang of diesel. It was a working smell, a living smell, the smell of a place where people had decided to stop hiding and start building. Finch breathed it in and felt something in her chest loosen — not trust, not yet, but the absence of the constant, grinding tension she had carried since waking on the highway.

People moved through the spaces between. Not many — maybe fifty, she estimated, though it was hard to count in the dim light. They glanced at her as she passed, some with curiosity, some with suspicion, most with the blank, guarded expressions of people who had learned not to invest emotionally in strangers. A woman carrying a bucket of water looked up as Finch passed, then looked away. A man welding something behind a screen of corrugated iron paused to watch, the blue-white light of his torch throwing sharp shadows across his face before he turned back to his work. Two children — a boy and a girl, neither older than ten — stood on a stack of tyres and stared at Finch with the unblinking intensity of small animals.

John led her through a narrow corridor formed by two rows of containers and into an open area at the centre of the compound. A fire pit dominated the space, ringed by stones and fed by a pile of scrap wood that someone had sawn into manageable lengths. The fire was large — larger than a cooking fire, larger than a warming fire — the kind of fire that served as a beacon, a meeting point, a declaration that we are here and we are not going anywhere. Its light pushed back the darkness in a thirty-metre radius, painting everything in shades of amber and deep shadow.

A few people sat around it, warming their hands, their faces lit amber by the flames. They looked up as Finch approached. One of them — a young man, maybe twenty, with a shaved head and a scar on his chin — began to stand, but John waved him back down with a gesture that was casual but carried the weight of authority. The young man sat. His eyes followed Finch as she passed, and she felt them like a physical touch.

"Wait here," John said. "Mara will come to you."

He left. Finch stood alone in the firelight, her backpack hanging from one shoulder, the kitchen knife somewhere in the straps, and she waited.

The fire was warm. It was the first real warmth she'd felt since waking on the highway — not the grey, ambient chill of an overcast day, but the deep, radiating heat of burning wood, the kind of warmth that found the cracks in your clothing and the hollows of your body and filled them. She turned toward it instinctively, holding her hands out, feeling the heat on her palms, and for a moment she was not in a post-apocalyptic compound or a world that had ended. She was just cold, and the fire was warm, and that was enough.

She looked around. The compound was busy, even at this hour. People moved with purpose — carrying things, fixing things, talking in low clusters. She saw a woman hanging laundry on a line strung between two containers, the clothes dark shapes against the evening sky. She saw a man sharpening a blade on a whetstone, the rhythmic scrape of steel on stone carrying across the compound like a heartbeat. She saw someone on a platform above the containers — a watchtower, maybe, or a lookout — silhouetted against the sky, scanning the darkness beyond the walls.

She noticed, too, the details of construction. The containers weren't just stacked — they were joined, bolted together, with doorways cut through their walls to create connected rooms. Walkways had been welded between levels, made from steel grating that let the rain through. Stairs — some salvaged, some improvised from truck parts — connected the ground to the upper levels. It was crude, but it was functional, and the cumulative effect was something that looked less like a ruin and more like a village. A strange, angular, metallic village, but a village nonetheless.

She didn't wait long.

Mara emerged from one of the container structures, moving with the kind of purposeful stride that suggested she was always on her way somewhere and simply tolerating the spaces in between. She was perhaps forty, with sharp features and dark hair threaded with grey, cut short in a way that was practical rather than fashionable. She wore practical clothes — cargo pants, boots, a dark jacket — and she carried herself with the authority of someone who had earned it rather than been given it. There was a knife on her belt — not a kitchen knife, not an improvised weapon, but a proper blade, well-maintained, with a sheath that had been oiled recently. She walked like someone who knew exactly where every exit was.

She stopped in front of Finch and looked at her. It was the same kind of look John had given her, but sharper. More comprehensive. Like she was reading a book very quickly and deciding whether to keep it. Her eyes moved over Finch's face, her hair, her clothes, her posture, the way she held her bag. Finch felt herself being measured, assessed, categorised, and she stood very still and let it happen because she had no energy left for anything else.

"I'm Mara," she said. "Sit down."

There was a log near the fire pit. Finch sat. The log was rough and damp and she didn't care. Mara remained standing, which put her at a height advantage that Finch suspected was deliberate.

"Tell me everything," Mara said. "Start from the beginning."

So Finch did.

She talked for a long time. She talked about the knock at the door when she was eight. The policeman. The truck wrapped around the tree. The eight years of nothing — the grief, the mother who shrank, the father who was absent, the house that was full of his ghost. She talked about waking up on the highway, the Hollows, Liam, the church, the walk to the Junkyard. She talked about her mother, and her father, and her lavender hair, and the colouring book with the purple horse, and all of it came out in a rush that she couldn't have stopped if she'd wanted to.

As she talked, she was aware of the people around the fire. The young man with the scar on his chin was watching her, his chin resting on his hand, his expression unreadable. An older woman — grey-haired, heavyset, with hands that looked like they could bend steel — had stopped warming her hands and was listening with her head tilted slightly, the way you tilt your head when you're hearing something you recognise. A man with a bandage around his forearm leaned forward, his elbows on his knees, his face a study in the particular expression of people who are listening to someone else's story and measuring it against their own.

Mara didn't interrupt. She didn't ask questions. She just listened, her face unreadable, her arms crossed over her chest, her weight shifting occasionally from one foot to the other. When Finch finally ran out of words — when the story reached the present and there was nothing left but the fire and the dark and the sound of her own breathing — she stood in silence and stared at the flames.

The fire cracked. A log shifted, sending a shower of sparks into the sky. They rose like orange stars and vanished into the darkness above.

"So you have no idea what year it is," Mara said finally.

"Until today, I thought it was twenty twenty-four."

"And you've never heard of Qwantum Labs."

"No."

"Or Avarice."

"No. Liam mentioned them on the way here, but I've never—"

"You know, most people who show up at this gate come in one of two flavours. Either they've been out there surviving on their own and they're half-dead and desperate, or they're running from something and they're terrified. You're neither of those things." Mara paused. "You're something I've never seen before."

"I'm telling you the truth."

"I believe you. That's the problem." Mara looked at her for another long moment. Then she turned and walked away, and Finch thought for one terrible moment that she was leaving — that the conversation was over and the answer was no and she was going to be sent back out into the dark.

The firelight flickered. The people around it shifted, their faces momentarily shadowed. Finch sat on the log and felt the cold creep back in, filling the space where Mara's presence had been. She thought about the highway. She thought about the Hollows. She thought about the dark beyond the gate and what it would mean to walk back into it.

But Mara came back.

She came back with a bowl of something — rice and vegetables, still steaming — and a bottle of water, and she set them on the log next to Finch. The bowl was metal, dented, the rim chipped in two places. The rice was white and slightly overcooked, each grain soft and swollen. The vegetables were whatever had been available — chunks of carrot, strips of something green that might have been silverbeet, a few pale cubes of potato. They were glistening with oil or butter, and the steam that rose from them carried a smell that made Finch's mouth water so violently she had to clench her jaw.

"Eat," Mara said. "You can stay tonight. Tomorrow we'll figure out the rest."

Finch ate. She ate because she was starving and because the food was hot and real and it was the first thing she'd eaten in the new world that wasn't cold beans from a tin. The rice was slightly overcooked and the vegetables were whatever had been available, and it was the best meal she'd ever had. The warmth of it spread through her body like water through dry earth, filling the hollow spaces, reaching the places that cold had settled. She ate slowly, deliberately, trying to make it last, but her hands moved faster than her intentions and the bowl was empty before she was ready.

She stared at the empty bowl. A few grains of rice clung to the sides. She scraped them out with her finger and ate them too.

Mara sat across the fire and watched her eat. Her expression hadn't changed — still sharp, still assessing, still unreadable. But there was something in her eyes that Finch couldn't quite name. Not warmth, exactly. Something adjacent to warmth. The way a gate is adjacent to a door — it lets things through, but it doesn't open the same way.

Around them, the fire popped and hissed, and somewhere in the distance a dog barked — a real dog, alive, the sound of normalcy in a world that had forgotten it. The people around the fire had resumed their conversations, their attention redirected, though Finch noticed that the older woman with the steel hands was still watching her, her head still tilted, her expression still one of careful recognition.

"You said your father's name was Caleb Adderwood," Mara said.

Finch looked up. "Yeah."

"Caleb Adderwood. That's — I knew a Caleb once."

The words hung in the air like smoke. Finch felt something shift in her chest — a tectonic movement, deep and slow, the kind of shift that happens deep underground before the surface even knows it's moving.

"You knew a Caleb," she repeated.

"Before all this. Before the outbreak. He was — it was a long time ago." Mara's voice had changed. The assessment was gone, replaced by something softer, more distant, like a radio signal fading as the source moved away. "Never mind." She stood up. "Finish your food. John will show you where you can sleep."

She left again. Finch sat by the fire and stared at the flames and thought about her father, which was what she always thought about when someone said his name, which was what she thought about every day, which was what she had never stopped thinking about since the night the policeman came to the door.

*I knew a Caleb once.* The words turned over in her mind, each rotation revealing a new facet. It was nothing. It was nothing at all. There were probably thousands of Calebs in Sydney before the outbreak. It meant nothing. It meant everything. It was a thread, thin and fragile, connecting her to something she couldn't yet see.

She finished her food. She found John. He led her through the compound — back through the narrow corridors, past the communal kitchen, past a row of containers that she guessed were storage based on the padlocks on their doors. The compound was quieter now, the fires burning lower, the voices hushed. John walked with his shotgun cradled in one arm, his stride easy and unhurried, the stride of a man who knew every shadow in this place and had already decided which ones were dangerous.

He led her to a container that had been converted into a dormitory — a long, narrow space lined with sleeping mats, most of them occupied by sleeping forms. The air inside was warm and close, heavy with the smell of bodies and blankets and the faint, earthy scent of unwashed hair. The light from the corridor fell across the room in a narrow wedge, illuminating a patch of floor and the edge of a mat, and beyond that was darkness and the soft, rhythmic sound of breathing.

He pointed to an empty mat at the far end.

"That's yours. Don't make noise. Some of the kids wake easy."

There were children in here. Finch could see them — small shapes under thin blankets, breathing softly in the dark. She could see the rise and fall of their chests, the occasional twitch of a dreaming limb, the dark tangle of hair against a pillow. She set her backpack down next to the mat, keeping the knife close — always close, the weight of it against her hip a reminder that the world was not safe and never would be again — and lay down on her back and stared at the ceiling.

The ceiling was corrugated iron. It was cold and it was hard and it was the ceiling of a room in a place called the Junkyard in a world that had ended, and Finch was sixteen years old and a very long way from home.

She thought about her mother. She thought about the kitchen in Brookvale, the light spilling through the doorway, the sound of pots and the hum of a song. She thought about the flannel jacket hanging on the hook by the door and the boots by the back door and the wedding ring on the dashboard of a truck that had wrapped itself around a tree. She thought about the colouring book and the purple horse with the green mane and the wax crayons scattered like fallen soldiers.

She thought about the word *Caleb* and the way Mara had said it, and the way her voice had changed, and the way she had walked away.

She closed her eyes. She did not expect to sleep. She was wrong.

---

# Chapter Four: New Faces

Morning came grey and cold, the kind of overcast that settles over Sydney in winter and refuses to leave. Finch woke to the sound of voices and the clatter of activity — people moving, things being carried, the rhythmic clang of metal on metal from somewhere outside.

She sat up. The other mats were empty. Whatever passed for the dawn routine in the Junkyard had already begun, and she'd slept through it.

She found her way out of the container and into the compound. The air was sharp with smoke from cooking fires and the faintly metallic tang of rain-soaked steel. People moved around her — purposeful, busy, each with somewhere to be and something to do. Nobody paid her much attention. The new person novelty had apparently worn off overnight.

She found the communal kitchen — a covered area near the centre of the compound with a long table, a few stoves, and a woman behind the counter who looked like she'd been awake since four in the morning and was already tired of everyone's questions.

"You're the new one," the woman said. Not a question.

"Finch."

"Grab a plate. There's porridge. Eat fast — Mara wants to show you around."

The porridge was thick and slightly lumpy and had something in it that might have been dried apple. Finch ate it standing up, leaning against the counter, watching the flow of people through the kitchen. There were maybe thirty or forty people in total that she could see, ranging from elderly to very young. A handful of children — the youngest maybe four or five, the oldest around twelve — sat at one end of the table, eating with the focused intensity of children who had learned that food was not guaranteed.

Mara appeared beside her without preamble. "Done?"

"Almost." Finch scraped the last of the porridge from the bowl and drank the water that had been set out.

"Right. Come with me."

The tour was brisk and efficient. Mara walked her through the compound, pointing out key locations the way you'd point out fire exits in a building. The water system — rainwater collection filtered through sand and charcoal. The food stores — mostly preserved and dried goods, supplemented by whatever could be scavenged on runs. The medical area — a container with a surprising amount of supplies, organised with military precision. The workshop — where a man named Greg was rebuilding an engine, and where another person was sharpening blades on a grinding wheel.

"The wall perimeter is patrolled around the clock," Mara said. "Two-person shifts, four-hour rotations. Everyone pulls their weight here. You work, you eat. You don't work, you leave."

"What can I do?" Finch asked.

"That's what I'm trying to figure out." Mara stopped walking and turned to face her. "You're sixteen. You've got no survival training, no combat experience, no useful skills for the world as it currently exists. Don't look at me like that — I'm not being cruel. I'm being honest. Most people who show up here, even adults, are useless for the first few weeks."

"That doesn't mean I can't learn."

"No. It doesn't." A flicker of something crossed Mara's face — not quite a smile, but a softening. "Liam said you held yourself together when you found the Hollows. Most people scream and run. You watched."

"I was too scared to run."

"Maybe. Or maybe you're someone who thinks before they move. I can work with that."

She introduced Finch to people as they walked. Greg in the workshop, who nodded and went back to his engine. An older woman named Pat who ran the medical area, who looked at Finch's bare feet in the too-big thongs and immediately went to find her a proper pair of boots. A man named Dave who handled food logistics and who said "We could always use another pair of hands in the kitchen" in a way that suggested the kitchen was the least dangerous assignment he could offer.

And then there were the kids.

They found Finch at the midday meal, the way children find anyone who looks like they might be interesting — with the sudden, overwhelming force of a small, curious tide. There were five of them ranging in age from about six to twelve, and they gathered around Finch's table with the unselfconscious boldness of children who had grown up in a world where strangers were either dangerous or irrelevant and they had already decided Finch was neither.

"You've got purple hair," said the oldest, a girl of about twelve with a freckled face and a missing front tooth.

"Lavender," Finch corrected.

"What's lavender?"

"It's a flower."

"Never seen one."

The girl's name was Reef. The others were Cass, who was eight and quiet; Bo, who was seven and not quiet at all; Wren, who was six and clung to Reef's arm like a lifeline; and a boy named Spike who was eleven and who regarded Finch with the suspicious squint of someone who had not yet decided whether she was worth his time.

"We're not allowed to go past the wall," Reef explained, as though this were a boring but necessary fact of life, like gravity or homework. "But I've seen the Hollows from the watchtower. They're gross."

"They are gross," Finch agreed.

"Have you killed one?" Spike asked.

"No."

"Have you seen one?"

"Yeah. Three of them. On the road."

"What did you do?"

"I ran."

Spike made a face that clearly communicated his disappointment in this answer. "I wouldn't run," he said. "I'd fight."

"You're seven," Reef said.

"Almost eight."

"That's the same as seven."

"It is not—"

"Bo, shut up. Spike, shut up. Everyone shut up." Reef turned back to Finch with the air of someone who had better things to do than referee. "Do you want to see the watchtower?"

"Reef, she just got here," Pat said from somewhere behind them. "Let her eat."

Reef sighed the sigh of someone burdened by the incompetence of adults, and the five of them dispersed, already arguing among themselves about something that may or may not have involved a lizard.

Finch watched them go. Something ached in her chest — something warm and sharp at the same time, like a bruise being pressed.

The afternoon passed. Finch helped in the kitchen, scrubbing pots and carrying water. She was awkward at it — everything in this world required a kind of physical knowledge she didn't have, the muscle memory of survival — but she worked hard and didn't complain, and by the end of the day Dave had upgraded his assessment of her from "useless" to "not entirely useless."

She ate dinner with the other survivors around the fire pit. The food was simple — a stew made from dried meat and root vegetables, thick enough to stand a spoon in — but it was hot and it was enough, and the people around her ate with the quiet gratitude of people who had not always had enough.

They didn't talk much to her. Not out of hostility — more out of the careful, deliberate privacy that comes from living in close quarters with a small group of people. Everyone knew everyone's business, which meant everyone knew the basics about Finch already, and there was no need to ask.

After dinner, the fire burned low and people drifted away to their sleeping quarters. Finch stayed. She liked the fire. She liked the way it moved and the way it smelled and the way it made the dark feel smaller.

The kids had told her there would be a campfire tonight — a proper one, bigger, with stories. And sure enough, as the evening deepened, the fire pit was restocked and more people gathered, sitting on logs and crates and overturned buckets. Someone brought out a guitar — not Ollie's guitar, not the guitar from her old life, but a battered acoustic with three strings — and played something slow and familiar that Finch couldn't quite place.

She sat at the edge of the group, her knees drawn up, and for a few minutes she almost forgot. Almost forgot the Hollows and the empty highway and the world that had ended. Almost felt like a normal teenager at a normal gathering, listening to music by a fire.

Then Mara was there.

She appeared beside Finch the way she always appeared — suddenly, silently, with the air of someone who had materialised from the shadows specifically because the shadows needed her elsewhere.

"Come with me," Mara said.

"What's wrong?"

"Nothing's wrong. The leader wants to see you."

Finch looked up at her. Mara's face was lit by the firelight, and in the shifting orange glow she looked older than she had in daylight, and more tired, and more like someone who carried the weight of many things and was not always sure she could bear them.

"The leader," Finch repeated.

"Our leader. He's — well, you'll see. Come."

Finch stood up and followed her.

They left the fire pit and crossed the compound, moving through narrow alleys between containers and structures. The Junkyard was quieter now, the fires low, the voices hushed. Mara led her to a structure that Finch hadn't noticed before — a tower, taller than anything else in the compound, built from welded steel and shipping containers stacked four high. A ladder ran up the outside.

"Up there," Mara said. "He's waiting."

"Who is he?"

Mara looked at her. That softening again, the one from the first day, the one that wasn't quite a smile.

"Go up and find out."

Finch climbed. The ladder was cold under her hands and the rungs were uneven and the wind picked up as she ascended, tugging at her lavender hair and the edges of her hoodie. She climbed past the first level, the second, the third. At the fourth level, there was a platform — a wide, railed lookout that offered a panoramic view of the compound below and the dark, empty landscape beyond.

A desk sat on the platform. A simple wooden desk with a chair behind it, and a lamp — battery-powered — casting a warm circle of light. There were papers on the desk, maps, a radio. And there was a person sitting in the chair, facing away from her, looking out over the dark land.

"Come in," the person said. "Don't just stand on the ladder."

A man's voice. Deep, steady, with a faint rasp that might have been natural or might have been from years of breathing smoke and dust.

Finch stepped onto the platform. Her heart was beating very fast, though she didn't know why. Something about this felt momentous, though she couldn't have explained what.

The man swivelled the chair around.

He was tall. Even sitting, she could tell he was tall — long-limbed, broad-shouldered, with the kind of frame that spoke of physical work and endurance. His hair was dark, streaked with grey, and his face was weathered and lined in a way that suggested he had aged more than his years should have required. He had a beard, close-trimmed, and his eyes—

His eyes were pale grey-blue. Silver, in the light.

Finch's breath stopped.

She knew those eyes. She had seen them every morning in the mirror for sixteen years. She had seen them in photographs on the mantelpiece and in dreams that came at three in the morning and in the face of the man who had held her at the kitchen table while her mother made tea for a policeman who had just told them he was dead.

"Hello, Finch," said Caleb Adderwood.

She didn't move. She didn't breathe. She stood on the platform at the top of the watchtower with the wind in her hair and the firelight far below and the dark stretching out in every direction, and she stared at her father.

"What," she said.

And that was where the first chapter ended.

---

# Chapter Five: The Long Night

Caleb Adderwood did not look like the man Finch remembered.

The father of her childhood was solid and warm, with a broad smile that crinkled the corners of his silver eyes and hands that could palm a basketball like it was a dinner plate. He smelled like diesel and sawdust and the particular brand of aftershave he kept in the medicine cabinet next to her mother's perfume. He laughed loudly and without reservation, and when he hugged her, which was often, his arms were a fortress.

The man sitting in the chair at the top of the watchtower was made of different material. He was leaner — not thin, but stripped down, as though something had been pared away from him over the years, some essential softness that had once been there. His face was harder, the lines deeper, the jaw more pronounced. His hair, dark with threads of silver, was longer than she'd ever seen it, pulled back from his face with a leather tie. His hands, resting on the arms of the chair, were scarred — a web of old marks across the knuckles and the backs, some pale and faded, others newer and pink.

But the eyes were the same. Pale grey-blue. Silver in the battery-powered light. Her eyes. The eyes she saw in the mirror every morning.

"Finch," he said again, and his voice cracked on the syllables, just slightly, the way a bone cracks before it breaks. "You'd better sit down."

She didn't sit down. She couldn't. Her legs had become ornamental — they existed, technically, but they bore no weight and responded to no commands. She stood at the edge of the platform and stared at the man who was her father and tried to make the world make sense.

"You're dead," she said.

"I know."

"You died. Eight years ago. There was a crash — the truck — the policeman came to the house and told Mum and I was there, I was sitting in the doorway, I heard him say there were no remains, and Mum—" Her voice splintered. She pressed on. "And Mum fell apart. She fell apart for years. She still hasn't put herself back together. And you're here. You're sitting in a chair."

"Yes."

"Explain."

Caleb leaned forward. The chair creaked under his weight. He looked at her with those eyes — those impossibly familiar eyes — and she could see the exhaustion in them, the deep, geological weariness of a man who had been carrying something heavy for a very long time and had finally, at last, set it down.

"I'll tell you everything," he said. "But I need you to understand something first. What I'm about to tell you — it's going to change the way you see everything. The world, the virus, all of it. And I can't undo that."

"I'm already standing in a junkyard in the middle of the apocalypse talking to my dead father. I think my capacity for shock is pretty well tapped out."

The ghost of a smile crossed his face. It was a smile she recognised — the one he used to give her when she said something clever that she wasn't supposed to say.

"Alright," he said. "Sit."

She sat. She sat on the cold metal floor of the platform, her back against the railing, her knees drawn up, and Caleb told her the story.

It began with the crash.

"The truck left the road on the Pacific Highway," he said. "I remember that much. I remember the steering wheel pulling to the right, and I remember trying to correct, and I remember the sound — the engine, the tyres, the metal. And then there was light."

"Light?"

"A light. Not headlights. Not the sun. Something else. Something — white. Completely, totally white. It filled the cab. It filled everything. I couldn't see the dashboard, I couldn't see my hands, I couldn't see anything except white. And then it stopped."

He paused. His hands tightened on the arms of the chair.

"When it stopped, I wasn't in the truck. I was in a room. A small room, white, with fluorescent lights in the ceiling and a metal bed beneath me. I was strapped to the bed — wrists, ankles, chest. Leather restraints. I couldn't move."

Finch's mouth was dry. "Where?"

"That's what I asked. I asked a lot of things, actually. I shouted for a while. I'm not proud of that. And then the door opened, and a man walked in."

Caleb told her about the man. He was tall, mid-fifties, wearing a lab coat and an expression of clinical interest, like Finch's father was a specimen that had been placed on the table for his examination. He introduced himself as Dr. Robert Qwantum.

"Qwantum," Finch said.

"Qwantum Labs. The same company whose virus destroyed this world. At the time, I didn't know any of that. I didn't know where I was. I didn't know what had happened. I just knew I was strapped to a bed and there was a man in a lab coat looking at me like I was an equation he was solving."

Robert Qwantum explained things, eventually. He explained with the calm, measured cadence of a man who had rehearsed this explanation many times and no longer felt the need to tailor it to his audience. He explained that Caleb had been brought here from Earth One. That this was Earth Two — a parallel world, identical in almost every way, except for the details that mattered. He explained that Earth One's inhabitants had stronger immune systems, more resilient biology, a kind of genetic robustness that Earth Two's people lacked. He explained that this robustness was the key.

"To what?" Finch asked.

"To everything. Cancer. Heart disease. Alzheimer's. Every ailment that plagued humanity on this Earth. He believed — Robert Qwantum believed — that the blood of someone from Earth One could be used to create a cure. Not just a treatment. A universal cure. The end of disease."

"And he needed Dad's blood."

"He needed my blood. My bone marrow. My cells. Everything." Caleb's voice was flat now, stripped of emotion, the way a voice gets when the emotion has been felt so many times that it's worn smooth. "He said it like he was offering me a compliment. He said I was genetically ideal. That the probability of my crossing over was so low as to be effectively impossible, and that I should consider myself fortunate."

"Fortunate."

"That's the word he used."

Caleb was held in that room for two years. Two years in a white room with fluorescent lights and a metal bed and the constant, antiseptic smell of whatever they used to clean the floors. Robert Qwantum and his team took blood — gallons of it, it seemed, drawn in such quantities that Caleb would pass out and wake up on the same bed with a new needle mark in his arm. They took marrow samples from his spine. They took tissue biopsies, muscle samples, spinal fluid. They ran tests that had no names and produced no results he was allowed to understand.

"He was meticulous," Caleb said. "I'll give him that. He was thorough and methodical and he never raised his voice. That was the worst part. He could have been conducting a lab experiment on a mouse and his tone wouldn't have been different."

Finch felt something cold settle in her stomach. "Did you try to escape?"

"Every day. The restraints held. The room was sealed. There were guards outside the door — not Hollows, not yet, but armed men, private security, the kind of people who didn't ask questions because they were paid not to." He paused. "I should mention — at this point, the virus didn't exist. Qwantum Labs was a research facility. A biotech company with government contracts and corporate investors and a campus the size of a small town. The Hollows came later."

"What happened?"

"The experiments failed. Whatever Qwantum was looking for in my blood, he couldn't isolate it. Or he could isolate it, but he couldn't replicate it. Or he could replicate it, but it didn't work the way he expected. I don't know the details — I'm not a scientist. What I know is that after two years of taking everything from me, Robert Qwantum was no closer to his miracle cure than he'd been on the first day."

"So he stopped?"

"He didn't stop. He changed direction. If he couldn't find the answer in my blood, he would create it. He would engineer it. He would take what he'd learned from me and build something new — something that would do what my biology did naturally, but for everyone. For all of Earth Two."

The Hollow virus.

Caleb told her how it happened. Robert Qwantum, desperate and brilliant and willing to cross any line in the pursuit of his vision, pivoted from extraction to engineering. He used Caleb's genetic material as a template — a blueprint for the kind of biological resilience he wanted to create. He spliced it, modified it, enhanced it. He worked in a sealed laboratory wing with a team of scientists who understood enough to follow his instructions and not enough to ask why.

The virus was designed to rewrite the human body from the inside out. To strip away vulnerability, to rebuild the immune system, to create something stronger. What it actually did was destroy everything it touched. It ate through healthy tissue like acid through paper. It attacked the brain first, erasing personality, memory, identity — everything that made a person a person. Then it rebuilt the body into something that could survive without any of those things. Something that moved. Something that hungered. Something hollow.

"There was a containment breach," Caleb said. "Or maybe there wasn't. Maybe he released it on purpose — I've never been sure. What I know is that one morning the alarms went off, and the guards outside my door ran, and the door to my room was unlocked, and I walked out into a building that was falling apart."

He escaped during the chaos. The labs were overrun — staff, security, everyone. The virus spread through the campus in hours, through the city in days, through the world in weeks. Caleb Adderwood walked out of Qwantum Labs alive because he was the only person on the planet who was immune.

He wandered for months after that. Alone, moving through the ruins of a world that had ended around him. He saw things he wouldn't describe to Finch, not tonight, maybe not ever. He survived the way Liam had described surviving — by being careful, by being quiet, by being lucky.

And then, eventually, he found Mara.

"I'd been alone for almost a year," he said. "I was — I wasn't doing well. I was sleeping in the open, eating what I could find, avoiding the Hollows. And one day I collapsed on a road, just — fell down, because I was dehydrated and I hadn't eaten in two days. And a woman found me."

"Mara."

"Mara. She was with a small group of survivors — six people, living in a warehouse. She took me in. She nursed me back to health. She—" He stopped. Something shifted in his expression, something private and tender. "She became the most important person in my world. The only person who knew me — really knew me. I told her everything. About Earth One, about Qwantum Labs, about the virus. She believed me. I don't know why. I'd have thought I was crazy."

"Why did she?"

"Because she'd seen the virus do things that no natural virus should do. She'd seen it rewrite people. She knew it was engineered, even before I confirmed it." He paused. "Over time, more survivors found their way to us. The compound grew. People needed someone to make decisions, to set rules, to keep them alive. I didn't want the job. Mara didn't want it either. But someone had to do it, and I was the only person who understood how the virus worked, who it targeted, why it did what it did. So they looked to me, and I—"

"You became the leader."

"I became the leader."

The wind picked up on the platform, tugging at Finch's hair and the edges of her hoodie. Below them, the fire pit had burned to embers. The compound was silent except for the distant sound of the windmill turning.

Finch sat with her knees drawn up and her arms wrapped around them, and she tried to process what her father had told her. She tried to hold all of it in her mind at once — the parallel worlds, the light, the lab, Robert Qwantum, the virus, all of it — and make it fit into a shape she could understand. But it didn't fit. It was too big, too strange, too far from anything she had ever imagined.

"I'm on another Earth," she said.

"Yes."

"You're telling me that there's another version of the world — another version of everything — and I'm on the wrong one."

"There's no wrong one. There's just — different ones. Earth One is yours. Earth Two is this one. The virus only exists here. The Hollows only exist here. On Earth One, everything is normal. Your mother is normal. Brookvale is normal."

"My mother is alone."

"Yes."

"She's alone because you were taken."

"Yes."

The word was a stone dropping into still water. Finch felt the ripples move through her — anger, grief, confusion, all of it churning together into something she couldn't name.

"You could have come back," she said. "If there's a way here, there's a way back. You could have come back to us."

Caleb's face changed. The exhaustion deepened, and beneath it something else surfaced — something raw and open, a wound that had never healed.

"I tried," he said. "For years, I tried. The light that brought me here — I've never been able to find it again. I don't know how it works. I don't know why it chose me. I've spent every year since I escaped looking for a way back, and I have found nothing. Not a trace. Not a whisper."

He looked at her, and his silver eyes were bright in the lamplight, and she could see the tears he was holding back with the same iron discipline that had kept him alive for seven years in a world that wanted him dead.

"You being here," he said. "You being here — it means something. I don't know what yet. But you're here, and I'm here, and we're alive, and that—"

His voice broke. He pressed his hand over his eyes and breathed, and Finch sat on the cold metal floor and watched her father cry for the first time in her life.

She didn't move toward him. She didn't reach out. She sat where she was and let the tears come for him, because some moments are too big for comfort, too vast for touch. She sat and she breathed and the wind moved across the platform and the embers glowed far below and the world — the wrong world, the other world, the only world she had — turned beneath them.

After a while, Caleb lowered his hand. He looked at her. She looked at him.

"I need time," Finch said.

"I know."

"I can't — I can't just accept all of this. You've had seven years. I've had seven hours."

"I know."

"I'm angry."

"You should be."

"I don't know if I can forgive you."

"I don't expect you to."

She looked at him. He looked at her. The wind blew. The world turned.

"Can I go back to my mat now?" she asked.

"Yes," Caleb said. "Of course you can."

She climbed down the ladder in the dark, her hands sure on the rungs even though her vision was blurred. She found her way through the compound to the container dormitory. She found her mat. She lay down on her back and stared at the corrugated iron ceiling and thought about her mother, alone in a house in Brookvale with a flannel jacket hanging on a hook by the door and a pair of boots by the back door and a wedding ring on the dashboard of a truck that had wrapped itself around a tree.

She thought about Earth One. A world without Hollows, without Qwantum Labs, without Avarice. A world where the rain fell on the roof and her mother made tea and her father drove trucks and everything was normal and small and safe.

She thought about the colouring book and the purple horse with the green mane.

She didn't sleep for a long time. When she finally did, she dreamed of a light — white and total and absolute — filling a truck cab on the Pacific Highway, filling everything, erasing the road and the rain and the tree and the sound of impact, replacing it all with a silence so complete it was almost a sound of its own.

In the morning, she would begin again.

---

# Chapter Six: The Shape of Things

The weeks that followed were the strangest of Finch's life, which was saying something, because the preceding weeks had involved waking up on a destroyed highway in a parallel dimension.

She settled into the Junkyard the way you settle into a language you don't quite speak — with effort, with mistakes, with the gradual, exhausting acquisition of vocabulary. The routines of survival. The particular skills required to exist in a world that had been stripped of everything that made modern life effortless. She learned which rainwater containers were for drinking and which were for washing. She learned the sound the gate made when it opened — a low, grinding shriek that carried across the compound — and the sound it made when it closed — a heavy, final clunk that meant you were inside and the world was outside. She learned which paths through the compound were quickest in the dark, which containers creaked in the wind, which of the cooking fires produced the most smoke and which the most heat.

She learned the people.

Not all at once. The Junkyard was not a place that revealed itself quickly. It was layered, like sediment — the outer layer was the one you saw, the daily business of eating and working and patrolling, but beneath that were deeper strata: the alliances, the resentments, the histories, the quiet alliances and quiet betrayals that accumulate in any small community over time.

Dave, who ran the food logistics, turned out to be a retired chef. He had worked in a restaurant in Surry Hills before the virus, and he approached the problem of feeding fifty people with the same creative intensity he had once applied to degustation menus. The result was food that was better than it had any right to be — stews thickened with foraged mushrooms, bread baked in a repurposed oil drum, a jam made from wild berries that was so good Finch actually closed her eyes the first time she tasted it.

"That's a reaction," Dave said, watching her. "Most people just shovel it in."

"It's incredible," Finch said, her mouth full.

"Technique," Dave said, tapping his temple. "It's all technique. You can make anything taste like something with the right technique."

Pat, who ran the medical area, was a former nurse. She had been a ward sister at Royal North Shore before the collapse, and she brought to her current work the same unflappable competence she had once applied to emergency departments. The medical container was immaculate — supplies catalogued, surfaces cleaned, procedures posted on the wall in neat handwriting. She checked on Finch every morning with the same brusque efficiency.

"You're settling in," Pat said one morning, shining a penlight into Finch's eyes. "Good colour in your cheeks. How's your sleep?"

"Bad."

"That'll improve. Your body will catch up with the reality eventually. In the meantime, eat well, drink water, and don't skip the morning stretches. Mara will kill you if you skip the morning stretches."

Mara.

Mara was, as Finch had quickly come to understand, the operational backbone of the Junkyard. Caleb was the leader in the sense that he made the big decisions — who to trade with, when to fortify, how to allocate resources on a strategic level. But Mara was the one who made sure the decisions actually happened. She ran the daily operations with a precision that bordered on military, assigning roles, resolving disputes, coordinating patrols, managing the endless logistical puzzle of keeping fifty people alive in a world designed to kill them.

She also, it turned out, had decided that Finch needed training.

"Up," Mara said, appearing at Finch's mat at dawn three days after her arrival. "We're starting today."

"Starting what?"

"Learning not to die."

The training was brutal. Not in the dramatic, cinematic way — no one was throwing Finch into pits or making her run laps in the rain. It was brutal in the way that all real training is brutal: repetitive, exhausting, deeply humbling, and punctuated by moments of genuine fear.

Mara taught her how to move quietly. How to place her feet, how to distribute her weight, how to step over debris without making a sound. She taught her how to listen — not just to hear, but to listen, to distinguish between the shuffle of a Hollow and the footfall of a person, between the wind and something moving through the wind.

"The Hollows are slow," Mara said. "But they don't get tired. They don't stop. If they hear you, they will follow you until they find you or until you find something sharp. So don't let them hear you."

She taught her how to use the knife. Not the kitchen knife Finch had scavenged from the motel — Mara replaced it with something better, a short-bladed utility knife with a wrapped handle and a sheath that hung from her belt. She taught her the grip, the stance, the mechanics of a strike. She taught her where to aim — the head, always the head, because the Hollows' bodies continued to function even when everything above the neck had already decayed.

"Aim for the temple," Mara said. "If you miss the temple, go for the base of the skull. If you miss that, you're already dead, so don't miss."

She taught her how to read a map, how to identify landmarks, how to navigate without a compass. She taught her basic first aid — pressure on a wound, the recovery position, how to clear an airway. She taught her the names of edible plants, the ones that grew wild in the overgrown streets and parks of the dead city, and the ones that would kill you.

"You're a fast learner," Mara said one afternoon, after Finch had successfully navigated a two-kilometre course through the compound's perimeter and returned within the allotted time. "That's good. It means you might live."

"High praise."

"Don't get used to it."

Mara was not warm. She was not cold, either — she was something in between, something functional, like the temperature of water that's good for washing. She had a dry, clipped way of speaking that could pass for harshness but was actually efficiency. She didn't waste words because words were a resource, like water or ammunition, and resources were finite.

But occasionally — rarely, and always unexpectedly — something softer surfaced. A hand on Finch's shoulder after a difficult training session. A quiet "well done" that was more surprising than any shout.
