Once, when Finch cut her hand badly during knife practice and sat on the ground with blood running between her fingers, Mara had knelt beside her and bandaged the wound with a gentleness that was so at odds with everything else about her that Finch had looked up in surprise.

Mara met her eyes. "Don't read into it," she said. "I just need both your hands intact for tomorrow's session."

But Finch had read into it. And she understood, in the slow, accretive way that you understand the people who matter, that Mara's reserve was not absence of feeling but the opposite — a great, careful damming of feeling, built up over years of loss and survival, that she permitted to leak only in the smallest, most controlled amounts.

The children helped.

Reef, Spike, Bo, Wren, and Cass became Finch's shadow orbit — not constantly, but with a regularity that suggested they had decided she was theirs. They appeared at meals, at the fire pit, at the edges of training sessions. They asked questions. So many questions. About lavender. About Brookvale. About Earth One. About everything.

"What's a car?" Bo asked, which seemed like a stupid question until Finch realised Bo had never seen a working car.

"What's a school?" Wren asked, which made Finch's chest hurt so badly she had to look away.

"Do you have Hollows on Earth One?" Spike asked, which was the first question Finch didn't have a good answer for.

"No," she said. "We don't have Hollows."

"What do you have instead?"

"Ordinary death, I suppose."

Spike considered this. "Boring," he said.

Reef was the oldest and the most perceptive. She watched Finch with an attention that was almost adult, noting the things Finch didn't say as much as the things she did. One evening, as the fire burned low and the other children had drifted away, Reef sat beside Finch on the log and said, quietly, "You miss your mum."

It wasn't a question.

"Yeah," Finch said.

"I miss mine too. She was one of the first ones. When the virus started."

"I'm sorry."

"It's okay. I mean, it's not okay. But it's — it's what happened. And now I'm here, and Mara's here, and you're here, and that's enough." She paused. "Most days."

Most days. Finch filed the phrase away in the growing archive of things she was learning about this world and the people in it. Most days. Not every day. Not all days. Just most.

Her father.

The relationship with Caleb was the hardest thing.

Not because he was cruel or neglectful or any of the things a father in a story might be. He was none of those things. He was present and attentive and clearly, painfully devoted to her. The problem was the gap. Eight years of absence that couldn't be filled with a single conversation in a watchtower, no matter how honest or how raw. He was her father, and he was a stranger, and the space between those two facts was a country she didn't know how to cross.

They ate together most evenings. Caleb would come down from the watchtower at sunset, and they would sit at the edge of the fire pit, and they would eat, and they would talk. The conversations were stilted at first — full of pauses and careful silences, two people feeling their way around each other like people crossing a room in the dark.

Caleb asked about her childhood. Not the broad strokes — he knew those, in the way that someone knows a story they've been told secondhand — but the details. The small, specific, irreplaceable details that make a life a life.

"Did you like school?" he asked.

"It was fine. I wasn't popular. I wasn't unpopular. I was just—"

"Normal."

"Yeah."

"You were always going to be normal. You were the most normal child. You ate the same breakfast every day for three years — toast with butter, cut diagonally. You refused to wear shoes until you were four. You coloured in all your colouring books from back to front."

"You remember that?"

"I remember everything about you. Every single thing." He said it with a weight that made Finch look at him, and in his silver eyes she saw something that she recognised because she felt it too — the particular, exquisite pain of loving someone you'd lost and found and lost the shape of in the finding.

She asked about his years. The wandering, the loneliness, the slow building of the Junkyard. He told her things. Not everything — she could tell he was editing, choosing the things she could bear to hear and leaving the rest in silence. But enough.

He told her about the first Hollow he'd killed. A woman, or what had been a woman, standing in the middle of a road in Parramatta, groaning at the sky. He'd spent twenty minutes trying to communicate with her before he understood that there was no one left to communicate with.

He told her about Mara. About finding her, about being found by her, about the slow, careful construction of a partnership that had become the foundation of everything they'd built.

"She saved my life," he said. "Not just in the physical sense. She gave me a reason to keep living. After everything that happened — after the lab, after the escape, after seeing what the virus did — I was ready to stop. I was ready to lie down and let the world take me. And she wouldn't let me."

"She's remarkable."

"She is."

"Do you love her?"

The question surprised both of them. Finch hadn't intended to ask it — it came out the way things sometimes come out in this new world, blunt and unvarnished, without the protective layer of social convention that had once governed conversation.

Caleb looked at her for a long moment. "Yes," he said. "I do."

"Is that okay? That I'm asking?"

"It's more than okay. It's — it's normal. You being normal, asking normal questions. It's what I've been missing."

Nights on the watchtower became a ritual.

After the first week, Finch began climbing up after dinner, when the compound was settling into its nighttime rhythms and the fires were burning low. She would find Caleb at his desk, or leaning against the railing, looking out at the dark. Sometimes they talked. Sometimes they didn't. Sometimes they just stood side by side and watched the stars — which were the same stars she'd known on Earth One, the same constellations, the same indifferent beauty.

"Do you think Mum knows?" Finch asked one night. "That I'm gone?"

"I don't know."

"Do you think she's looking for me?"

"I think your mother has spent eight years looking for me, and if you've disappeared in the same way, she will—"

He stopped. Finished, silently, with his face turned toward the dark.

"She'll fall apart," Finch said.

"Yes."

"We have to find a way back."

"I know."

"Have you ever been close? To finding a way?"

"Once. I found — a place, about three years ago, where the light had been. A field outside Campbelltown. The grass was dead in a perfect circle, about ten metres across, and the air tasted different. Like ozone. Like the moment before a storm. I went there every day for six months. I sat in the centre of the circle and I waited. And nothing happened."

"Nothing."

"Nothing. Eventually the circle filled in. The grass grew back. The air stopped tasting like ozone. Whatever had been there — whatever had brought me here — it was gone."

They stood in silence. The stars burned. The wind turned the windmill.

"There has to be a way," Finch said.

"There is. I believe that. I have to believe that, because the alternative is accepting that I'm here forever, and I can't — I won't accept that. Your mother is there. You're here. There's a door somewhere, and I'm going to find it."

She looked at him. Her father. Her stranger. Her impossible, improbable, alive father.

"Together," she said.

He looked at her. And for the first time since she'd arrived, he smiled. Not the ghost of a smile, not the thin, tired smile of a man managing his own exhaustion. A real smile. A smile that reached his eyes and lit them from within and made him look, for just a moment, like the man in the photographs on the mantelpiece in Brookvale.

"Together," he said.

---

# Chapter Seven: The Run

The supply run was Caleb's idea, and it was the first time Finch saw him in his other role — not the father, not the watchtower, not the man with the silver eyes and the cracked voice, but the leader. The planner. The one who looked at a map and saw not terrain but possibilities, not distance but risk, not a destination but a series of decisions that would determine whether everyone came home alive.

"We need a run," Caleb said over breakfast, addressing Mara and John and a handful of the compound's core members. "The water filters are degrading, the generator fuel is low, and we're out of nails. If we're going to reinforce the north wall before winter, we need materials."

"The north wall is holding," John said.

"The north wall is fine now. It won't be fine in three months. I want it done before the cold sets in."

Mara nodded. "I'll put a team together."

"You, me, and Finch."

Mara looked at him. "She's been here a week."

"She needs to see what's out there. Not from the watchtower. From the ground."

"Her first run is usually supervised—"

"Which is why she'll be with us. Two of the best. Unless you don't think we're good enough."

Mara's expression didn't change, but something shifted in her eyes — a flicker of amusement, maybe, or recognition of the move he was making. Letting her daughter run in the field was either the most protective or the most foolish thing he could do, depending on how you looked at it.

"Fine," she said. "But she follows my lead. She does what I say, when I say it, without question. Agreed?"

"Agreed," Caleb said, looking at Finch.

"Agreed," Finch said, because she had learned in a week that when Mara set a condition, the negotiation was already over.

They left at dawn.

The vehicle was a Land Cruiser — or what had been a Land Cruiser, before seven years of post-apocalyptic modification. The chassis was original, as was the engine block, but most everything else had been rebuilt or replaced. The body was a patchwork of welded steel plates, the windows were polycarbonate rather than glass, and the interior had been stripped down to bare metal and fitted with bench seats and storage racks. It ran on diesel that Greg refined from whatever fuel sources they could scavenge, and it sounded like a tank and handled like one too.

Mara drove. Caleb rode shotgun with a crossbow propped against the dashboard and a shotgun within arm's reach. Finch sat in the back on a bench seat that vibrated with every revolution of the engine, wedged between crates of empty jerrycans and a toolbox that rattled like bones.

They headed south, through the industrial sprawl that surrounded the compound, and into the suburbs. The streets were empty — not the dramatic, cinematic emptiness of a disaster movie, but the quiet, mundane emptiness of a place where life had simply stopped. Cars sat in driveways. Shopping centres stood with their doors open and their shelves bare. Playgrounds stood motionless, their equipment rusting in the rain.

"There's a hardware store on the other side of Hurstville," Mara said, navigating a turn around a fallen power line. "We hit it last month and it was still producing. Nails, timber, wire, pipe. Anything we can carry."

"And the Hollows?"

"Low density. This area was evacuated early — most of the population either got out or got turned in the first wave. We might see a few stragglers, but nothing we can't handle."

"Stragglers," Finch repeated from the back seat.

"That's what we call the lone Hollows. The ones that didn't join a pack. They're easier to deal with — slower, more predictable. One person with a knife can take down a straggler."

"And a pack?"

Mara glanced at her in the rear-view mirror. "A pack, you run."

The hardware store was exactly as Mara had described — a squat, functional building on a commercial strip, its parking lot littered with abandoned cars and its interior dark and dusty but still stocked. They entered carefully, Mara first with her machete drawn, then Caleb with the crossbow, then Finch with her utility knife and her heart hammering so hard she could feel it in her teeth.

The store was empty. No Hollows, no people, no movement. Just rows of shelving units standing in the half-light like sentinels, and the smell of old paint and chemical preservatives.

"Quick and quiet," Mara said. "Fill the jerrycans from the pipe fittings aisle. Caleb, grab the nails — every box you can find. Finch, timber. Two-by-fours, plywood, whatever you can carry."

They worked. Finch carried timber in armfuls, stacking it in the back of the Land Cruiser while the others loaded heavier items. The work was physical and immediate and she was grateful for it — grateful for the simplicity of carrying things from one place to another, for the way it occupied her hands and her attention and left no room for the larger, more complicated things she was trying not to think about.

They were halfway through loading when Mara held up a fist. Silence. Finch froze, a length of plywood balanced across her shoulders.

A sound. Distant, but distinct. The groan of a Hollow.

Mara pointed. Finch looked. At the far end of the parking lot, behind a row of abandoned cars, a shape was moving. Just one. A straggler, shuffling aimlessly between the vehicles, its groan a low, continuous drone.

Mara gestured: wait. They waited. The Hollow moved past the cars, groaned at nothing, shuffled behind a wall and out of sight.

Mara pointed at the Land Cruiser. They loaded the last of the materials in silence and climbed in and Mara started the engine and they pulled out of the parking lot and back onto the road.

"That was close," Finch said.

"That was nothing," Caleb said. "That was a Tuesday."

They headed back toward the compound, the Land Cruiser lurching over potholes and debris. Finch sat in the back and watched the dead city slide past the windows — the empty buildings, the overgrown gardens, the occasional flash of colour from a billboard that had somehow survived the years of weather and neglect.

And then Mara slowed the vehicle.

Ahead, on the road, there was something. A symbol. Painted on the side of a building — a warehouse, its doors hanging open — in black spray paint. A circle with an A inside it. Still fresh. The paint was barely dry.

"Avarice," Caleb said, his voice dropping to a register Finch hadn't heard before.

"How recent?" Mara asked.

Caleb leaned out the window, studying the mark. "Days. Maybe less."

"Route?" Mara asked.

"Keep going. Don't deviate. If they've marked this area, they're either here or they've moved on. Either way, we don't engage."

Mara accelerated. The Land Cruiser's engine roared and the vehicle surged forward, and Finch gripped the edge of the bench seat and tried to make herself small.

They were two blocks from the warehouse when the sound came.

An engine. Not the Land Cruiser's diesel growl — something else. Higher-pitched, more aggressive. A vehicle behind them, accelerating.

Mara looked in the rear-view mirror. Her face changed.

"Caleb."

"I see them."

Finch turned and looked through the polycarbonate rear window. A truck — a modified pickup, its bed reinforced with steel plating — was coming up fast behind them. There were people in the truck. Three, maybe four. She could see shapes in the back, silhouettes against the grey sky.

The circle-and-A symbol was painted on the truck's door.

"Go," Caleb said. "Go now."

Mara floored it. The Land Cruiser surged forward, its engine screaming, its tyres struggling for grip on the cracked road surface. Behind them, the truck kept pace. It was faster — lighter, more agile — and it was gaining.

Finch looked back again. The truck was closing. She could see the driver now — a figure behind the wheel, face obscured by shadow. In the bed of the truck, three people stood, holding on to the steel framework. One of them was shouting something she couldn't hear over the engine noise.

"Mara—" Finch started.

"I know. I know." Mara swerved around an abandoned car, cutting across the oncoming lane. The truck followed. She swerved again, back into their lane, and the truck swerved with them.

"They're toying with us," Caleb said. He had the crossbow in his hands now, the bolt loaded, aimed at the rear window. "They're driving us."

"Driving us where?"

"Doesn't matter. We don't stop."

The chase went on for five minutes that felt like five hours. Mara drove with a ferocity that Finch had never seen in anyone — all sharp turns and sudden acceleration and the kind of split-second decision-making that separated the living from the dead in this world. Caleb leaned out the passenger window with the crossbow, his face set, his jaw tight.

The truck was relentless. It kept pace through every turn, every swerve, every desperate manoeuvre. It was faster, but Mara was better, and the streets were her territory in a way they would never be the territory of people who drove like they were chasing something.

Then, ahead, a gap. An intersection that led to a wider road — a dual carriageway that ran straight for half a kilometre toward the edge of the industrial zone. Mara took it at speed, the Land Cruiser's tyres screaming on the bitumen, and for a moment the truck fell behind, forced to slow for the turn.

It wasn't much. But it was enough.

Mara drove. The Land Cruiser's engine hammered. The road opened up. The industrial zone fell away, replaced by open scrubland, and the truck — visible now in the mirror, still pursuing but losing ground — began to fall back.

And then the radio crackled.

It was a two-way, mounted on the dashboard, and it had been silent the entire trip. Now it erupted with static, and then a voice — calm, professional, utterly devoid of panic:

"Junkyard compound, this is outer perimeter. We have a situation at the main gate. Black Vultures are at the gate. Repeat, Black Vultures are at the main gate. They're requesting a parley. They have — they have a message."

Caleb reached for the radio. His hand was steady. "Copy, John. What's the message?"

Static. Then John's voice, tight with something Finch couldn't identify:

"They want the lavender-haired girl. They're calling her a member of Avarice."

The Land Cruiser was silent except for the engine and the wind and the distant sound of the truck that was still, somewhere behind them, pursuing. Mara looked at Caleb. Caleb looked at Mara. And both of them looked at Finch.

"That's not possible," Caleb said.

"I know," Mara said.

"They think I'm—" Finch started, and her voice came out small and strange and very far away. "They think I'm Avarice?"

"Drive," Caleb said. "Just drive."

They drove. The road stretched ahead of them, grey and empty and endless. Behind them, the truck had turned back, its engine fading into the distance like a held breath finally released. The radio was silent now. The wind filled the space where words had been.

Finch sat in the back of the Land Cruiser and felt the world shift beneath her — not physically, not in any way she could point to, but in the deep, internal way that the world shifts when you realise that the ground you're standing on is not solid. That it never was. That it might never be.

They were going back to a compound where a group called the Black Vultures was waiting for her. A group that wanted her. A group that thought she was something she wasn't.

And somewhere behind them, painted on the wall of a warehouse in black spray paint, the circle-and-A waited like a question she didn't know how to answer.

---

# Chapter Eight: The Ultimatum

They could see the Black Vultures from fifty metres out.

The Junkyard's main gate was flanked by vehicles — three of them, parked in a semicircle that blocked the entrance with the casual authority of people who were accustomed to taking what they wanted. Trucks, mostly. Modified the way everything in this world was modified — welded steel, reinforced beds, the kind of functional ugliness that came from prioritising survival over aesthetics.

People stood around the vehicles. Maybe fifteen, Finch counted. Armed. Crossbows, machetes, a few firearms — real ones, not the improvised weapons she'd seen in the Junkyard. The Black Vultures were better supplied than the Junkyard, which made them more dangerous in the way that having resources always makes a group more dangerous.

At the centre of the semicircle, leaning against the hood of the largest truck, was a woman.

She was tall — taller than Mara, taller than most of the men in her entourage. Lean, almost gaunt, with the kind of angular physicality that suggested not frailty but efficiency, as though every unnecessary ounce had been burned away by years of living hard. Her hair was black, cropped close to her skull. Her skin was dark, sun-weathered, and her eyes — Finch could see them even from this distance — were sharp and bright and utterly devoid of warmth.

She held a machete. Not drawn, not raised, just held — resting against her thigh, the blade catching the grey light. It was longer than Mara's, wider, and it looked like it had been used. Recently.

Mara stopped the Land Cruiser thirty metres from the gate. They sat in the vehicle for a moment, engine idling, looking at the tableau ahead of them.

"That's Sable," Caleb said. "Black Vultures' leader."

"She looks like she eats people," Finch said.

"She doesn't eat people. She does everything else." Caleb opened his door. "Stay in the vehicle."

"Like hell I'm staying in the—"

"Finch." His voice was the leader's voice. Firm, final, brooking no argument. "Stay. In. The vehicle."

He got out. Mara got out. They walked toward the gate, side by side, and Finch watched them go and then did exactly what she'd been told not to do and got out of the Land Cruiser.

She didn't approach the gate. She stood by the vehicle, one hand on the door handle, watching. She could hear the conversation — fragments carried on the wind, terse and careful, the verbal equivalent of two people circling each other with blades drawn.

"Caleb Adderwood," Sable said. Her voice was low and clear and carried effortlessly across the distance. "It's been a while."

"Sable."

"I hear you've been busy. Expanding the compound. Taking in strays." Sable's eyes flicked past Caleb, past Mara, and found Finch. They locked on her with an intensity that made Finch's skin prickle. "I see you've been busy indeed."

"She's nobody," Caleb said. "She's a kid who showed up at our gate. She's not Avarice."

"She's got the look."

"The look?"

"The lavender hair. The face. We've been looking for her — for someone like her — for months. She matches the description of one of Avarice's most wanted operatives."

"That's insane. She's sixteen years old."

"So was the girl who killed thirty-seven of my people in Wollongong." Sable's voice didn't change. It stayed flat and clear and cold, like a blade that had been honed to the point where it no longer needed to be sharp. "She was sixteen too. She walked into our compound with a smile and she walked out thirty-seven bodies later. Avarice trains them young."

"She's not Avarice," Mara said.

"You don't know that."

"I know her. She's been here a week. She's not a killer."

"People change in this world, Mara. People become things they never imagined. Avarice offers them power, purpose, belonging. It's a tempting offer when the alternative is hiding in a scrapyard and eating Dave's porridge."

Caleb stepped forward. "What do you want?"

Sable straightened. She pushed off the hood of the truck and stood to her full height, and in that moment Finch understood why she was feared. Not because of the machete, not because of the people behind her, but because of the absolute certainty that radiated from her like heat from a fire. She was certain of herself, certain of her cause, certain that what she was about to say was right.

"I want the girl," Sable said. "Hand her over, and we walk away. You'll never see us again. The Black Vultures and the Junkyard will have no further conflict."

"And if we refuse?"

Sable looked at him. The machete shifted in her hand — not raised, just adjusted, the way you'd adjust your grip on a tool.

"Then we burn your compound to the ground," she said. "And everyone in it."

The silence that followed was absolute. Finch could hear her own heartbeat. She could hear the wind. She could hear the faint, distant groan of a Hollow somewhere in the empty streets beyond the compound walls.

Caleb didn't move. "You're bluffing."

"I don't bluff. Ask anyone who's dealt with me. I say what I mean, and I mean what I say." Sable paused. "You've got twenty-four hours. Midnight tomorrow. Hand her over, or we start burning."

She turned. Her people turned with her, moving with the practised synchronicity of a group that had rehearsed this — or performed it many times before. They climbed into their vehicles. The engines started. The trucks pulled away from the gate and turned south and were gone, swallowed by the dead streets like they had never been there at all.

The compound was silent.

Caleb and Mara walked back to where Finch was standing. Their faces were grim. The word that moved between them, unspoken, was a weight they all felt but none of them named.

Inside the compound, word had spread. People stood in clusters, talking in low voices. The children were in the dormitory — Finch could see them through the container's open door, huddled together, Reef's arm around Wren. The mood was the mood of people who had been given bad news before and knew that bad news was the only kind that ever came.

The council convened in the communal kitchen. Caleb, Mara, John, Pat, Greg, Dave, and a handful of others. Finch stood at the edge of the group, because no one had told her to leave and she wasn't going to leave.

"Sable's not wrong about one thing," John said. "The Vultures have the firepower to do what she's describing. If they hit us with a full assault, we'd hold — maybe — but the cost would be high."

"Higher than giving up one person?" Greg asked, and then looked at Finch and had the decency to look uncomfortable.

"She's not Avarice," Caleb said. "I know that. Mara knows that. But the Vultures don't. And even if we could convince them — which we can't, because they won't listen — they've lost thirty-seven people. They're grieving. They're angry. They want someone to blame, and they've decided it's her."

"We could fight," Mara said. It was the first time she'd spoken. Her voice was level, but Finch could see the tension in her jaw, the way her hands gripped the edge of the table. "We could fortify. We could make it costly enough that they decide it's not worth it."

"And lose how many of ours in the process?" Caleb said. "Ten? Fifteen? Twenty? For someone they think is an enemy?"

"For someone who's innocent."

"Innocence doesn't stop a crossbow bolt."

The room fell silent. Caleb stood at the head of the table, his hands flat on its surface, his head bowed. He looked, in that moment, like a man who had been asked to solve an equation that had no solution — every variable pointing toward loss, every possible answer wrong.

He looked up. He looked at Finch.

"I won't give you up," he said. "I want you to know that. Whatever happens, I will not hand you to those people."

"Dad—"

"But." He paused. The word sat in the air like a held breath. "But the compound — the people here — they didn't choose this. They didn't choose to have a target painted on them because of someone else's mistake. And I — I can't ask them to die for it."

Mara looked at him. Something passed between them — a conversation in a glance, the kind of shorthand that develops between people who have survived together for years.

"You're not suggesting what I think you're suggesting," Mara said.

"I'm suggesting that we leave."

The word landed like a stone. Leave. The compound. The Junkyard. The only home any of them had known in the years since the world ended.

"If we leave," Caleb said, "the Vultures have no target. They have no grievance. They walk away. The compound survives. Everyone here survives."

"And you don't," Mara said.

"I'm not asking anyone to come with me. Finch and I will leave. Alone. We'll go west, into the mountains, into nowhere. The Vultures won't follow us — they don't care about us as individuals. They care about the perceived threat. Remove the threat, and they remove themselves."

"You're talking about exile," Mara said. "You're talking about walking into the wilderness with no supplies, no shelter, no backup, and no plan."

"I'm talking about protecting these people."

Mara looked at him for a long time. Then she stood up. The chair scraped against the floor. She looked at the faces around the table — John, Pat, Greg, Dave — and then she looked at Finch, and then she looked back at Caleb.

"I'm coming with you," she said.

"Mara—"

"Don't." She held up a hand. "Don't argue with me. You're not walking into the wilderness with a sixteen-year-old and no support. You'll die in three days."

"I've survived seven years—"

"You've survived seven years in a compound with walls and people and supplies. This is different, and you know it." She paused. "I'm coming. That's not a negotiation."

Caleb looked at her. The hardness in his face softened — just for a moment, just a flicker — and in that flicker Finch saw something that she recognised. Love. Not the dramatic, cinematic kind. The quiet, steady, stubborn kind. The kind that doesn't ask permission.

"Alright," Caleb said.

They turned to Finch. She stood at the edge of the table, her hands at her sides, her lavender hair falling across her face. She looked at her father. She looked at Mara. She looked at the faces of the people who had, for one brief week, been something like a community around her.

"I didn't ask for this," she said. "I didn't ask to be here. I didn't ask to look like whoever they think I look like. And I know that people will die if I stay."

"We don't know that for certain," John said.

"We know it well enough."

She looked at Caleb. "When do we leave?"

"Midnight. Before the deadline."

"I need to say goodbye to the kids."

Caleb nodded. "I know."

The hours before midnight passed with the slow,沉重 quality of time that is running out. Finch packed her backpack — the same grey hiking pack she'd found in the service station what felt like a lifetime ago. She added the utility knife Mara had given her, a water bottle, a bag of dried food that Dave pressed into her hands without a word, and a small first aid kit that Pat had prepared with military precision.

She found the children in the dormitory. They were awake. All of them. They sat on their mats in a circle, and they looked up when Finch appeared in the doorway with her pack on her back and her face set in the particular expression that children learn to recognise as the face of someone who is about to say something they don't want to hear.

"You're leaving," Reef said.

"Yeah."

"Because of the Vultures."

"Yeah."

"That's not fair."

"No. It's not."

The silence that followed was thick with all the things children feel and don't have the words for. Finch knelt down so she was at their level — Reef, Spike, Bo, Wren, Cass — and she looked at each of them in turn.

Reef's eyes were red. She was holding it together with the fierce, trembling determination of a twelve-year-old who had decided that crying was a luxury she couldn't afford. But the tears came anyway — not sobbing, just a slow, steady flow, like water finding its level.

"I'll come back," Finch said.

"You don't know that."

"I know that I want to."

Reef nodded. She wiped her face with the back of her hand and sniffed, hard. "You'd better."

Spike stepped forward. He was eleven years old and he stood like he was trying to take up as much space as possible, his shoulders squared, his chin raised. He extended his hand.

Finch took it. His grip was surprisingly firm. He held it for a moment, then released it.

"Don't die," he said.

"I'll try."

"Try harder than that."

Bo came next. She was seven and she had no words for what she was feeling, so she simply threw her arms around Finch's waist and held on with a strength that was startling in someone so small. Finch held her. She held her and felt the small, warm body against hers and the arms around her back and the face pressed into her stomach, and she thought about Earth One and her mother and the house in Brookvale and the colouring book and the purple horse.

"It's okay, Bo," she said. "It's okay."

Bo let go. She didn't say anything. She just stepped back and wiped her eyes and stood very straight, like she was trying to be brave by force of will.

Wren was next. She was six and she was silent in the way that six-year-olds are silent when something is too big for words. She held out her hand. In her palm was a stone — grey, smooth, about the size of a walnut. It was ordinary in every way except for the fact that Wren was giving it to her, which made it the most valuable thing Finch had ever held.

"What is it?" Finch asked.

"It's for luck," Wren said. "Mara says there's no such thing as luck, but I found it anyway."

Finch closed her fingers around the stone. It was warm from Wren's hand. "Thank you," she said. "I'll keep it safe."

"You keep it," Wren said. "It'll keep you."

And then Cass. Cass, who was eight and quiet, who watched everything and said almost nothing, who had the particular stillness of a child who had learned early that the world was a place where observation was safer than participation. She stood in front of Finch and looked up at her with dark, serious eyes.

"You'll come back," she said.

It wasn't a question. It wasn't a request. It was a statement of fact, delivered with the absolute conviction of a child who has decided that something is true and will not entertain the possibility that it isn't.

"Yeah," Finch said, because what else could she say? "I'll come back."

She left the dormitory. She didn't look back. If she looked back, she would see Reef's face, and Spike's hand, and Bo's arms, and Wren's stone, and Cass's certainty, and she would not be able to walk out of the gate. She would not be able to do what needed to be done. She would stay, and people would die, and it would be her fault.

She walked through the compound. The gate was open. The Land Cruiser was waiting. Caleb was in the driver's seat. Mara was in the passenger seat. The engine was running, its growl a low, steady heartbeat in the midnight silence.

Finch climbed into the back. She put her pack on the seat beside her. She held Wren's stone in her pocket and felt its warmth against her palm.

Caleb looked at her in the rear-view mirror. "Ready?"

"No," she said. "But let's go anyway."

The Land Cruiser pulled out of the gate and turned west, into the dark. The compound fell away behind them, its fires shrinking to pinpricks, then to nothing. The road stretched ahead, empty and black and vast, and the Blue Mountains rose in the distance like a wall between them and whatever came next.

They drove into the night. The engine hummed. The headlights carved a tunnel through the dark. And the Junkyard — the walls, the fires, the children, the life — disappeared behind them like a dream you can't quite hold onto when you wake.

---

# Chapter Nine: The Bridge

They drove through the night.

The road climbed gradually, rising out of the coastal plain and into the foothills of the Blue Mountains. The landscape changed as they ascended — the suburban sprawl fell away, replaced by dense bushland that pressed against the road on both sides. The trees were tall here, ancient eucalypts with pale bark and silver-green leaves that caught the headlights and threw them back in fragments.

Caleb drove in silence. Mara sat beside him, awake and watchful, her machete across her knees. Finch sat in the back and watched the dark through the polycarbonate windows and tried to make her mind stop spinning.

The stone in her pocket was warm. She held it and turned it and felt its smoothness against her thumb, and she thought about Wren saying *it'll keep you*, and she thought about Cass saying *you'll come back*, and she thought about the weight of those words — the weight of promises made to children who had already lost too much.

At three in the morning, they stopped. Caleb pulled the Land Cruiser off the road into a clearing, killed the engine, and they sat in the dark and listened to the silence. No Hollows. No Vultures. Just the wind in the trees and the creak of cooling metal.

"Rest," Caleb said. "Two hours. I'll take first watch."

Mara didn't argue. She tilted her seat back and closed her eyes and was, within minutes, either asleep or performing the approximation of sleep that survivors learn to perform when they need to rest but can't afford to be unconscious.

Finch didn't sleep. She lay across the back seat with her head on her pack and stared at the ceiling of the vehicle and listened to the night. The night was loud, in the way that wilderness nights are loud — a dense, layered soundscape of insects and wind and the occasional distant call of an animal she couldn't identify. It was so different from the silence of the dead city that it took her a moment to understand what she was hearing.

Life. The sound of life continuing in the absence of humans. The insects didn't care that the world had ended. The trees didn't care. The wind blew on, indifferent and eternal, and the world turned beneath it the way it had always turned.

Two hours later, they were driving again. Dawn came grey and cold, the sky a uniform white that pressed down on the mountains like a lid. The road narrowed, winding through dense forest, climbing steadily. The air was thinner here, sharper, and when Caleb cracked a window the breeze that came through was cold enough to make Finch pull her hoodie tighter.

By mid-morning, they had reached the mountains proper. The Blue Mountains were everything their name suggested — not blue exactly, but a deep, hazy grey-green that shifted to blue in certain lights, a colour produced by the eucalyptus oils that hung in the air and refracted the sunlight. The road switchedback through steep terrain, climbing along ridgelines that dropped away into valleys thick with mist.

"The bridge is about twenty kilometres ahead," Caleb said, consulting a map he'd spread across the dashboard. "It crosses a valley — one of the deep ones, the ones that weren't properly surveyed until the twenties. The road on the other side goes through to the western plains."

"And then what?" Finch asked from the back seat.

"And then we keep going. West. Into the interior. There are towns out there — small ones, scattered. We find shelter, we find supplies, we regroup."

"With no plan beyond 'keep going.'"

"Plans are a luxury. Right now, direction is enough."

The bridge appeared around a bend in the road, and Finch's breath caught.

It was a suspension bridge — a massive, old thing, built in an era when engineering was as much art as science. The main span was perhaps two hundred metres, suspended from concrete towers that rose from the valley floor like sentinels. The valley below was deep and shadowed, filled with the dark shapes of trees and the thin silver thread of a river far below.

The road surface on the bridge was cracked and weathered but appeared intact. The railings were rusted but present. The concrete towers were stained with age but showed no obvious signs of structural failure.

"It looks old," Finch said.

"It is old. But it's been here for fifty years. It'll hold."

Caleb drove onto the bridge. The Land Cruiser's tyres hummed on the road surface — a different sound than asphalt, a kind of resonant vibration that Finch felt through the seat and into her bones. The railings blurred past on either side, and through them she could see the valley — the trees, the mist, the distant river.

Halfway across.

They were halfway across when the sound came.

It was not a dramatic sound. It was not an explosion or a crack or a groan. It was a sound like a sigh — a deep, structural exhalation, as though the bridge itself had been holding its breath for fifty years and had finally, at last, let go.

The road surface tilted.

Not much. Just a few degrees. Just enough for Finch to feel it in her stomach, that lurching, sickening sensation of gravity shifting beneath you. The Land Cruiser slewed to the left. Caleb fought the wheel. Mara grabbed the dashboard.

"Go!" she shouted. "Go, go, go!"

Caleb accelerated. The engine roared. The Land Cruiser surged forward, its tyres screaming on the tilting road. The concrete tower ahead of them — the far tower, the one that marked the end of the span — seemed to lean, to shift, to move in a way that concrete towers should not move.

The railing on the left side of the bridge gave way. It peeled outward like the lid of a tin, dropping into the void with a sound that was swallowed by the valley before it could reach the bottom. The road surface cracked — a visible, audible crack, running from the left edge to the centre, widening as Finch watched.

The tower leaned further. The cables above them sang — a high, taut, agonised sound, like strings on an instrument being tuned to destruction.

And then the world dropped.

The road surface broke. Not all at once — it fractured in sections, the concrete slabs tilting and separating and falling like dominoes. The Land Cruiser was in the middle of it, caught in the collapse, and Finch felt the seat beneath her disappear and the ceiling above her rush away and the air fill with dust and the sound of breaking concrete and the deep, final groan of a structure that had reached the end of its endurance.

The vehicle fell.

It fell a distance that Finch's mind could not measure — it felt like falling forever, like falling through layers of time and space and memory. She saw the sky above her, white and flat and indifferent. She saw the cables of the bridge whipping past like veins. She saw the trees below, reaching up like hands.

The Land Cruiser hit the water.

The impact was absolute. The world became a roar and a flash and a pressure that crushed her ears and filled her nose and mouth with cold, silty water. She was underwater. She was in the back seat of a vehicle that was sinking in a river at the bottom of a valley in a world that was not her own, and the water was pouring through the shattered windows with a force that pinned her against the seat.

Panic. Pure, white-hot, animal panic. Every nerve in her body screamed at her to fight, to struggle, to push against the water and the weight and the darkness. She kicked. She clawed at the seat belt — she'd been wearing it, she'd been wearing the bloody seat belt — and it wouldn't release, and the water was rising, rising, filling the cab, pressing against her chest.

A hand. Someone's hand, gripping her shoulder. Pulling.

The seat belt clicked. Released. The hand pulled her forward, through the gap where the front windshield had been, and the water rushed around her and she was moving, moving upward, the surface a white blur above her.

They broke the surface.

Finch gasped. The air was sharp and cold and tasted like river water and mud. She was treading water, her head above the surface, her lungs burning. Beside her, Mara — it had been Mara's hand, Mara who had unbuckled her, Mara who had pulled her free.

"Caleb!" Finch screamed. "Where's Caleb?"

"He's — I pushed him out the other side. He's—" Mara's voice was strained, her body labouring against the current. "He's on the bank. I saw him."

"We have to—"

"I know. Swim. Swim to the bank. Go!"

Finch swam. She swam with an energy she didn't know she had, cutting through the cold water with strokes that were clumsy and desperate and effective enough to move her toward the rocky shore she could see ahead. The current was strong — not overwhelming, but present, tugging at her legs and her arms and trying to pull her downstream.

Behind her, she heard Mara.

Not swimming. Struggling. A sound that was half gasp and half cry, muffled by the water. Finch turned.

Mara was in the water. She was close — five metres, maybe less. But she wasn't swimming. She was — Finch couldn't see, the water was churning, the current was pulling — she was pushing something. The Land Cruiser. The Land Cruiser was still in the water, sinking, and Mara was—

"Mara! What are you doing?"

Mara's head broke the surface. Her face was pale, her hair plastered to her skull. She was holding something — the door of the Land Cruiser, or something in the Land Cruiser. A crate. A supply crate that had been in the back.

"Leave it!" Finch screamed. "Mara, leave it!"

"Supplies," Mara gasped. "We need—"

"Forget the supplies! Swim!"

Mara let go of the crate. She turned toward the shore. She began to swim.

But the current had her now. The river, swollen with recent rain, was stronger than it looked, and Mara was tired — tired from the fall, tired from the water, tired from the years of carrying everything and everyone. Her strokes were slow, laboured. She was moving, but not fast enough.

Finch turned back. She swam toward Mara, fighting the current, her arms burning. She reached her just as Mara's head dipped below the surface. She grabbed Mara's jacket — the dark jacket she always wore, the one that made her look like the operational backbone of the Junkyard — and she pulled.

Mara's head came up. She coughed. Water poured from her mouth and nose.

"I've got you," Finch said. "I've got you. Kick. Kick toward the bank."

They swam. Together, side by side, fighting the current. Finch's arms screamed. Her lungs burned. The bank was close — ten metres, five — but the current was pulling them downstream, away from it, and the water was cold, so cold, and Mara was heavy beside her, her movements growing slower, weaker.

And then Mara did something that Finch would replay in her mind for the rest of her life.

Mara stopped swimming.

She turned to Finch. She looked at her with those sharp, clear eyes — the eyes that had assessed her on the first day, the eyes that had trained her, the eyes that had softened, just once, just briefly, when she thought no one was watching. And she smiled.

It was a small smile. A private smile. The smile of someone who has made a decision and is at peace with it.

"Go," Mara said.

"No."

"Finch. Go."

"I'm not leaving you."

"You are. You're going to swim to that bank, and you're going to find your father, and you're going to keep going. You're going to live."

"I won't—"

"Listen to me." Mara's hand found Finch's arm. Her grip was weak — weaker than it should have been, weaker than Finch expected from the woman who had thrown her to the ground during knife training and told her to get up and do it again. "I have loved your father for six years. I have loved this compound. I have loved those children. And I have loved—" She paused. Her eyes brightened. "I have loved having you here, even if it was only for a week. Even if it was never enough."

"Mara—"

"Swim. Now. Before I can't hold on any more."

The current pulled. The water surged. Mara's grip on Finch's arm loosened, and Finch felt the separation beginning — felt the distance opening between them, inch by inch, the way distance opens between two people when one of them is letting go.

Finch kicked. She kicked toward the bank with everything she had, and she felt Mara's hand slip from her arm, and she turned and saw Mara — saw her clearly, in the flat grey light of the mountain morning, her dark hair floating around her face like a halo, her eyes open, her expression calm — and then the current took her, and she was gone.

Finch reached the bank. She pulled herself out of the water and collapsed on the rocks and lay there, shivering, gasping, her body trembling with cold and exhaustion and something worse than either of those things.

Caleb was there. He was on the bank, ten metres upstream, on his hands and knees. He was alive. He was wet and bleeding from a cut on his forehead and he was alive. He looked at Finch. He looked at the river.

"Where's Mara?" he asked.

Finch couldn't answer. She opened her mouth and what came out was not a word but a sound — a sound that came from somewhere deeper than language, deeper than thought, from the place where grief lives before it becomes grief. It was the sound of losing someone you had only just begun to know, someone who had become essential in the space of a week, someone who had given her life so that Finch could have hers.

Caleb understood. He understood because he saw Finch's face, and because the river was empty, and because Mara was not on the bank. He sat on the rocks beside his daughter and he put his arm around her shoulders and he held her while she shook.

They sat there for a long time. The river flowed past, indifferent. The mist hung in the valley like a curtain. The bridge — or what was left of it — creaked and groaned above them, its broken span hanging like a broken wing.

Eventually, Caleb stood. He pulled Finch to her feet. They were wet, they were cold, they had nothing except the clothes on their backs and a knife and a backpack that had somehow survived the swim. The Land Cruiser was gone. The supplies were gone. Mara was gone.

"We have to keep moving," Caleb said. His voice was flat. Not cold — flat. The flatness of a man who has compressed his grief into a space small enough to carry, because the alternative was to be crushed by it.

"Yeah," Finch said. "We have to keep moving."

They walked. They walked along the riverbank, following the water downstream, because the river would lead somewhere and somewhere was better than here. They walked in wet clothes through cold air and their teeth chattered and their skin was pale and their footsteps left dark prints on the rocks.

They walked until the sun began to set, and then they found a sheltered spot beneath an overhang of rock, and they huddled together for warmth, and they did not speak about Mara because there were no words for what had happened and the silence was less painful than the attempt.

Finch held Wren's stone in her pocket. It was cold now. It had been cold since the river. But she held it anyway, and she thought about what Wren had said — *it'll keep you* — and she thought about Mara's face in the water, and the smile, and the word *go*, and she closed her eyes and let the tears come.

They came. They came the way the river came — steadily, powerfully, impossible to stop. She cried for Mara, and she cried for the children, and she cried for her mother, alone in a house in Brookvale, and she cried for her father, who held her in the dark and said nothing because nothing needed to be said.

In the morning, they would walk again. But tonight, they grieved.

---

# Chapter Ten: New Territory

The river led them west.

They walked for two days along its bank, following the water through a valley that grew narrower as they went, the walls closing in on either side like the pages of a book being slowly shut. The terrain was rough — loose rocks, fallen trees, the occasional stretch of bank so steep that they had to wade through shallows to get around it. They moved slowly, carefully, conserving energy and heat and whatever reserves of hope they had left.

On the first morning, before they started walking, Caleb stopped at a bend in the river and pulled a strip of fabric from the edge of his jacket. It was dark — the same dark that Mara's jacket had been. He had torn it during the night, Finch realised. While she was sleeping, or pretending to sleep, he had torn a piece from the only thing he had left of Mara, and now he held it in his hands and looked at it for a long time.

He set it on a flat rock. He placed a stone on top of it. He stood there for a moment, his head bowed, his hands at his sides, and then he turned and walked on.

Finch stopped at the rock. She looked at the fabric — a dark rectangle, held down by a grey stone, already beginning to absorb the moisture of the morning air. It was the smallest memorial she had ever seen. It was the most important thing in the world.

She added her own stone. Small, round, from the riverbed. She set it beside Caleb's. She didn't say anything. She didn't know what to say. The dead don't need words. They need the living to remember, and that was enough.

They walked.

The valley opened on the second day, the walls falling away, the river widening into a slow, deep flow that reflected the grey sky like a mirror. The bushland thinned, replaced by open grassland dotted with eucalypts and the occasional rocky outcrop. The air was warmer here, or maybe they were just getting used to the cold.

They talked, on the second day. Not about Mara — that would come later, in quieter moments, in the watchtower that wasn't there any more. They talked about the immediate, the practical, the things that needed to be discussed in order to keep walking.

"Cass said I'd come back," Finch said.

"We might."

"Do you think we will?"

Caleb was quiet for a long time. "I think we'll find somewhere. Somewhere safe enough to stop, long enough to think. And then we'll decide."

"That's not an answer."

"No. It's not."

They reached Bathurst in the late afternoon of the second day.

The town appeared gradually, the way towns do when you're approaching from the bush — a scattering of buildings, then more, then streets, then the particular density of a settlement that has been built and lived in and left. Bathurst was not destroyed the way Sydney had been destroyed. The Hollows had passed through, but the town had been small enough, remote enough, that the worst of the damage was superficial. Buildings stood. Streets were intact. The houses had doors and windows and roofs, though many of the windows were dark and many of the doors hung open.

They entered cautiously, the way they'd entered everywhere since the bridge — Caleb in front with the knife, Finch behind with her hand on the utility blade. The streets were empty. The town had that particular quality of emptiness that all dead towns share — not the dramatic emptiness of ruins, but the quieter emptiness of a place where life simply stopped.

They found a house — intact, secure, with a front door that locked and curtains that drew. They searched it room by room, the way you search a space in this world: methodically, silently, with the assumption that anything could be anywhere. The house was empty. It had been a family home — photos on the walls, children's drawings on the fridge, a bookshelf still full of paperbacks. Someone had left in a hurry and had not come back.

Finch found a jacket in the bedroom closet — men's, too large, but warm. She found food in the kitchen — canned goods, a packet of rice, a jar of honey that was still good. She found a candle and matches. She found a blanket.

They ate in the kitchen, sitting on the floor, surrounded by the ghosts of a family they would never know. The food was cold and tasteless and it was the best meal Finch had eaten since leaving the Junkyard, because it was food and they were alive and for now, that was enough.

In the morning, Caleb found a campus.

It was a college — or what had been a college. A regional campus, the kind that satellite universities set up in country towns, with a cluster of buildings arranged around a central courtyard. The buildings were intact. The courtyard was overgrown, grass pushing through the paving stones, but the structures themselves were solid.

"Shelter," Caleb said. "Real shelter. We could stay here."

"For how long?"

"As long as we need to."

They made the campus their base. It took a day to secure the buildings, to check for Hollows, to establish the basics of habitation — water, food storage, a watchpoint. The campus was defensible. The buildings were arranged in a way that created natural chokepoints, and the courtyard was hidden from the road by a perimeter wall.

On the third day in Bathurst, they heard the engine.

It was distant at first — a low, mechanical growl, growing steadily louder. Caleb and Finch froze. They were in the courtyard, sorting supplies, and the sound cut through the quiet like a blade.

"Inside," Caleb said. "Now."

They were inside the main building, in a ground-floor classroom with the door shut and the curtains drawn, when the vehicle arrived. They heard it pull up outside the campus wall. They heard doors open and close. They heard voices.

Multiple voices. At least four, maybe more. And one voice that was clearer than the others — a man's voice, young, carrying the particular authority of someone who was used to being listened to.

"Search the buildings. Carefully. They might be in here."

Finch looked at Caleb. Caleb looked at Finch. His hand was on the knife. His jaw was set.

The footsteps came. Through the building, down the corridor, past the classrooms. They were methodical — not the shuffling of Hollows, not the chaotic movement of a panic. They were searching. Systematically, room by room.

The classroom door opened.

A young man stood in the doorway. He was maybe twenty — lean, sun-darkened, with close-cropped hair and a scar that ran from his left ear to his jaw. He held a machete at his side and he was watching them with the kind of focus that comes from years of having to decide very quickly whether someone was a threat.

He looked at Caleb. He looked at Finch.

"Lavender hair," he said. "That's her."

"Don't—" Caleb started.

"Relax. I'm not going to hurt you." The young man stepped back from the door, lowering the machete. "My name is Cameryn. And you, mate, have got one hell of a story ahead of you."

Finch stared at him. Not at his face — at something else. Something about the way he stood, the way he held himself, the particular angle of his jaw. The scar. The close-cropped hair. The lean frame.

She had seen this face before. Or not this face — a face like it. A face that was almost the same but not quite, the way a reflection is almost the same but not quite.

"You look like Liam," she said.

Cameryn's expression changed. The wariness didn't leave, but something else surfaced — surprise, maybe, or recognition.

"You know Liam?"

"He found me. On the highway. He brought me to the Junkyard."

Cameryn was quiet for a moment. Then he said, "He's my brother."

The information landed and settled. Liam's brother. The same face, the same build, the same scar — or a version of it, the parallel-world version, the Earth Two echo.

"You're Black Vultures," Caleb said. It wasn't a question.

"We were," Cameryn said. "Some of us. A splinter group. We split off about a year ago — Sable's methods got too extreme, even for us. We've been operating independently since then."

"You attacked the Junkyard."

"We didn't. Sable did. We weren't part of that." Cameryn looked at Finch. "You're the one she's looking for. The one she thinks is Avarice."

"I'm not Avarice."

"I know. Sable's wrong about you. She's wrong about a lot of things. That's why we left." He paused. "But she's not wrong about Avarice. They're real, and they're getting stronger."

He told them. Avarice had consolidated in the snowy mountains north — the high country, the places where the Hollows were sparse and the terrain was harsh enough to deter casual pursuit. They had a compound, or something like it. A base of operations. From there, they launched raids on settlements across the region, taking supplies, taking people, leaving the circle-and-A burned into whatever they touched.

"How many?" Caleb asked.

"Hundreds, maybe. They recruit. They take people — willing or otherwise. They've got weapons, they've got vehicles, and they've got a leader who's smart enough to stay hidden."

"And the girl? The one Sable thinks I am?"

Cameryn looked at her. "There's a girl in Avarice. Lavender hair. She's — she's done things. Bad things. Sable lost people to her. That's why she's hunting."

"It's not me."

"I believe you. But Sable won't. Not until she sees proof, and even then, maybe not."

Finch felt the weight of the misunderstanding settle on her shoulders like a physical thing. She looked like someone who had committed atrocities. She wore the face of a killer. And there was nothing she could do about it except keep walking and hope that the truth was enough.

"There's a place," Cameryn said. "Beyond the mountains. To the north, past the snow line. A farm. Self-sufficient, fortified, led by people who've been keeping out of the conflicts. They take in refugees. They don't ask questions."

"How do you know about it?"

"I've been there. Traded with them. They're good people. Real good. If you could get there — past Avarice, past the snow, through the mountains — you'd be safe. Both of you."

"The mountains," Caleb said. "Through Avarice territory."

"Or around. There's a route — longer, but safer. Goes through the western passes. Avarice doesn't patrol there as heavily. It's a hard walk, but it's possible."

Caleb looked at Finch. She looked at him. The question hung between them, unspoken but understood: Was this what they were doing now? Walking from one impossible thing to the next, following a trail of whispered safe harbours and distant hopes, always one step ahead of someone who wanted them dead?

"We'll go through," Finch said.

Caleb studied her face. "You're sure?"

"No. But I'm tired of running. And I'm tired of being afraid of someone else's war." She paused. "Besides, Wren gave me a stone. I've got luck."

Caleb didn't smile. But something shifted in his eyes — something that might, in better times, have been pride.

Cameryn gave them supplies before they left — food, water, a compass, a hand-drawn map of the mountain passes. He walked them to the edge of Bathurst and pointed north, toward the blue-grey wall of the mountains that rose above the town like a challenge.

"One more thing," Cameryn said, as they turned to go. "Liam — my brother. He's out there somewhere. Ranging. If you find him, tell him—" He stopped. Swallowed. "Tell him Cam says come home."

"We will," Finch said.

They walked. North, toward the mountains. The road climbed. The air cooled. The bushland thickened, the eucalypts giving way to denser forest, the ground beneath their feet turning from red earth to grey stone.

Behind them, Bathurst shrank and vanished. Behind it, the river flowed on, carrying with it the memory of a dark jacket floating on the current and a smile that meant *go* and a piece of fabric on a rock with a stone on top.

Ahead, the mountains waited. Snow-capped, ancient, indifferent to the wars of the people who moved beneath them. Somewhere in their depths, Avarice waited too, with its circle-and-A and its lavender-haired operative and its hunger for destruction.

But ahead of all of that — beyond the snow, beyond the passes, beyond the danger — there was a farm. A safe harbor. A place where people had decided that survival was not enough, that living was what mattered, and that the two were not the same.

Finch walked beside her father. The stone was warm in her pocket. The mountains rose. The sky was grey and vast and full of the promise of snow.

She didn't know what was coming. She didn't know if they would make it. She didn't know if the farm was real or if the safe harbor was a myth or if the mountains would swallow them whole.

But she was alive. Her father was alive. And somewhere behind her, carved into the fabric of a world that was not her own, was the story of a woman named Mara who had loved fiercely and given everything and asked for nothing.

Finch walked. The mountains waited. The story continued.

---

End of Part One: The World That Wasn't

---

## Part Two: The Mirror

---

# Chapter Eleven: Into the Snow

They left Bathurst on a morning so cold that Finch could see her breath crystallise in front of her face and hang there for a moment before the wind tore it away.

Cameryn had given them supplies — food, water, a better set of winter gear, including coats that were at least two sizes too large for Finch but warm enough to matter. He had also given them a map, hand-drawn on the back of a flattened cardboard box, showing the mountain passes and the locations of known Avarice patrols.

"The mountain passes are Avarice territory," Cameryn had said, tapping the map with a calloused finger. "Every trail, every road, every goat track that goes through this range. If they find you, they'll kill you. Or worse."

"Unless they think I'm their leader," Finch had said.

Cameryn had looked at her with an expression that was difficult to read — something between sympathy and fear. "Yeah. Unless that. But I wouldn't count on it. The real one — the real Finch, if that's who she is — she's got a reputation. A very specific reputation. If you show up looking like her but acting like you, they might get confused. And confused people make mistakes."

"What kind of mistakes?"

"The kind that leave you dead."

They headed north, following the road out of Bathurst into the rising country. The landscape changed as they climbed — the eucalyptus forests gave way to snow gum, their trunks pale and smooth against the dark earth, their canopies thinning as the altitude increased. The air thinned with them. The temperature dropped. Snow appeared on the ground in patches, then in drifts, then in deep, unbroken sheets that swallowed the road and everything on it.

Finch had never seen snow. Not like this. In Sydney, snow was a news story — something that happened somewhere else, reported on with the mild fascination of people who would never experience it firsthand. But here it was, knee-deep and then waist-deep, muffling every sound and turning the landscape into something abstract and beautiful and terrifying. The trees were heavier with it, their branches bending under the weight, and when the wind blew, curtains of white powder fell from the canopy in slow, almost gentle cascades. The sky was a low ceiling of grey, pressing down on the mountain, and the light was flat and shadowless, the kind of light that made distance impossible to judge. A ridgeline that looked fifty metres away might have been a hundred. A valley that seemed shallow might have been a hundred metres deep. The snow rewrote the world's geometry, and Finch learned quickly to distrust her eyes.

The cold was worse than anything she had experienced. It was not the damp cold of the coast, which found its way through clothing and settled into your skin. This was a dry, penetrating cold that went deeper — past the skin, past the muscle, into the bone itself. It settled into her joints and made them stiff. It found the gaps in her clothing — the space between glove and sleeve, the exposed strip of neck above her collar — and it poured in like water through cracks. Her fingers went numb within the first hour, and stayed numb. Her toes followed. Her face, exposed to the wind, burned with a cold that felt like heat, a paradox that made her dizzy when she turned her head too quickly.

They walked for a day and a half. Caleb led the way, breaking trail through the snow, his long legs punching through the crust and sinking into the powder beneath. Finch followed in his footsteps, grateful for every one, and the cold ate into her bones with the patient persistence of something that had all the time in the world. Each step was an effort — the snow grabbing at her legs, the weight of the pack on her shoulders, the thin air burning her lungs. She developed a rhythm: step, breathe, step, breathe. She did not allow herself to think beyond the next step. If she thought about how far they had to go, or how much colder it would get, or how many days they might need to walk like this, the task would become impossible. So she reduced the world to a single footfall and then another, and another, and she kept moving.

Caleb was struggling more than he let on. She could see it in the set of his shoulders, in the way he paused more frequently, in the ragged edge of his breathing. He was older, and the weeks of captivity in the Avarice compound had weakened him — the sparse food, the cold cell, the sleepless nights of planning and listening. He was leaner than when she had last seen him, his face sharper, his eyes more deeply set. But he didn't complain. He didn't slow down. He broke trail with the same methodical determination he brought to everything, and Finch followed, and neither of them said a word about how hard it was.

They didn't talk much. The cold made conversation difficult — every word came out as a puff of white, and the wind snatched it away before it could reach the other person. So they walked in silence, father and daughter, two exiles in a frozen world, and the mountains closed around them like a fist. The silence was not empty, though. It was full of the sounds of snow — the creak of it underfoot, the soft hiss of it falling from branches, the distant, almost sub-audible groan of a glacier somewhere high above, shifting its weight. Finch found that she could read the mountain through these sounds, the way she had once read the city through traffic and voices and the hum of electricity. The mountain spoke, if you knew how to listen.

At night they sheltered where they could — in the lee of a rock face, beneath the overhang of a cliff, once in the hollowed-out shell of an old ski lift station whose corrugated walls still blocked the wind. They ate cold, because any fire would be visible for miles against the white landscape, and they huddled together for warmth, their bodies pressed back to back, sharing what little heat they had. Finch lay awake for hours in those shelters, staring at the darkness, feeling the cold seep into her from every direction — from the snow beneath her, the air above her, the stone at her back. Sleep came in fragments, thin and shallow, more like brief absences than rest. She dreamed of warmth — her mother's kitchen, the light through the doorway, the smell of cooking — and woke each time to the reality of the mountain, and the cold, and the knowledge that they were still far from safety.

On the second day, they found the first marking.

It was painted on a rock face beside the trail — the circle-and-A symbol, bright red against the grey stone. It was crude but clear, a territorial marker, and Caleb stopped when he saw it and studied it with the expression of someone reading a warning sign. The red paint was vivid against the snow, almost aggressive in its brightness, as though it were shouting. Finch stared at it and felt something tighten in her chest — not fear, exactly, but the precursor to fear, the moment when the mind begins to calculate odds and find them wanting.

"This means we're in their range," he said. "From here on, we move quiet. No talking unless necessary. No fire. No light."

They moved through the mountains like ghosts. The snow was deeper here, up to Finch's thighs in places, and progress was agonisingly slow. Caleb broke trail, his breath coming in ragged gasps, and Finch followed, sinking and struggling and trying not to think about how cold she was. More markings appeared as they climbed — painted on rocks, carved into tree trunks, even spray-painted on the side of an abandoned ski lift pylon. The frequency of them was its own message: you are entering a place that belongs to someone else, and they have been here recently.

She thought about Mara instead. About the river, the bridge, the cold water. About the cairn of stones on the bank. About the way Mara's hand had felt on her arm when she pulled her from the water — strong, certain, final. She thought about the words Mara had said — *I knew a Caleb once* — and the way her voice had changed, and the thread of connection it implied. She held the thought like a warm stone in her hand, turning it over, letting it occupy the space that fear might otherwise have filled.

On the second afternoon, they saw the compound.

It appeared first as a dark smudge against the white mountain, a disruption in the landscape that took several minutes to resolve into its component parts. Then, gradually, details emerged — straight lines where nature made curves, dark shapes that were too regular to be trees, a glint of light off metal. Caleb saw it too. He stopped, dropped to one knee in the snow, and studied the view through the gap between two rocks. Finch crouched beside him and looked.

It was built into the side of a mountain, using the remains of what had once been a ski resort. The main building — a long, low structure that had probably been a lodge — was reinforced with steel and concrete. The original timber-frame walls had been sheathed in corrugated iron, bolted on with industrial fixings that caught the flat light. Watchtowers on either end, made from stacked shipping containers, their tops fitted with floodlights that were dark now but would blaze to life at dusk. A perimeter wall made from more containers and snow-packed earth, the gaps filled with welded steel and barbed wire. Vehicles parked inside the wall, including motorcycles and at least one armoured truck. Smoke rose from several points within the compound — cooking fires, heaters, the breathing machinery of a place where people lived and worked.

And people. Avarice members, moving through the compound with the practised efficiency of a military operation. They wore dark clothes and leather jackets, and every one of them bore the circle-and-A symbol. They carried weapons — blades, clubs, firearms. They were young, most of them, no older than twenty-five, and they moved with the casual confidence of people who had never lost a fight. Finch counted at least twenty visible from their vantage point, but there were surely more inside the buildings, out of sight. The compound was large — much larger than she had expected — and it was organised with a precision that spoke of long planning and rigid discipline. Paths between the buildings were cleared of snow. Equipment was stacked in neat rows. Nothing was wasted, nothing was random.

"We go around," Caleb whispered. "Stay below the ridgeline. Keep out of sight."

They tried. They picked their way along a slope above the compound, staying below the tree line, keeping the rocks between them and the watchtowers. For twenty minutes, it worked. They moved carefully, deliberately, placing each footstep with precision, and the compound below continued its routines undisturbed. The snow was deep here but not as deep as on the exposed slopes — the trees broke the wind, and their canopy caught some of the snowfall, leaving patches of bare earth and frozen leaf litter that were easier to walk on. Caleb moved from tree to tree, using the trunks as cover, and Finch followed his example, pressing herself against the bark, listening for sounds that didn't belong to the mountain.

For a while, they heard nothing but the wind and the crunch of their own footsteps. The compound's sounds — voices, the clank of metal, the distant growl of an engine — drifted up to them faintly, muffled by distance and snow. Finch allowed herself a fraction of hope. They were past the worst of it. Another hundred metres and they would be below the ridgeline, out of sight, beyond the patrol range. Another hundred metres and—

Then the ambush came.

One moment they were moving along the ridge. The next moment, there were people everywhere — emerging from behind rocks, from snow banks, from positions they hadn't even known existed. Five, then ten, then fifteen, surrounding them in a closing circle with weapons drawn and expressions that ranged from curious to hostile. They appeared so quickly and so silently that Finch's mind struggled to process the transition — from alone to surrounded, from moving to trapped. One second the forest was empty. The next it was full of people, and every one of them was looking at her, and every one of them was armed.

Finch's blood went cold. Not metaphorically — she felt it happen, a physical sensation, a draining of warmth from her extremities as her body redirected resources to her core. Her hands went numb. Her vision sharpened. The world narrowed to a tunnel: the weapons, the faces, the closing circle.

Caleb's knife was in his hand. Finch reached for hers.

"Easy," said a voice from behind them. "Real easy. Drop the steel."

They turned. A man stood on the ridge above them, looking down with the patient expression of someone who had done this many times. He was muscular, heavyset, with a scar across his nose and a shotgun pointed at Caleb's chest. His breath steamed in the cold air. His eyes were flat and unhurried, the eyes of someone who held every card and knew it. Behind him, two more Avarice members flanked the ridgeline, their weapons raised, their stances wide and balanced.

Finch's heart was hammering so hard she could feel it in her throat. She looked at the circle of armed people around them and counted — fifteen, exactly as Cameryn had warned — and she felt the geometry of the situation close around her like a trap. There was no way to fight. Not like this. Not outnumbered seven to one with weapons trained on them from every angle. Caleb knew it too. She could see it in the set of his jaw, in the way his knife hand trembled — not with fear, but with the effort of holding back the instinct to fight.

"There's two of us and fifteen of you," Caleb said. "We're not looking for trouble."

"Then you shouldn't have come to our door." The man gestured with the shotgun. "Move. Now. Down the hill. Into the compound."

Caleb looked at Finch. It was a quick look, a fraction of a second, but she read everything in it — the apology, the calculation, the wordless instruction to stay close and stay alive. She nodded. He lowered his knife. One of the Avarice members stepped forward and took it from his hand, then turned to Finch and took hers. The loss of the knife was like losing a limb. She felt the empty space at her belt as though it were a wound.

They moved.

The walk down to the compound was the longest Finch had ever made. The Avarice members surrounded them in a loose formation — four in front, four behind, the rest flanking — and they moved with a practised ease that told Finch this was routine. They had done this before. Many times. They knew every step of the route, every handhold in the rock, every place where the snow was treacherous. They moved through their territory the way fish move through water, and Finch and Caleb were the foreign objects, the contaminants, the things that didn't belong.

The cold pressed in from every direction. Finch's legs ached with the effort of descending the slope — the snow was deep and uneven, and the Avarice members, who knew the terrain, moved faster than she could comfortably follow. She stumbled twice, catching herself on rocks, feeling the ice bite through her gloves. No one helped her. No one slowed down. They moved with the impersonal efficiency of people escorting cargo, and Finch reminded herself that cargo was exactly what she was.

As they descended, the compound grew larger and more detailed. The walls resolved into individual containers, each one bearing scorch marks and dents that spoke of use and age. The watchtowers loomed above, their floodlights dark now but ominous in their silence, promising illumination when darkness fell. The main lodge — the long, low building she had seen from the ridge — was closer now, its reinforced walls massive and solid against the snow. The Avarice symbol was everywhere — painted on walls, burned into doors, stamped on equipment. It was not just a mark of ownership. It was a declaration. This place, everything in it, every person who moved through it — all of it belonged to the circle-and-A.
