Inside the compound, the scale of Avarice's operation became clear. This was not a ragtag group of survivors. This was an army. The lodge had been converted into a command centre. The surrounding structures — former ski cabins, storage buildings — had been repurposed into barracks, armouries, and something that looked like a workshop. People trained in the open area — hand-to-hand combat, weapons handling, tactical movement. Two figures grappled in the snow, their movements precise and violent, one throwing the other to the ground with a controlled aggression that was more technique than anger. A third person nearby practiced knife strikes against a wooden post, the blade hitting the same spot again and again with a rhythm that was almost meditative. Others watched, or waited their turn, or moved between buildings with equipment, their breath rising in white plumes.

The people they passed looked at Finch and Caleb with expressions that ranged from indifference to contempt. A few openly stared. One — a young woman with a shaved head and a scar across her throat — stepped close enough to Finch to touch her, and did, reaching out with one finger to lift Finch's chin and examine her face. The touch was proprietary, invasive, the way you'd examine livestock at market. Finch pulled her chin away, and the young woman smiled — a thin, knowing smile — and stepped back.

They were brought to the main building and taken to a room that had been cleared of furniture and replaced with a single chair. The room smelled of cold concrete and something metallic — old blood, maybe, or the residue of the room's previous function, whatever that had been. The walls were bare, the floor stained, the single window blocked with welded steel. A light hung from the ceiling on a length of wire, its bulb naked and harsh. It was a room designed for interrogation, or for punishment, or for both.

Caleb was separated from Finch, taken in a different direction, and she heard his voice — sharp, angry — before a door closed and cut it off. The sound of his voice being cut short was worse than the sound of his anger. It was the sound of connection being severed, of the one person she trusted in this world being pulled away from her.

"Where are you taking him?"

"Shut up. Sit down."

The guard who said it was young — barely older than Finch — but he said it with the authority of someone who had been given power and intended to use it. He pushed her toward the chair. She stumbled, caught herself on the armrest, and sat. The guard left. The door closed. The lock turned.

She sat. The room was cold and bare and lit by a single overhead light. She sat in the chair and waited, and the minutes passed, and the cold seeped into her bones, and she thought about Wren's stone in her pocket and her father's silver eyes and the cairn of river stones on the bank of a river that was very far away. The silence was absolute — no sound of Caleb, no sound of footsteps, no sound of the wind outside. Just the hum of the light bulb and the slow, steady drip of condensation from the ceiling and the sound of her own breathing, which seemed very loud in the emptiness.

She thought about the Avarice members. Their youth. Their discipline. The way they had moved through the compound with the casual confidence of people who had never been challenged. She thought about the symbols on the walls, the weapons on their belts, the scars on their hands. She thought about what Cameryn had said — *the kind that leave you dead* — and she tried to calculate her chances and found she couldn't. She didn't know enough. She didn't know what they wanted, or what they would do, or whether the girl with the lavender hair who ran this place would see Finch as a curiosity or a threat.

The door opened.

The woman who walked in was not what Finch expected. She was young — Finch's age, or close to it — and she was tall, almost exactly Finch's height, and she moved with the fluid, coiled energy of someone who was always ready to fight. She wore a leather jacket — dark, scuffed, with the Avarice symbol burned into the left shoulder — and beneath it, a muscular frame that spoke of years of training. Her arms were bare from the elbow down, and they were corded with muscle and covered in scars — long, pale lines that ran across her forearms like latitude marks on a globe.

Her hair was lavender.

Finch stared. The other woman stared back. The same face — not identical, not exactly, but close enough to be a mirror. The same angular jaw, the same pale grey-blue eyes, the same tall frame. But where Finch was lanky and uncoordinated, this woman was solid and powerful. Where Finch looked uncertain, this woman looked certain. Absolutely, terrifyingly certain. There was a weight to her presence that had nothing to do with physical mass — a gravity, a certainty, the kind of authority that could not be faked and could not be resisted.

"Well," the other Finch said. "Aren't you interesting."

She pulled up a chair, sat down across from Finch, and crossed her legs. The leather of her jacket creaked. The circle-and-A on her shoulder caught the light. Up close, Finch could see more details — the small nick on her lower lip, the faint scar above her left eyebrow, the way her eyelashes were darker at the tips than at the roots. She could see the way the other Finch's hands rested on her knees — relaxed, open, but ready. Always ready.

"You've got my face," she said.

"I could say the same thing."

"My name is Finch Adderwood. This is my territory. My people. My world." She leaned forward. "And you are either the most dangerous person who's ever walked into my compound, or the stupidest. And I haven't decided which yet."

"Neither. I'm nobody. I'm just—"

"Just what? Lost? Wandered in from the highway? Found your way up the mountain by accident?" The other Finch smiled. It was a thin, hard smile. "Nobody wanders into Avarice territory by accident. You're here for a reason. I just haven't figured out what it is yet."

"I'm from Earth One."

The words came out before Finch could stop them. She didn't know why she said them — desperation, maybe, or the instinct that sometimes the truth is the only weapon you have. The room was cold, and the light was harsh, and the other Finch's silver eyes were fixed on her with an intensity that made Finch feel transparent, as though every thought and every fear were visible beneath her skin.

The other Finch went very still. The smile didn't change, but something behind it did — a flicker of something that might have been recognition, or memory, or pain.

"Earth One," she repeated.

"I woke up on the highway three weeks ago. I don't know how I got here. My father — he was taken from Earth One to Earth Two by Robert Qwantum. There was a lab. Experiments. He escaped. He's been here ever since."

"Robert Qwantum." The other Finch said the name the way Caleb had said it — like a curse. "I know that name."

"Then you know I'm telling the truth."

"I know you're telling me something." The other Finch stood up. She was taller than Finch had realised — exactly Finch's height, down to the centimetre. "Lock her up. Lock the man up too. Separate cells. I want to think about this."

"Think about what?"

The other Finch leaned close. Her breath was warm on Finch's face. Her eyes were silver, and in them Finch could see something she recognised — the same emptiness, the same hunger for something that had been taken away.

"Think about what to do with you," she said. "Because there can't be two of us. The world isn't big enough for that."

She left. The door closed. The lock turned.

And Finch Adderwood of Earth One sat in a bare room in a compound in the snowy mountains and wondered, not for the first time, how exactly she had gotten here and whether she was going to survive. The light hummed above her. The cold pressed in from every direction. And somewhere beyond the walls, beyond the snow, beyond the mountains, the world continued its slow, indifferent turning, unconcerned with the fate of a girl in a chair in a room she should never have entered.

---

# Chapter Twelve: The Tyrant's Story

They kept Finch in the cell for three days.

The cell was a former ski storage room — small, windowless, cold. It had been designed to hold equipment, not people, and it had the impersonal, functional quality of a space that didn't care what was kept inside it. The walls were unfinished concrete, pitted and stained, with brackets still bolted to them where racks of skis had once hung. The floor was bare, the concrete cold enough to burn through the thin sleeping mat that was her only barrier against it. A bucket in the corner served as a toilet, and the fact that it was there, positioned with deliberate visibility, was its own kind of statement: you are not a guest here. You are cargo.

A light stayed on twenty-four hours, a bare bulb in a steel cage on the ceiling, buzzing with the monotonous persistence of a fly. It never dimmed, never flickered, never changed. It was always the same — the same brightness, the same hum, the same flat, shadowless illumination that turned the concrete grey and her skin the colour of old paper. Finch learned to hate it the way you learn to hate a sound that never stops — not all at once, but gradually, the irritation building layer by layer until it became a kind of low-grade fury that sat behind her eyes and pulsed in time with the buzzing.

They brought her food once a day — rice and meat, not bad, not good — and water, and that was all. The food arrived at irregular intervals, which was its own cruelty. Sometimes it came in what she guessed was the morning, sometimes in the evening, sometimes in the dead hours when she had lost track of time entirely. A guard — she never saw the same one twice — would slide a tray through the slot in the door, and the tray would clatter against the concrete, and the slot would close, and she would be alone again.

She ate because eating was a kind of defiance. Because if she stopped eating, she would be giving up, and she was not ready to give up. She ate the rice and the meat and drank the water and tried not to think about how long this would last.

Caleb was somewhere else. She could hear him sometimes, muffled through the walls — shouting, demanding to see his daughter, threatening in the quiet, controlled way that meant he was genuinely angry. His voice was a low, continuous pressure against the concrete, like water against a dam. The Avarice members ignored him. He was a problem for later.

On the third day, the other Finch came back.

She brought a chair. She carried it into the cell with one hand, the way you'd carry a tool, and set it down across from Finch with a scrape of metal on concrete that set Finch's teeth on edge. She sat down, crossed her legs, and studied Finch with those silver eyes, and Finch studied her back, and the two of them sat in the buzzing silence like mirror images, each one seeing in the other the ghost of a life that might have been.

"You really are from Earth One," the other Finch said. It wasn't a question.

"How do you know that?"

"Because I can tell. I can tell you're — soft. Unbroken. You haven't been through what I've been through. You haven't seen what I've seen. You've got that—" She gestured vaguely, a sweep of her hand that encompassed everything Finch was and everything she wasn't. "That innocence. It's almost nauseating."

"You don't know anything about me."

"I know everything about you. I know you because you're me. Different circumstances, different choices, different scars — but the same. Underneath." She leaned forward, and the chair creaked under her weight, and Finch saw the muscles in her forearms tighten, the veins standing out against skin that was tanned and scarred and utterly unlike Finch's own pale, unmarked arms. "I was six when it happened. The outbreak. I was six years old, and I was in my house with my mum and dad, and they came through the window. The Hollows. Six of them. They came through the kitchen window like water through a crack, and my dad tried to fight them off, and my mum tried to get me to the back door, and—"

She stopped. Her jaw tightened. Finch saw something in those silver eyes — something deep and old and still bleeding, a wound that had never been allowed to close.

"They ate my parents in front of me," the other Finch said. "Right in front of me. I was under the table. I could see it. I could see my mum's face as — as they—"

She stopped again. Breathed. Her hands, resting on her knees, were very still — the stillness of someone who had learned, over many years, to hold their body under absolute control because the alternative was to fall apart.

"A neighbour. A woman named Mara. She came through the back door with a hammer and she got me out. She carried me out of the house while my parents were — while the Hollows were—" Another breath. "She saved my life. She was the only person who did."

The name hit Finch like a punch. Mara. The same Mara. The Mara who had been Caleb's partner. The Mara who had died in the river. The Mara who had saved a version of Finch from a version of the outbreak. The name sat between them like a stone, heavy and immovable, connecting two worlds that should not have been connected.

"Mara," Finch said. "What happened to her?"

"She raised me. Sort of. We were in a group — survivors, holed up in a building in the Blue Mountains. For two years, it was okay. Mara taught me how to survive. How to fight. How to be hard." The other Finch's voice had taken on a different quality — slower, heavier, as though each word cost her something. "She had a son. A boy named Eli. He was — he was the only person who was kind to me after my parents died. He shared his food. He let me use his blanket when I was cold. He was nine, and he was gentle, and he was everything I wasn't."

"What happened to Eli?"

The other Finch's face changed. It was subtle — a tightening around the eyes, a hardening of the jaw — but Finch saw it because it was her own face, and she knew what that expression looked like. She had seen it in the mirror, on mornings when the grief for her father was too large to contain.

"He died," the other Finch said. "He died because of me."

She didn't explain. Finch didn't ask. The silence between them was thick with things that couldn't be said, the way silence gets when two people are standing on opposite sides of the same memory. The bare bulb buzzed. The concrete walls held their cold. And somewhere beyond them, the wind moved through the mountains with a sound like breathing.

After a long time, the other Finch spoke again. Her voice was different now — quieter, rougher, stripped of the performance she usually wore like armour. She spoke the way people speak when they've stopped trying to impress the person they're talking to and have started, against their better judgement, to simply be honest.

"He went to get water. From the collection point outside the building. He didn't tell anyone. He was nine — he thought he could handle it. He thought it was close enough, safe enough, that the rules didn't apply." She paused. "I was supposed to be watching him. That was my job. Mara had assigned it to me — she said it would teach me responsibility, teach me to care about someone other than myself. And I was eight, and I was distracted, and I was sitting on the stairs drawing in the dirt with a stick when he slipped past."

Finch felt something move in her chest — not pity, not exactly, but the recognition of a wound that was shaped like her own. She thought about the times she had failed to pay attention, the times she had been somewhere else when she was supposed to be somewhere here. She thought about the colouring book and the purple horse and the night the policeman came to the door, and how different things might have been if she had been paying attention, if she had been looking up instead of down.

"The group found him at the water point. Two Hollows had gotten him. Maybe three. He was — they didn't leave much. He was nine years old and he was dead because an eight-year-old girl was drawing in the dirt." The other Finch's voice was flat now, stripped of emotion, the way a voice gets when the emotion has been felt so many times that it's worn smooth. "Mara was the one who found the body. She carried him back. She laid him on the floor of the basement and she sat beside him and she didn't move for three hours. I sat in the corner and watched her, and I wanted to say something — I wanted to say I was sorry, I wanted to say it was my fault, I wanted to say anything — but there was nothing to say. Nothing that would bring him back. Nothing that would fix what I had broken."

"And then they threw you out."

"And then they threw me out. Not right away. Not that day. It took them a week. A week of sitting in that basement with Eli's empty mat, with Mara's silence, with the looks — God, the looks. The way they looked at me. Like I was something broken. Something dangerous. Something that needed to be removed before it broke anything else." She smiled, and the smile was terrible — a rictus, a mask, the shape of amusement without any of its substance. "They were right, of course. I was dangerous. I just didn't know it yet."

"Mara tried to stop them?"

"Mara fought them. Verbally. She stood in the doorway with her arms spread and she said 'She's a child, she's a child and she needs us,' and they said 'She'll get more of us killed,' and she said 'She's eight years old, she didn't mean for this to happen,' and they said 'It doesn't matter what she meant, it matters what happened.' And they were right about that too. Intent doesn't matter when a nine-year-old boy is dead on the floor."

The other Finch leaned back in the chair. The metal groaned under her weight. She looked up at the bare bulb, and the light caught her face in a way that made her look younger — not the tyrant, not the warlord, but a girl. A girl with silver eyes and lavender hair and a wound that would never heal.

"Mara begged," she said. "I'd never seen her beg before. She was proud — so proud, the way you have to be when the world has taken everything and you're still standing. But she begged. She begged them to let me stay. She begged them to give me another chance. She begged them to remember that I was a child, that children make mistakes, that what happened to Eli was a tragedy but not a crime."

"And they said no."

"They said no. And Mara — she let me go. Not because she wanted to. Because she couldn't stop them. Because one person against twenty is not a fight, it's a sacrifice, and she had already sacrificed enough." The other Finch's voice cracked, just slightly, on the last word, and Finch saw something move behind her eyes — something quick and bright and immediately suppressed, like a bird startled from a branch. "She walked me to the door. She held my face in her hands — the way she always did when she was saying something important — and she said, 'You are not what happened to you. You are what you choose to become.' And then she let me go."

The words hung in the air. *You are what you choose to become.* Finch turned them over in her mind, testing their weight, their shape, their meaning. She looked at the girl who had heard those words and become this — this tyrant, this warlord, this collector of Hollows and broken children — and she wondered if the words had been a prophecy or a curse.

"She let you go," Finch repeated.

"She let me go. And I walked out into the world alone, and I was angry — so angry, Finch. Angrier than I'd ever been. Angry at Mara for not fighting harder. Angry at the group for being afraid. Angry at Eli for dying. Angry at my parents for being dead. Angry at the world for existing in this broken, cruel, impossible way." She looked at Finch, and her silver eyes were bright. "I was angry for years. I was angry through every cold night in the mountains, through every empty stomach, through every wound I stitched myself. And then one day I decided that anger wasn't enough. I needed power. I needed control. I needed to make sure that no one would ever throw me out again."

"And Avarice was born."

"Avarice was born."

The silence returned. The bulb buzzed. The concrete held its cold. And Finch Adderwood of Earth One sat in a cell in a compound in the snowy mountains and looked at the worst version of herself and felt something she hadn't expected to feel.

Not pity. Not fear. Not horror, though there was horror there, deep and real. What she felt was grief. Grief for the girl who had been broken, for the woman who had broken, for the distance between them that was measured not in choices but in the quality of the hands that had held her when she fell.

"The group blamed me," the other Finch continued, her voice returning to its harder register, the vulnerability retreating behind the mask. "They said I was reckless. Reckless. I was eight years old. I was eight, and a nine-year-old boy was dead, and they threw me out. Mara couldn't stop them. She tried, but they were — they were scared. Of me. Of what I might become."

"What did you become?"

The other Finch smiled. Not the thin, hard smile from before. A different smile — wider, wilder, with something broken in it, the smile of someone who had looked at the worst version of themselves and decided to make it a uniform.

"I became this." She spread her arms. "I lived alone for four years. In the mountains, in the cold, in the empty places where nobody wanted to go. I built my bike from scrap when I was twelve — a motorcycle, fully functional, from parts I scavenged. I trained myself to fight, to hunt, to survive. I ate what I could find. I slept where I could find shelter. I learned to read the weather, to read the ground, to read the silence of a forest that might or might not be empty."

She paused, and her eyes went distant — not looking at Finch, not looking at the cell, but looking at something else entirely, something that existed only in the space behind her eyes.

"There were nights in the mountains when the cold was so bad I thought I would die. Not dramatically — not the way my parents died, not the way Eli died. Just — quietly. Slowly. The kind of cold that seeps into your bones and stays there, and you stop shivering because shivering takes energy and you don't have any left. I would lie in whatever shelter I'd found — a cave, a hollow tree, the wreck of a car — and I would feel my body shutting down, piece by piece, and I would think: *This is it. This is where it ends.* And then morning would come, and the sun would rise, and I would be alive, and I would be angry about it. Angry that I had survived another night. Angry that the world kept insisting on my existence when I had not asked for it and did not want it."

She looked at Finch. "Do you know what it's like to be angry at the sun for rising?"

Finch didn't answer. She didn't need to. The question was rhetorical, and they both knew it.

"But I kept going. Because that's what we do, isn't it? That's what you and I do, underneath everything. We keep going. Not because we want to. Because stopping isn't an option. Because the alternative to continuing is lying down and letting the world take you, and I had already seen what the world did to people who lay down."

She straightened in the chair. The vulnerability receded, replaced by something harder, something sharper.

"I was sixteen when I found others — kids my age, alone, discarded, thrown out by groups who didn't want them or couldn't keep them. I gave them a purpose."

"Avarice."

"Avarice. It started with five kids and a dream of never being afraid again. Now it's — this." She gestured at the walls, the compound, the army beyond. "Forty people. More. We take what we need. We kill who we have to. We don't apologise."

"And you kill people for fun."

"We kill people who deserve it."

"Who decides who deserves it?"

"I do."

The silence that followed was heavy with implications. Finch looked at the girl sitting across from her — this version of herself, this warped mirror — and she saw the loneliness in her. It was there in the set of her jaw, in the way her eyes moved, in the particular tension of her shoulders. She saw it because she recognised it. She had seen it in her mother's face, in the years after her father disappeared. She had seen it in the faces of the children at the Junkyard. She had seen it in her own reflection, in the quiet hours before dawn, when the world was too large and too empty and too wrong.

"You're going to lock me up forever?" Finch asked.

"No. I'm going to do something much more interesting." The other Finch stood up. She walked to the door, opened it, and turned back. Her face was half-lit by the corridor light, half-shadowed, and in that divide she looked like two people at once — the tyrant and the child, the warrior and the girl who had hidden under a table. "I'm going to keep you alive. For now. Because you're me, and killing you would be like killing a part of myself, and I've already done enough of that."

She paused in the doorway. For a moment — just a moment — her face softened. It was barely perceptible, a shift of perhaps a millimetre in the muscles around her eyes, a slight relaxation of the jaw. But Finch saw it, because it was her own face, and she knew what that expression looked like. It was the expression of someone who had just said something true and was immediately sorry for it.

"Don't make me regret this," the other Finch said. And then she was gone, and the door was closed, and the lock had turned, and Finch was alone with the buzzing light and the cold concrete and the echoing weight of a story that was hers and not hers, a mirror held at an angle she had never expected to see.

She left. The door closed. The lock turned.

And Finch sat in the buzzing light and thought about the girl who had become this — this tyrant, this warlord, this broken thing with lavender hair and a leather jacket — and she thought about the differences between them. The choices. The circumstances. The way a life could bend under pressure and never straighten out again. She thought about Mara's words — *You are what you choose to become* — and she thought about how the tyrant had chosen, and how the choosing had been shaped by the things that came before it, by the hands that had held her and the hands that had let her go.

She thought about Mara. The real Mara, the Earth Two Mara, who had saved a little girl and raised her and then lost her son and watched the girl she'd saved become something terrible. She thought about what that must have been like — to love someone and watch them become unrecognisable, to reach for them and find only rage and iron where tenderness used to be. She thought about the Mara who had died in the river, the Mara who had held her arm and said *go*, the Mara who had smiled — that small, private smile — before the current took her.

And she thought about the cairn of river stones, and the water, and the dark, and she wondered if the Mara who had died in the river had somehow, in some way, saved them both. If love, even love that came too late, even love that arrived in the wrong shape and the wrong world, was still love. If it still counted.

The bulb buzzed. The walls were cold. And Finch Adderwood of Earth One lay down on the mat and closed her eyes and did not sleep, because sleep was a country she could not reach tonight, not after what she had heard, not after what she had seen in the face of the girl who was her and was not her and might, under different skies, have been someone else entirely.

---

# Chapter Thirteen: The Pets

The weeks in the cell blurred together.

Time moved differently when you had nothing to measure it by — no sunrise, no sunset, no change in the quality of light. The bare bulb on the ceiling was always on, always buzzing, always casting its flat, shadowless illumination across the concrete walls. Finch's body adapted to the monotony in ways she didn't fully understand. She slept when exhaustion overtook her, which was often — the cold and the hard floor and the relentless light conspired to drain her of energy, and she would drift into a thin, restless sleep that was more like a series of brief unconsciousnesses than actual rest. She woke with the same confusion each time, not knowing if an hour had passed or six, not knowing if it was day or night outside the windowless walls.

She exercised. It was the only thing she could do. She did push-ups on the cold concrete until her arms gave out, then lay on her back and stared at the buzzing light and waited for her heart rate to slow. She did sit-ups, crunches, leg raises — the basic movements that Mara had taught her at the Junkyard, repurposed now as a way to keep her body from going soft. She shadowboxed, throwing punches at the air, working through the combinations Mara had drilled into her: jab, cross, hook, pivot. The movements felt absurd in the confined space, a kind of desperate pantomime, but they kept her sane. They kept her connected to the person she had been before the cell.

She thought. Too much. The thinking was the worst part — worse than the cold, worse than the light, worse than the food. Her mind turned over the same subjects like a dog worrying a bone: her father, Mara, the Junkyard, Earth One, her mother alone in a house in Brookvale. She thought about the tyrant — about the girl she had met, the girl with her face and her eyes and a completely different soul. She thought about the story the tyrant had told, and she turned it over and examined it from every angle, looking for the seams, the places where the narrative deviated from what she understood about the world and about herself.

The other Finch — the tyrant — visited periodically. Not to talk, exactly, but to study her. She would appear at the door without warning, sometimes in the morning, sometimes in the dead hours, and she would sit in the chair and watch Finch with those silver eyes, and Finch would watch her back, and sometimes they would talk and sometimes they wouldn't. The visits were irregular, which was another kind of control — Finch could never predict when they would come, could never prepare herself, could never arrange her face into an expression that wasn't raw.

She told Finch things. About her life. About the years alone in the mountains. About building the bike — a custom-built machine, welded together from the frames of three different motorcycles, painted black, with reinforced handlebars and a steel bumper on the front. She described the process with a strange, almost tender precision — the way she had stripped each frame down to its essential components, the way she had taught herself to weld by watching rusted tutorials on a tablet that had held its last charge for three years, the way the first time the engine had turned over she had sat in the snow and cried. Not from joy. From the shock of having made something that worked, after years of nothing working.

She told Finch about the day she met the first of her followers — a boy named Dex, fifteen, abandoned by his group, hungry and angry and ready to follow anyone who offered him a purpose. She had found him in a valley, sitting by a fire that was more smoke than flame, his clothes hanging off his bones, his eyes the flat, distant eyes of someone who had decided to stop caring whether he lived or died. She had given him food. She had given him a blanket. She had given him something to believe in, which was the rarest thing in this world — rarer than food, rarer than water, rarer than ammunition.

She told Finch about training them. About turning frightened, broken children into warriors. About the discipline, the drills, the combat training. About the first time they raided another group — how the fear on the faces of the people they attacked had felt like the best thing she'd ever experienced. Like power. Like safety. Like the opposite of being six years old and hiding under a table while your parents were torn apart.

"You understand," the other Finch said one evening, sitting in the chair with her legs crossed, her knife turning in her hands. The blade caught the light from the bare bulb and threw it back in bright, moving fragments that danced across the walls. "You understand why I did it."

"I understand you were hurt."

"Everyone was hurt. That's not an excuse. That's a fact. The difference between me and everyone else is that I decided to be the one doing the hurting instead of the one getting hurt."

"And Mara?"

The other Finch's face tightened. The knife stopped turning. "Mara was — complicated. She tried to find me. After the group threw me out, she tried. She searched for two years. But I didn't want to be found. I was angry. At her, at the group, at the world. I was angry because she had saved me and then she hadn't been able to save me from — from becoming this."

"Did you ever see her again?"

"No. Not before she—" The other Finch stopped. Something crossed her face that Finch couldn't read — a shadow, a flicker, something that lived in the space between memory and regret. "Not before she disappeared. I heard she was with a group near Sydney. The Junkyard. But I never went there."

"You never went there because—"

"Because I didn't want to see the woman who saved me look at me and see a monster."

The word hung in the air between them. Monster. It sat there, heavy and awful, and Finch looked at the girl who had called herself one and saw, beneath the leather and the blade and the hardened exterior, the small, terrified shape of a child who had never stopped running from the thing she'd become.

On the fourteenth day, the other Finch came to the cell with a different energy. She was moving quickly, her eyes bright, her knife already in her hand. There was something electric about her — a charge, a current, the way the air feels before a storm breaks. She stood in the doorway and looked at Finch with an expression that was half amusement and half something harder.

"We're going for a ride," she said.

"What?"

"You and me. We're going for a ride. I want to show you something."

She pulled Finch to her feet and led her out of the cell, through the compound, past guards who looked away as they passed, past barracks where people slept or sharpened blades or stared at nothing. The compound was different in the cold — the air sharp with frost, the ground hard, the breath of the people they passed rising in white plumes. Finch's feet were bare on the frozen concrete and the cold bit into her soles with every step.

They reached where the bike was parked. It was exactly as she'd described it — a Frankenstein machine, black and angular, with the Avarice symbol painted on the fuel tank in white. The engine was exposed, the wiring visible, the whole thing held together by welds and stubbornness. On the front bumper, secured with heavy straps, were two figures.

Hollows.

Two of them. Strapped to the front of the bike, facing forward, their grey skin stretched tight over their bones. They were alive — or whatever the Hollow equivalent of alive was. Their milky eyes stared ahead. Their mouths opened and closed in slow, rhythmic motions, like fish gasping at the surface of a pond. Straps held them in place at the neck, the wrists, the waist. Their arms hung free, the fingers twitching, the nails — yellowed and cracked — catching the light.

Finch felt her stomach turn. The smell hit her next — a thick, organic stench, sweet and rotten, the smell of tissue that was decaying even as it persisted. It clung to the bike, to the straps, to the air around the Hollows themselves. She could taste it at the back of her throat.

"What the fuck."

"My pets," the other Finch said, with the casual pride of someone showing off a collection. "They don't eat — not much, anyway. They just — exist. Growl. Look scary. Great for morale."

"That's insane."

"That's effective. There's a difference." The other Finch threw a leg over the bike and started the engine. It roared to life with a sound like a chainsaw — a raw, mechanical scream that echoed off the compound walls and sent a flock of birds scattering from a nearby rooftop. "Get on."

"And if I don't?"

The other Finch looked at her. The silver eyes were hard and bright and completely without mercy.

"Then I feed you to them instead."

Finch got on. She sat behind the other Finch, her hands gripping the seat rail, her body pressed against the cold leather of the tyrant's jacket. The Hollows were directly in front of her, their groans competing with the engine's roar, their dead eyes reflecting the grey sky.

The bike screamed out of the compound and into the mountains. The other Finch drove like she lived — fast, reckless, without regard for consequence. The road was a narrow track through snow and rock, and the bike hit every bump and rut at speed, jolting Finch's spine and rattling her teeth. The wind was a physical force, pressing against her face, pulling at her hair, stealing the warmth from her skin. The cold was absolute — not the cell's cold, which was stagnant and dead, but a living cold, a moving cold, a cold that found every gap in her clothing and settled into her bones.

The Hollows on the front bumper groaned into the wind, their hair whipping, their dead eyes streaming with moisture that might have been tears or might have been something else. One of them turned its head — slowly, mechanically, the way a clock turns — and its milky eyes found Finch's face, and it groaned louder, and its mouth opened wider, and she could see the grey tongue and the yellowed teeth and the dark hollow of a throat that had forgotten what it meant to swallow.

They rode for an hour, through snow and rock and forest, the landscape blurring past in a smear of white and grey and dark green. The mountains rose around them, massive and indifferent, their peaks lost in cloud. The air was thin at this altitude, and Finch's lungs worked harder to draw breath, each inhale a small, painful victory.

The other Finch pulled up at a clearing. She killed the engine and the silence that followed was enormous — a ringing, aching silence that was almost louder than the roar it replaced. She got off and walked to the edge of the cliff and looked out over the valley below.

"This is where I found Dex," she said. "Right down there. In that valley. He was sitting by a fire, alone, and he was so thin I could count his ribs through his shirt. I gave him food. I gave him a purpose. I gave him something to believe in."

"You gave him violence."

"I gave him survival. Violence is just survival with ambition."

Finch stood beside her. The valley below was vast and empty and beautiful. Snow covered everything, and the light was the colour of iron — flat, grey, without warmth. The trees were dark shapes against the white, their branches bare, their trunks black. The river that had carved the valley was frozen, a white scar running through the landscape like a vein of ore in rock. The wind moved through the trees with a sound like breathing, and Finch thought about the sound the Hollows made, and the sound the wind made, and how the two were almost the same.

She thought about the tyrant's words — *violence is just survival with ambition* — and she turned them over, testing their truth. She thought about Mara, who had survived without violence, who had chosen kindness when cruelty was easier. She thought about the Junkyard, where people built walls instead of weapons, where children played in the firelight, where food was shared and stories were told. She thought about the difference between surviving and living, and whether the tyrant knew the difference, and whether it mattered.

"You could come with me," Finch said. The words surprised her. She hadn't planned to say them — they came from somewhere deeper than intention, from the place where instinct lives, the place that sees a wounded thing and wants to help it. "To Earth One. If there's a way there. You could start over."

The other Finch looked at her. For a moment — just a moment — something moved in her expression. Not hope. Something adjacent to hope. The way a gate is adjacent to a door.

"There is no starting over," she said. "There's only going forward. And forward, for me, is this." She spread her arms, encompassing the valley, the mountains, the compound, the army, all of it. "This is what I built. It's ugly and it's cruel and it's mine. And I'm not leaving it for a world that doesn't exist."

"You don't know that it doesn't—"

"I know enough. I know what I am. I know what I've done. And I know that there's no world — no Earth One, no Earth Two, no parallel universe anywhere — where what I've done doesn't matter." She looked at Finch, and her silver eyes were very bright. "You're the good version. The one who got to keep her parents, her home, her innocence. I'm the other one. The one who got the table and the hammer and the dead boy and the four years alone. And I've made my peace with that. Mostly."

The word *mostly* hung in the air like a held breath. Finch heard it, and she understood it, and she felt something shift in her chest — the same tectonic movement she had felt when Mara had said *I knew a Caleb once*, the deep, slow recognition of something that mattered but could not be fixed.

"You're going to kill me, aren't you?" Finch said.

"Not yet. Maybe not ever. I haven't decided." The other Finch turned to face her. Her face was very close — close enough for Finch to see the faint scar above her left eyebrow, the small nick on her lower lip, the way her eyelashes caught the light. "But I'll tell you this — you're not leaving. Not alive. Because if you leave, and you tell people what you've seen here, and where we are, and how many we have — that's a threat. And I don't tolerate threats."

"I'm not going to tell anyone. I just want to go home."

"There is no home. There's this. There's now. There's what we make of it."

"There's Earth One. My world. Where my mother is."

The other Finch went quiet. Something shifted in her expression — something beneath the hardness, beneath the cruelty, something small and young and buried very deep. It was there for a moment, visible the way a fish is visible beneath the surface of dark water, and then it was gone, replaced by the mask.

"You have a mother," she said.

"Don't you?"

"I had a mother. She's dead. She died when I was six, in a kitchen, while I watched from under a table." The other Finch's voice was flat. Controlled. "So no. I don't have a mother. I have this. I have forty people who follow me because they're scared and I'm not. I have a bike and two Hollows and a compound in the mountains and a reputation that makes grown men piss themselves."

"That sounds lonely."

"Lonely is safe. Lonely is alive. Lonely is all I've got."

They rode back to the compound in silence. The Hollows on the front bumper groaned into the wind. The snow fell. The mountains closed around them like a fist.

That night, in her cell, Finch lay on the mat and stared at the buzzing light and thought about the two of them — the girl she was and the girl she might have been. The choices that separated them. The pain. The loneliness. The terrible, corrosive armour that trauma builds around a heart.

She thought: *There but for the grace of God go I.*

She didn't sleep. The light buzzed. The walls were cold. And somewhere in the compound, in a room that Finch had never seen, the other Finch sat on a bed and sharpened her blade and stared at nothing and felt the loneliness like a physical thing, a weight on her chest, a hand at her throat, and she breathed through it the way she always did — one breath at a time, one moment at a time, one day at a time, until the days became years and the years became this.

This.

---

# Chapter Fourteen: The Other Girl

The flashback began, as flashbacks do, without warning.

She was six years old, and the world was ending.

The kitchen smelled like pancakes. Not just any pancakes — her mother's pancakes, the ones with the slightly crispy edges and the soft, fluffy centres that she made every Sunday morning without fail, standing at the stove in her blue dressing gown with her hair still damp from the shower. The batter was in a bowl on the counter, yellow and thick, with the faint smell of vanilla that her mother always added — just a splash, she said, it makes all the difference. The spatula had a cartoon character on the handle — a frog, she thought, or maybe a dinosaur; she had never been able to decide — and her mother used it with the easy, practised wrist-flick of someone who had made ten thousand pancakes and would make ten thousand more.

Her father was at the table, reading the newspaper on his phone. He did this every morning — sat with his coffee, his thumb scrolling, his face arranged in the particular expression of mild disapproval that meant he was reading about politics. He wore a grey jumper that had a hole in the elbow, and his hair was uncombed, and he looked, in that moment, exactly like the kind of father she would remember for the rest of her life: comfortable, present, completely unaware that the world was about to end.

The morning light was coming through the window. It was a good window — large, clean, the kind of window that let in the whole morning and made the kitchen feel like the centre of the universe. Through it, she could see the garden — the lemon tree, the clothesline, the fence with the missing slat that her father kept meaning to fix. She could see the street beyond, quiet and suburban, the kind of street where nothing ever happened. The light fell across the table in a bright rectangle, warming the newspaper, warming her father's hands, warming the world.

Everything was warm and safe and completely, irrevocably normal.

Then the window broke.

Not shattered — exploded. One moment it was glass, the next it was shrapnel, and through the gap came a shape, and then another, and another. Grey. Groaning. Moving with the terrible, relentless patience of things that had once been people and were now only hunger. The first one hit the counter with enough force to send the bowl of batter sliding to the floor. The second one knocked the stove — and the pan, and the pancakes, and the frog-handled spatula spinning across the tiles. The third came through the side window, the one above the sink, the one her mother had decorated with little glass birds that caught the light. The birds shattered. The glass fell like rain.

Her father screamed. Her mother screamed. The thing — the Hollow — landed on the kitchen counter and knocked the pan to the floor, the pancakes forgotten, the frog-handled spatula spinning across the tiles. Her father grabbed a chair. He held it like a weapon, like a shield, and he put himself between the Hollows and the back door where her mother was grabbing her hand, pulling her, running, but there were more of them, more and more, coming through every window, every door, the sounds of breaking glass and tearing wood and the groans, the endless groans.

The smell hit her — a thick, organic stench, sweet and rotten, the smell of something that was decaying even as it moved. It coated the back of her throat and stayed there, and she would smell it for years, in her dreams, in the quiet moments, in the spaces between one heartbeat and the next. She smelled it and she understood, with the terrible clarity that children sometimes have, that the people coming through the windows were not people any more.

Under the table. She was under the table. She didn't remember getting there. One moment she was standing, the next she was on the floor, curled into a ball beneath the tablecloth that her mother had sewn herself — blue with white flowers, the hem slightly uneven because her mother had never been good at straight lines — and through the gap between the tablecloth and the floor she could see everything.

Her father's legs. Her mother's legs. The grey shapes of the Hollows. The red that spread across the tiles like paint thrown from a bucket. The frog-handled spatula, lying on its side, the cartoon face staring at nothing. A pancake, half-cooked, on the floor. The way the light through the broken window caught the blood and turned it bright, impossibly bright, the way a child's painting is bright — all red, no shading, no restraint.

The sounds.

She would never forget the sounds. They were worse than the sight. Worse than the blood. The sounds of her parents being — being consumed. Torn apart. Devoured. The wet, tearing sounds, the crunch of bone, the animal grunts of the Hollows as they fed. Her father's voice, which had been screaming, which had gone quiet. Her mother's voice, which had been calling her name — *Finch, Finch, come to me, come to me* — which had also gone quiet. The sounds went on for a very long time, and she lay under the table and listened, and something inside her — something essential, something childlike and innocent and good — cracked and broke and fell away.

The door opened. A woman. Dark hair. Strong arms. A hammer in one hand, raised high. Her face was red — from exertion, from fear, from the effort of forcing herself through a house that was full of things that wanted to kill her. Her eyes were wide but focused, the eyes of someone who had made a decision and was not going to stop until it was done.

"Come with me," the woman said. "Come with me NOW."

Mara. The neighbour. The woman from two houses down who had a son named Eli and who brought them casseroles at Christmas and who always smiled, even when she was tired, especially when she was tired. She smelled like the lavender soap she used, and in the midst of everything — the blood, the groans, the shattered glass — that smell was the most real thing in the world. It was the smell of a person who was alive and trying to help, and the girl clung to it the way you cling to a rope when the water is rising.

Mara pulled her from under the table. The girl — six years old, trembling, silent, her eyes wide and empty — went with her. Mara's hand was around her wrist, tight enough to leave a mark, and the girl could feel the pulse in Mara's palm — fast, strong, alive. They ran through the back door, across the garden, over the fence. The girl's feet were bare — she had been in pyjamas, in the kitchen, on a Sunday morning — and the ground was cold and the grass was wet and the fence scraped her knee when she climbed it, but she didn't feel any of it. She felt nothing. She was empty. She was a shell that Mara was carrying through the world, and the shell went where it was told because it had forgotten how to do anything else.

Behind them, the groans continued. Ahead of them, the street was empty and the sky was grey and the world was very, very quiet.

They found a group. Survivors. Huddled in the basement of an office building, rationing food and water and hope. The basement smelled of damp concrete and unwashed bodies and the particular, acrid scent of fear that accumulates in enclosed spaces where people have been hiding for a long time. There were perhaps thirty of them — men, women, children — packed into a space designed for filing cabinets and photocopiers. They sat on the floor, on chairs dragged down from the offices above, on flattened cardboard boxes. They looked up when Mara brought the girl in, and their faces held the expressions Finch would come to recognise as the universal face of the survivor: exhaustion, suspicion, the desperate hope that this new person might bring something — food, information, a reason to keep going.

Mara brought the girl and her own son, Eli, and for two years they lived in that basement, and the girl learned the rules of the new world.

Rule one: Don't make noise.

Rule two: Don't trust anyone outside the group.

Rule three: Always carry a weapon.

Rule four: Never cry. Crying makes noise. Noise attracts Hollows. Hollows kill.

The girl learned. She learned quickly, the way children learn when survival is the teacher. She learned to fight — first with improvised weapons, then with real ones. She learned to move silently, to hide, to strike. She learned to read people's faces and know when they were lying, when they were scared, when they were planning something. She learned that the basement had a rhythm — the hours of silence during the day, when the Hollows were sluggish and the group conserved energy; the hours of activity at night, when scavenging parties went out and came back with whatever they could find. She learned that food was never enough, that water was always a concern, that warmth was a luxury. She learned that the other children in the group avoided her — not because she was cruel, but because she was quiet in a way that made them uncomfortable, the way a still animal in a field makes other animals nervous.

She learned Eli.

Eli was nine, and he was everything she was not. He was loud where she was silent. He was clumsy where she was careful. He was open where she was closed. He had his mother's dark hair and his father's wide smile, and he smiled often, even in the basement, even when food was scarce, even when the sounds of Hollows drifted down through the ventilation shafts like a lullaby from hell. He smiled because he was nine and nine-year-olds find ways to be happy even when happiness has no logical basis.

He shared his food with her. Every meal, without fail, he broke his portion in half and gave her the larger piece, and when she refused he said "I'm not hungry" in a voice that was clearly lying, and she took the food because she was hungry and because the gesture was the only kindness she had known since the kitchen. He let her use his blanket when she was cold — his one blanket, the thin, frayed thing that was all he had, and he gave it to her without being asked, without being told, without any expectation of return. He talked to her when no one else would — long, rambling monologues about nothing, about everything, about the video games he used to play and the dog he used to have and the treehouse his father had built in the backyard before the backyard became a memory.

She didn't know how to receive kindness. She had forgotten. The kitchen had taken it from her, had torn it out along with everything else, and what was left was a space shaped like a person but hollow in the places where warmth was supposed to be. Eli filled that space. Not completely — not in the way her parents had filled it, not in the way that was natural and deep and permanent. But he filled it the way water fills a crack — seeping in, finding the edges, making contact. She let him. She didn't know how to stop him. And in the silence of the basement, in the dark hours between one day and the next, she began to feel something she hadn't felt since the kitchen.

She began to feel safe.

She was eight when it happened. The accident.

Eli was nine. He was — he was a good kid. Quiet. Careful. He followed the rules. But he was nine, and nine-year-olds make mistakes, and the mistake he made was simple: he went to fetch water from the collection point outside the basement, and he didn't tell anyone, and the girl — who had been assigned to watch him — was distracted.

She was sitting on the stairs. The concrete was cold through her trousers, and she had found a stick — a pencil, actually, dropped by one of the adults — and she was drawing in the dirt that had accumulated in the corner of the stairwell. She was drawing the kitchen. She didn't know why. She hadn't drawn it before — hadn't been able to, hadn't wanted to. But that afternoon, with the pencil in her hand and the dirt beneath it, the kitchen emerged from the blank surface with a clarity that frightened her. The table. The window. The pan on the stove. The frog-handled spatula. Her mother's blue dressing gown. Her father's grey jumper with the hole in the elbow.

She was drawing, and she was lost in the drawing, and Eli slipped past her on the stairs and went outside, and she didn't see him, and she didn't hear him, and the pencil kept moving, and the kitchen took shape on the concrete, and the world kept turning, and Eli went to the water collection point and didn't come back.

They found his body at the water collection point. The Hollows had gotten him. Not many — maybe two, maybe three. But enough. Enough to end a nine-year-old boy who had made one mistake on one afternoon because an eight-year-old girl hadn't been paying attention. They carried him back in someone's arms — she didn't see whose — and they laid him on the floor of the basement, and his mother screamed. It was the only time in two years that Mara had screamed. The sound went through the girl like a blade, sharp and deep, and it lodged somewhere inside her and never came out.

The group turned on her. Not physically — they didn't hit her, didn't touch her. But the looks on their faces were worse. The accusation. The blame. The unspoken certainty that this girl, this strange, silent girl who had watched her parents die and never cried, who carried knives before she could properly hold a pen, who had something wrong with her, something broken — this girl was a danger. A liability. A threat.

They threw her out.

Mara tried to stop them. She stood in the doorway of the basement with her arms spread wide and her face red and her voice shaking, and she said "She's a child. She's a child and she needs us," and they said "She's a danger and she'll get more of us killed," and Mara lost. Because Mara was one person, and fear was forty.

The girl left. She walked out into the world alone, eight years old, carrying nothing but a knife and a silence that would never fully leave her. She walked through the door and into the street and the sky was grey and the air was cold and the world was empty, and she kept walking because there was nowhere to stop, nowhere to go, nowhere that would have her. She walked until the buildings thinned and the roads gave way to bush and the bush gave way to mountains, and then she kept walking, deeper, higher, into the places where no one went because no one wanted to.

Four years alone. Four years in the mountains, in the empty places, in the snow. She learned to hunt — rabbits, at first, then larger things, using snares she made from wire she stripped from abandoned fences. She learned to build — shelters from fallen branches and corrugated iron, lean-tos and windbreaks and, eventually, something that almost resembled a home. She learned to heal her own wounds — stitching cuts with fishing line and a bent needle, setting broken fingers with sticks and cloth, burning infections out with heated steel. She learned to read the weather by the colour of the sky and the direction of the wind and the behaviour of the birds, and she learned to read the ground by the tracks and the scat and the particular way grass bent when something heavy had passed over it.

She learned to be alone. That was the hardest lesson. Not the hunting, not the building, not the healing — the aloneness. The silence that had no end. The darkness that had no one to break it. The cold that had no warmth to counter it. She lay in her shelters at night and listened to the wind and the rain and the distant, mechanical groan of Hollows somewhere in the valley below, and she felt the weight of her own existence like a stone on her chest. She was eight, then nine, then ten, then eleven, and each year added another layer to the silence, another brick to the wall she was building around herself, another reason to stop hoping that someone would come.

No one came.

At twelve, she built the bike.

It took her three months. She found the first frame in a wrecking yard at the base of the mountains — a rusted skeleton of a motorcycle, half-buried in weeds, its engine seized, its wheels flat. She dragged it up the mountain on a sled she'd made from a piece of plywood and rope. She found the second frame in an abandoned workshop — less rusted, more complete, with an engine that might, with enough work, turn over again. She found the third in the basement of a farmhouse, still on its stand, covered in a tarp that had kept the worst of the weather off.

She taught herself to weld. There was a tablet in the farmhouse — dead, at first, but she found a solar charger in a shed, and after three days of sunlight the tablet came to life, its screen cracked but functional. She watched tutorials. She practiced on scrap metal, burning her fingers, melting through steel, filling the air with the acrid smoke of flux and the sharp, bright smell of molten metal. She blew up a battery. She set fire to a workbench. She singed her eyebrows off and didn't grow them back for six months.

But she learned. She stripped each frame down to its essential components — the engine, the transmission, the frame itself. She welded the best parts together, creating a hybrid machine that was ugly and angular and held together by welds and stubbornness. She rebuilt the engine, replacing pistons and gaskets with parts she scavenged from a dozen different machines. She taught herself to ride — crash, repair, crash again, repair again — until the bike became an extension of her body, a second skeleton, a way of moving through the world that was faster and louder and more visible than anything she had done before.

The first time the engine turned over — really turned over, catching and holding and settling into a rough, steady idle — she sat in the snow beside the bike and cried. Not from joy. From the shock of having made something that worked, after years of nothing working. From the realisation that she could build, that she could create, that the hands that had held a knife under a table could hold a wrench and make something live. She sat there and the engine idled and the snow fell around her and she felt, for the first time since the kitchen, that she was not just surviving.

She was building.

At fourteen, she found the Hollows. The two that were now strapped to her bumper. She had been — morbidly curious, she supposed. She had seen Hollows all her life, seen them killed, seen them shamble, and one day she had the thought: *What if I kept one? What if I kept one like a pet, like a guard dog, like a trophy?*

It was insane. She knew it was insane. But insane and alive was better than sane and dead, and so she captured two of them — sedated them with a drug she'd mixed herself from plants she'd found in the mountains, a paralytic that kept them still but conscious — and strapped them to her bike and kept them. She studied them. She learned their patterns, their responses, their limits. She learned that they could be controlled — not with reason, not with kindness, but with fear and proximity and the particular, terrible authority of someone who was not afraid of them.

People feared her. That was the point. The Hollows on the bike, the knife at her belt, the leather jacket with the symbol she'd burned into it herself — the circle-and-A, for Avarice, for the greed that the world had shown her and that she now showed the world. People saw her coming and they ran, and when they ran, they survived, and when they survived, they told others, and the story grew. The lavender-haired girl with the Hollows on her bike. The girl who didn't die. The girl who lived in the mountains and took what she wanted and answered to no one.

By sixteen, she had followers. Dex first, then others.

She found Dex in a valley south of the mountains, sitting by a fire that was more smoke than flame. He was fifteen, alone, abandoned by his group — thrown out the way she had been thrown out, for reasons that were different in detail but identical in shape. His clothes hung off his bones. His eyes were the flat, distant eyes of someone who had decided to stop caring whether he lived or died. She sat beside him without speaking and gave him food — dried meat, crackers, a tin of peaches she'd been saving — and he ate without speaking, and when he was done he looked at her with those flat eyes and said "Who are you?"

"Nobody," she said. "Like you."

She gave him a purpose. She gave him the knife and the jacket and the symbol and the rage, and she gave him something to believe in, which was the rarest thing in this world — rarer than food, rarer than water, rarer than ammunition. She gave him the idea that the world owed him something, and that taking it back was not just acceptable but necessary. She gave him permission to be angry.

He was the first. There would be others. Broken children, discarded children, children who had been thrown out of their groups the way she had been thrown out. She found them in valleys and basements and the shells of abandoned buildings, and she gathered them the way a fire gathers kindling — slowly, patiently, until the pile was large enough to burn.

She trained them. She turned frightened, broken children into warriors. She taught them to fight, to move, to think. She gave them discipline, structure, the particular clarity that comes from having a purpose and a leader and a cause. She was not kind — kindness was a luxury she could not afford and did not value — but she was effective. She made them strong. She made them feared. She made them hers.

The first raid came at sixteen, three months after she found Dex. They hit a small group near the coast — a dozen people, poorly armed, poorly organised. The fight lasted ten minutes. It was not a battle. It was a demonstration. She stood at the edge of it and watched her people work, watched them move with the precision she had drilled into them, watched them take what they wanted and leave nothing behind. The fear on the faces of the people they attacked was — she couldn't describe it. It was the best thing she had ever felt. Like power. Like safety. Like the opposite of being six years old and hiding under a table while your parents were torn apart.

She took their food, their weapons, their supplies. She took their fear. And she left them alive, because dead people don't spread stories, and stories were the point. Stories were the weapon. Stories were the wall between her and the world that had tried to destroy her.

By eighteen, Avarice was forty strong and the most feared group in New South Wales. They moved like a storm, striking without warning, taking what they needed, leaving nothing behind. The circle-and-A became a symbol of terror, and the lavender-haired girl became a legend, and somewhere deep inside her, the six-year-old who had hidden under a table and watched her parents die was still there, still hiding, still waiting for someone to open the door and pull her out.

No one came. No one pulled her out. The door stayed closed, and the girl became the tyrant, and the tyrant became the monster, and the monster sat on a motorcycle with two dead things strapped to the front and laughed at the fear in people's eyes because the alternative to laughing was screaming, and she had done enough screaming to last a lifetime.

That was the story. That was the other Finch's story. And Finch, the Earth One Finch, the girl with lavender hair and silver eyes who had woken up on a highway three weeks ago, lay in her cell and listened to the story and felt something inside her that she had never felt before.

Not pity. Not fear. Something closer to recognition. The recognition of looking into a mirror that showed not your face but your possible face, the face you would have worn if the world had been slightly different, if the choices had been slightly different, if the pain had been slightly different.

The recognition of looking at the worst version of yourself and knowing, with absolute certainty, that you were not that person — but also knowing, with equal certainty, that the distance between you was smaller than you wanted to believe.

She thought about the kitchen. The pancakes. The frog-handled spatula. The light through the window. She thought about how close she had been to being the other Finch — how a different set of hands, a different set of circumstances, a different quality of luck could have put her under that table and kept her there, could have broken her in the same places and left the same scars. She thought about Mara — both Maras, the one who had saved her and the one who had failed to save the other version — and she thought about the word *go* and the word *monster* and the space between them.

The light buzzed. The walls were cold. And Finch Adderwood of Earth One closed her eyes and slept, and in her sleep she dreamed of a kitchen that smelled like pancakes and a window that broke and a table with a tablecloth and a woman with a hammer and a boy who went to fetch water and didn't come back.

She dreamed of all the ways a person could be broken.

She dreamed of all the ways a person could survive.

---

# Chapter Fifteen: The Break

Finch woke to the sound of her father's voice.

Not shouting this time. Something worse — a voice that was very quiet and very calm, the way Caleb's voice got when he was past anger and into something colder and more dangerous. She had heard that voice only once before, in the Junkyard, when a trade had gone wrong and the other party had tried to cheat them, and Caleb had spoken in that same flat, measured tone that made the air feel thinner.

"Caleb," Finch said, pressing her face to the door. The wood was cold against her cheek, and through it she could feel the vibration of his presence — the small, precise sounds of someone working with their hands. "Caleb, what's happening?"

"Get away from the door."

"Dad—"

"Get away from the door, Finch. Now."

She stepped back. She heard movement on the other side — quiet, precise, the sound of someone working on a lock with tools they shouldn't have had. Metal on metal. A scrape, a click, the particular tension of a pin being lifted. It was the sound of patience, of planning, of three days compressed into a single, focused act. A minute later, the door opened, and Caleb stood in the frame with a thin metal rod in his hand and blood on his knuckles.

His face was different. Not angry — worse than angry. Calm. The calm of a man who had made a decision and was no longer interested in alternatives. His eyes were the colour of steel, and they moved across the cell with the quick, assessing sweep of someone who was already thinking three steps ahead. He was bruised — a deep purple bloom across his left cheekbone, a cut above his eyebrow that had bled and dried and bled again — but he moved without stiffness, the adrenaline burning through the pain like fire through paper.

"Where did you get that?" Finch whispered.

"The workshop. I've been planning this for three days." He looked at her — really looked at her, the way he used to look at her across the kitchen table in Brookvale, with that particular attentiveness that was his way of saying *I see you, I'm here, we're in this together*. "Can you run?"

"I can try."

"Don't try. Do. When we get outside, we go left. There's a vehicle parked near the workshop — a truck, keys in it. Greg always left the keys in. Habit."

"Dad, how did you get out of your cell?"

Caleb held up the metal rod. "I told you. Three days." He turned the rod in his fingers, and Finch saw that it was a piece of wire, bent at one end into a shape that was almost, but not quite, a lock pick. "I watched the guards. I listened to the lock mechanism. I practised on the inside of the door frame until I could do it blind."

His hands were shaking. Not much — a fine tremor, barely visible in the dim light — but enough for Finch to see. He noticed her looking and clenched his fists, and the shaking stopped.

They moved through the compound like shadows. It was night — the middle of the night, the dead hours when even the most dedicated sentries got drowsy. The snow muffled their footsteps. The air was so cold it burned their lungs with every breath, and the steam of their exhalations rose like ghosts beside them.

The compound was quiet. The watchtowers were staffed, but the sentries were looking outward, toward the perimeter, toward the threats that existed beyond the walls. They were not looking inward. They were not expecting a prisoner and a man they'd locked in a cell to be moving through the alleys between the barracks, staying close to the walls, pausing at every corner, listening for footsteps that weren't their own.

Caleb led. He moved with the same practised efficiency he'd shown on supply runs — one hand on the wall, the other on his knife, his weight on the balls of his feet. He stopped at intersections, checked both directions, signalled Finch forward with a gesture. It was like watching a man navigate a room in the dark — except the room was a compound full of armed people who would kill them both without hesitation.

The weapons room was near the workshop — a fortified structure with a heavy door that Caleb had watched being opened and closed enough times to know the mechanism. He worked the lock in under a minute, and they slipped inside.

The room smelled of oil and metal and the particular chemical scent of cleaning solvent. Shelves lined the walls, loaded with weapons and supplies — crossbows, machetes, boxes of bolts, coils of wire, first aid kits, ammunition. It was well-organised, catalogued with military precision, everything in its place and a place for everything. Finch's eyes moved across the shelves and found what she was looking for.

Her backpack was there. Her knife. Her jacket. Her boots. All of it, lined up on a shelf like exhibits in a museum of a person's life. She grabbed them and dressed quickly, the familiar weight of the knife at her belt like a friend's hand on her shoulder. The boots fit. The jacket was cold from the shelf but warm from her body within seconds. The knife was exactly where she'd left it, and the sight of it — the worn handle, the scuffed blade — made something in her chest crack open.

Caleb found his own gear — his machete, his crossbow, his jacket. He moved with the practised efficiency of a man who had been waiting for this moment. His hands were steady now. The trembling had stopped. He loaded the crossbow with the fluid, automatic motion of long practice, checked the blade of the machete, shrugged into his jacket. He looked like the leader he had been at the Junkyard — composed, certain, ready.

"Where to?" Finch asked.

"Out. Away. The farm Cameryn told us about — past the mountains, past Avarice territory. We find it, we go there, we figure out the rest later."

They were almost at the door when it opened.

The other Finch stood in the frame, backlit by the compound's floodlights. She was dressed — jacket, boots, the knife in her hand. Her lavender hair was loose around her shoulders, and in the harsh white light it looked almost silver, almost ghostly, the colour of something that existed between worlds. Her eyes were silver and bright and utterly calm. Not surprised. Not angry. Calm, in the way that a blade is calm — still, sharp, waiting.

"You know," she said, "I expected this. I even hoped for it."

"Dad—" Finch started.

Caleb stepped in front of her. "We're leaving. Don't try to stop us."

"I'm not going to stop you." The other Finch tilted her head, a slow, deliberate movement that was almost curious. "I'm going to make you fight for it. There's a difference."

She moved. Fast — faster than Finch had expected, faster than anyone that muscular should have been able to move. She was through the door and in the room in a single step, her knife leading, her body coiled and balanced and ready. The knife caught the light and held it — a bright, dangerous line in the air.

Caleb met her. Their blades clashed — machete against knife, steel against steel — and the sound was sharp and bright in the cold air, a sound that rang off the walls and filled the room with echoes. They fought with the desperate efficiency of people who knew what was at stake, trading blows and parries, each one looking for an opening, each one knowing that the first mistake would be the last.

Finch watched. She watched for three seconds. The fight was a blur of motion — Caleb's machete sweeping in a wide arc, the tyrant's knife deflecting it with a precise, economical movement, their bodies shifting and pivoting in the confined space. Then she moved.

She didn't think about it. She didn't plan it. She just — moved. The way Mara had taught her. The way her body had learned to move in three weeks of training at the Junkyard, and the weeks since, and the muscle memory that had been building in her bones without her knowing it. She stepped to the side, drew her knife, and drove it toward the other Finch's blind side.

The other Finch turned. Blocked. Finch's knife skidded off the flat of the other's blade. The tyrant smiled.

"There she is," she said. "There's the fighter."

They fought. The two Finches, the two versions, the two mirrors. Caleb had been pushed back — the tyrant had disarmed him with a move that was brutally efficient, a twist of the wrist and a strike to the forearm that sent the machete spinning into the corner. He was on the ground, his arm hanging at an odd angle, his face tight with pain.

But Finch was in now. She was in the fight, and something inside her — something she hadn't known was there — surfaced. Not rage. Not fear. Something quieter. Something determined. The quiet, stubborn refusal to be consumed by someone else's pain.

The tyrant was better. She was stronger, faster, more experienced. She fought like she had been fighting her whole life — because she had. Every blow was precise, every movement economical, every strike targeted at a vital point. Finch blocked what she could and took what she couldn't, and she felt the pain of each hit — the jab to her ribs, the strike to her shoulder, the scrape of steel across her forearm — but she didn't let it stop her.

"Don't you see?" the tyrant said, between strikes. Her voice was breathless but controlled, the voice of someone who could fight and talk at the same time, who had done this so many times it was second nature. "Don't you see that this is what we are? This is what we become? You think you're different from me, but you're not. You're just—"

"I'm not you," Finch said.

"You ARE me!"

"NO."

Finch drove forward. Not with skill — she had none. Not with technique — she had barely any. She drove forward with sheer, stubborn, desperate force, and the tyrant, who was used to fighting people who were afraid, was not prepared for someone who was too angry to be afraid.

The knife found its mark.

It went in under the tyrant's left eye, through the socket, into the brain. It happened fast — so fast that the tyrant's face didn't even change. One moment she was fighting. The next moment she was falling. The knife was still in her hand — no, it was in Finch's hand — no, it was in the tyrant's face, and the tyrant was on the ground, and her silver eyes were open and empty, and the blood was spreading across the concrete floor like a dark flower blooming.

The room was very quiet. The only sounds were the dripping of blood from the tyrant's face, the ragged sound of Finch's breathing, and the distant wind outside the walls.

Finch stood over her. Her hands were shaking. Her whole body was shaking. The knife was still in her hand — she could feel the weight of it, the slickness of it, the warmth of the blood on the handle. She looked at it. She looked at the body. She looked at her hands.

"Oh God," she said. "Oh God, oh God—"

Caleb was there. He put his arms around her. He pulled her back from the body. His grip was tight — tighter than it needed to be, the grip of someone who was holding on as much for himself as for her.
