Banks of equipment lined the walls — monitors, control panels, power distribution units, their surfaces covered in a thin layer of dust. Cables ran across the floor in neat bundles, converging on a central point.

The device.

It sat in the centre of the room on a raised platform, exactly as Mara had described. A disc, about two metres across, made of a material Finch couldn't identify — not metal, not glass, not plastic, but something that seemed to combine properties of all three. It was dark, inert, seemingly lifeless. But Finch could feel something emanating from it — a vibration, a hum, barely perceptible, like the distant sound of a tuning fork pressed against the base of her skull.

"That's it," Caleb said. His voice was barely above a whisper. "That's how I got here."

"That's how we all got here," Mara said.

Caleb approached the device. He circled it slowly, examining the surface, the controls, the connections. His face was — Finch had never seen that expression before. It was the expression of a man looking at the thing that had destroyed his life and potentially the thing that could restore it. Hope and hatred, tangled together so tightly they were indistinguishable.

"It's damaged," he said. "Look — here. The casing is cracked. And these connections — some of them are corroded. It's been sitting here for — how long?"

"Seven years," Mara said. "Since the outbreak. Nobody's touched it."

"Seven years without maintenance." Caleb ran his hand along the edge of the disc. "The calibrations will be way off. If we try to activate this without recalibrating first, we could end up anywhere. Anywhen."

"Can you recalibrate it?"

"I — I don't know. I'm not a scientist. I'm a truck driver." He looked at the control panels, the displays, the web of cables. "But these displays — they're still powered. If there's documentation, if there's a manual, if there's anything—"

He moved to the nearest console and began searching. Finch joined him, opening drawers, pulling out binders, scanning the labels on file folders. Mara watched from the doorway, too weak to move quickly but alert, her eyes tracking everything.

They found manuals. Printed ones, in a filing cabinet labelled "DISC CALIBRATION PROTOCOLS" in neat block letters. Technical documents, dense with diagrams and equations that made Finch's head spin. But Caleb read them. He read them slowly, carefully, the way a man reads a map when he knows the road is dangerous. He read them twice, three times, cross-referencing one document against another, making notes on a scrap of paper with a pencil he'd found in a drawer.

After an hour, he looked up.

"I think I can do it," he said. "It'll take time. Maybe a day. Maybe more. But I think I can do it."

"Do it," Finch said.

Caleb went to work. Finch and Mara kept watch. The facility was quiet — too quiet, the kind of quiet that made you aware of your own heartbeat, the sound of your own breathing, the creak of your own joints. Finch found herself glancing at doorways, at shadows, at the dark corridor they'd come down. Something felt wrong. Not wrong in a specific way — not a sound, not a movement, not a sign of danger. Just wrong. The way a room feels wrong when someone has been there and left.

She didn't mention it. She didn't want to worry Caleb while he was working. But she kept her knife close, and she kept her eyes open, and she kept one hand on the doorframe of the main chamber, ready to pull it shut if she needed to.

The hours passed. Caleb worked. The disc hummed, faintly, and the hum grew fractionally stronger as he adjusted settings and rerouted connections and did things to the control panels that Finch didn't understand. He talked to himself as he worked, muttering numbers and formulas and the occasional profanity, his brow furrowed in concentration.

By evening, he had made progress. The displays showed new data — coordinates, power levels, dimensional frequency readings that meant nothing to Finch but clearly meant something to Caleb. He was smiling, she realised. Not a big smile — not the kind of smile that changed his whole face — but a small one, the kind that lived in the corners of his mouth and the crinkles around his eyes.

"It's working," he said. "Not perfectly — the calibration is still approximate. But it's closer. Much closer than it was."

"Closer to Earth One?"

"Closer to Earth One."

They celebrated quietly. Mara managed a smile — a real smile, the kind that reached her eyes — and Caleb and Finch embraced, and for a moment the cold laboratory was warm with something that felt, impossibly, like hope.

And then Finch heard it.

A sound. From the corridor. From the direction of the entrance they'd come through. A sound that was not a bird, not the wind, not the generator.

Footsteps.

"Someone's here," she whispered.

Caleb's hand went to his knife. Mara's eyes narrowed. The three of them stood in the main chamber and listened, and the footsteps grew louder, and closer, and they were coming down the corridor.

The door opened.

And standing in the frame, backlit by the corridor lights, was a figure. Tall. Lean. A leather jacket with a dark stain where the left eye would be, and an eye patch over the wound, and a lavender mane of hair that fell around her shoulders like a veil.

The other Finch.

Alive.

---

## Chapter 21: The Ghost Returns

The door stood open like a wound.

For a long moment, no one moved. The hum of the Qwantum device filled the silence — a low, almost subliminal vibration that pressed against the inside of Finch's skull and made her teeth ache. Caleb's hand was frozen over the control panel, his fingers hovering above a dial he'd been turning only seconds before. Mara had gone utterly still in her chair, the colour draining from her face so rapidly that Finch could almost watch it happen, like watching a tide pull back from shore.

And in the doorway stood the girl who should have been dead.

The tyrant Finch — the other Finch, the dark mirror, the girl with the scarred hands and the dead eye and the empire of fear — stood in the fluorescent light of the Qwantum Lab with her hands at her sides, open and empty. She wore the same clothes she'd died in, or near enough: a black jacket split at the seams, dark trousers stiff with dried blood. The eye patch was new. It sat over her left eye like a bruise made permanent, the leather strap cutting across her temple and disappearing into the tangled mass of her dark hair. The remaining eye — her right, the good one, the one that had watched Finch across the prison cell in Avarice — was red-rimmed and wet.

She was breathing hard, as though she'd been running. Or as though she'd been standing in that doorway for a very long time and had only just found the will to speak.

"Don't," she said.

The word came out cracked and raw. Not a command. A plea.

Caleb recovered first. His hand moved from the dial to the heavy wrench lying on the workbench beside him. He lifted it slowly, deliberately, placing himself between the device and the door with the careful precision of a man who had learned long ago that survival was a series of small, calculated movements.

"Don't come any closer," he said. His voice was steady, but Finch could see the muscle in his jaw working, the tendon standing out like a cable beneath the skin. "Not one step."

Behind him, Mara had risen from her chair. She stood now with one hand on the backrest, her knuckles white, her body angled toward the door like a compass needle finding north. She was weak — the weeks of captivity had stolen the strength from her limbs and the colour from her face — but there was nothing weak about the way she looked at the girl in the doorway. Her eyes were the eyes of a woman who had seen this exact scene before, years ago, in a different world, and who had spent every day since wishing she had made a different choice.

The other Finch didn't move. She stood in the doorway with the harsh light behind her, and Finch noticed things she hadn't noticed before — the way the girl's shoulders curved inward, as though she were trying to make herself smaller. The way her fingers twitched at her sides, not with menace but with something that looked terribly like nerves. The bruise beneath the eye patch, fading from purple to a sickly yellow-green at its edges. The thin scar that ran from the corner of her mouth to her jaw, a wound that had healed badly and left a permanent reminder of violence.

She looked like a ghost. She looked like something that had crawled back from the dead with unfinished business.

Behind Finch, the device hummed its constant note, and the fluorescent lights buzzed, and the air in the lab felt thick with the particular tension that precedes violence — the held breath before a scream, the stillness before a storm. Finch could feel her own heartbeat in her throat, could feel the hammer's weight in her hand, could feel the distance between herself and the other Finch measuring itself in heartbeats rather than metres. Twelve heartbeats. Maybe fifteen. The distance at which a knife could reach, a gun could fire, a life could end.

"I'm not here to fight," the other Finch said again. Her voice was steadier this time, though it wavered at the edges like a flame in a draught. "I need you to understand that. I'm not here to fight. I'm not here to hurt anyone. I'm not —" She stopped. Swallowed. Her eye darted around the lab, taking in the banks of equipment, the shattered monitors, the scattered tools, the people arrayed against her like a jury. "I don't even know why I'm here. That's not — I do know. I know why I'm here. But I don't know *why* I know. If that makes any sense."

It didn't. Not really. Finch felt the words slide off her understanding like water off glass, but there was something in the way the other girl said them — a rawness, an honesty that didn't match the tyrant Finch had come to know — that made her hesitate. Made her hold the hammer a fraction lower.

Behind Caleb, Mara had not moved. She stood behind her chair now, both hands gripping the backrest, her face a study in controlled shock. Finch had seen Mara face down Hollows and Avarice soldiers and the collapse of the Junkyard with the same flat, assessing expression, but this was different. This was the face of a woman watching a ghost — not a metaphorical ghost, not a memory or a regret, but a literal ghost, standing in a doorway, bleeding from a wound that should have killed her, asking to be heard. Mara's lips were parted. Her breathing was shallow. Her eyes — those dark, deep-set eyes that had seen everything and missed nothing — were fixed on the other Finch with an intensity that bordered on hunger.

"How?" Finch asked. It was the only word she could find, and even as it left her mouth, she realised it wasn't the right question. There were a hundred ways the other girl could have survived a stab wound to the eye. Medical knowledge. Fortune. Stubbornness. The question she should have asked was *why*. Why come here. Why now. Why look at me with those red-rimmed eyes and speak in that broken voice instead of raising a weapon and taking what you want.

But the other Finch seemed to understand anyway.

"After you — after I —" She touched the eye patch, a gesture so small and quick it was almost involuntary. "After that, I went down. I remember the ground. Cold. Wet. I remember thinking, *This is it. This is how it ends.* And I was angry. Not afraid. Angry. Because I'd survived everything else — the Hollows, the exile, the years alone — and I was going to die because of a girl who looked like me."

She paused. Her eye found Finch's and held it, and in that single dark eye, Finch saw something she hadn't expected to see. Not hatred. Not hunger for power. Not the cold calculus of a tyrant. She saw exhaustion. She saw grief. She saw the vast, aching loneliness of someone who had been alone for so long that solitude had become her native language.

"The knife went through the eye," the other Finch continued, and her voice dropped to something barely above a whisper, as though the words themselves were dangerous, as though saying them too loudly would make them real again. "I felt it — I felt the blade go in. Not pain, at first. Just pressure. A cold, deep pressure, like someone pressing a finger against the back of my skull. And then the pain came, and the world went white, and then — nothing. Just the ground. Just the cold. I lay there in the mud outside the Avarice compound and I bled, and the blood ran down my face and into my mouth, and it tasted like iron and salt and something else, something sweet, and I thought — I thought I was finished."

Her hand dropped from the eye patch. Her fingers were trembling. Finch could see the fine tremor running through them, the way they shook like leaves in a wind that only the other Finch could feel.

"One of my people found me. One of the few I hadn't driven away by then — Kael. He was a straggler, a nobody, one of the nameless faces I'd collected over the years without ever learning their stories. But he came. When everyone else ran, when the compound fell apart after the battle, when Avarice crumbled like a house of cards in a storm, he came. He found me in the mud. He carried me back to a shelter — a basement, somewhere in the ruins, with a leaking pipe and a mattress that smelled like mildew. He cleaned the wound. He tried to stop the bleeding. He held a rag against my face for hours, changing it when it soaked through, talking to me the whole time — nonsense, mostly, just words, just the sound of a human voice — because I was slipping under and he knew that if I heard something, anything, I might hold on."

She stopped. Drew a breath that shuddered through her entire body.

"It got infected. Of course it did. We had nothing — no antibiotics, no clean water, no proper medical supplies. Just rags and dirty hands and whatever Kael could boil. The wound swelled. The skin around it turned black, then green. I developed a fever — high, so high I could feel my blood burning. I was delirious for weeks. I spoke in tongues. I screamed at things that weren't there. I clawed at the bandages until Kael had to tie my hands to the mattress."

Caleb's grip tightened on the wrench. "Someone."

"I don't know who." The other Finch shook her head, and there was genuine confusion in the movement, a bewilderment that seemed to come from deep within her. "An old woman. That's what I thought, at first. Grey hair. Weathered face. Hands that looked like they'd seen a thousand years of work. She sat beside my cot and she looked at me the way Mara used to look at me when I was small — with patience, and something that might have been love, and something that might have been sorrow, and something else entirely that I didn't have a name for."

Finch glanced at Mara. The older woman's face was unreadable, but her hands — those thin, capable hands — had tightened on the armrests of her chair until the knuckles were white.

"She told me to stop fighting," the other Finch said. "She told me I was tired, and that I didn't have to be tired any more. She told me to find you — you and Caleb — and to go with you. Through the portal. She said there was a door, and I was supposed to walk through it, and on the other side I was supposed to... I don't know. Start again. Be something different." She laughed, a short, broken sound that held no humour. "I thought I was hallucinating. Fever dreams. I told myself that for weeks. But she kept coming back. Every night, she was there. And the words didn't change. The words were always the same."

"She spoke to you every night?" Caleb asked, and there was something in his voice — not suspicion, not exactly, but the careful, probing tone of a man who had learned to separate truth from performance.

"Every night for weeks. And then the fever broke. I woke up, and Kael was there, and the wound was — healing. Not healed, but healing. And the old woman was gone. I never saw her again. But the words stayed. They stayed in my head like a brand, like someone had burned them into the inside of my skull, and I couldn't get rid of them no matter how hard I tried." She paused. "So I stopped trying. I stopped fighting the words, and I started listening to them, and I started doing what they said. I came here. I followed the energy. The device — when you started the recalibration, it sent out a pulse. A dimensional frequency. I felt it in Avarice, a hundred kilometres away. I felt it in my bones. And I knew. I knew where to go."

The lab was silent except for the hum of the device. Finch could hear her own heartbeat, loud and insistent, drumming against the inside of her ribs. She looked at the other Finch — really looked at her, past the eye patch and the scars and the evidence of violence — and she saw something she recognised. She saw herself. Not the tyrant, not the monster, but the broken thing underneath. The six-year-old girl who had watched her parents die. The child who had been alone in a world that wanted to eat her. The girl who had built walls so high that she'd forgotten there was a world on the other side.

"I don't understand it," the other Finch said, and her voice cracked on the last word, and Finch heard something in that crack that sounded like the beginning of surrender. "I don't understand any of it. But I know I can't keep living the way I was. I know that. I know it the way I know how to breathe. If I go back to Avarice, I'll die there. Not because someone kills me. Because I'll kill myself. Slowly. The way I've been killing myself for years."

She stepped forward. One step. Just one. But it was enough to bring her into the light, and Finch saw the tears on her face — actual tears, sliding down the scarred cheek, catching in the hollow beneath her cheekbone.

"I'm not here to fight," she said for the third time, and this time it didn't sound like a plea or a statement. It sounded like a prayer. "I'm here because a ghost told me to be. And I'm tired of being afraid of ghosts."

Caleb looked at Finch. Mara looked at Finch. The device hummed. The fluorescent lights buzzed. And Finch Adderwood stood in a laboratory at the end of the world and faced the girl she had tried to kill, and felt the shape of the next few days shift and rearrange itself like a kaleidoscope turning in the dark.

"Okay," she said, and the word tasted like copper and possibility. "Okay. But you're going to explain everything. Every single thing. And you're going to do it slowly."

The other Finch nodded. She wiped her face with the back of her hand, smearing the tears across the scar tissue, and for a moment — just a moment — she looked like what she was. Not a tyrant. Not a ghost. A sixteen-year-old girl who had been carrying the weight of the world since before she'd learned to read.

"Slowly," she agreed. And then, quieter: "Thank you."

She took a step into the lab. Then another. Her boots — battered, mud-caked, the soles worn thin from walking — made soft sounds on the concrete floor. She moved carefully, deliberately, the way you move through a room where you know you're not welcome but have been invited anyway. Her eye moved across the workbench, across the tools, across the device itself — that humming, glowing heart of the machine that had brought them all to this moment — and Finch saw something pass across her face that might have been recognition, or wonder, or the particular expression of someone seeing, for the first time, the thing that will change their life.

She stopped at the edge of the workbench. She looked at Caleb. She looked at the wrench in his hand. And then she did something that none of them expected.

She extended her hand. Not in aggression, not in demand, but in the simple, human gesture of introduction — the palm open, the fingers relaxed, the universal sign of *I am here, and I mean no harm*.

"My name is Finch," she said. "The other one. The bad one. The one you should probably still be afraid of." She paused. "But I'm going to try to be less of that. Starting now. Starting here. If you'll let me."

Caleb looked at the hand. He looked at Finch. He looked at Mara. And then, slowly, with the careful deliberation of a man who understood that this moment — this single, fragile, irreplaceable moment — would determine the shape of everything that followed, he set the wrench down on the workbench and took the other Finch's hand.

He held it for three seconds. Four. Five. The handshake of a man greeting a stranger, and the embrace of a man welcoming a daughter, and the handshake and the embrace were the same thing, performed simultaneously, in the space between two heartbeats.

"Welcome back," he said.

---

## Chapter 22: Proof

Trust is not a gift. It is not granted in a moment of eloquence or a single tear shed in fluorescent light. Trust is built, brick by brick, through a thousand small acts of vulnerability and a thousand small acts of faith. The other Finch would learn this. They all would. But first, there was the matter of the weapons.

"You're going to give them to me," Caleb said. He still held the wrench, though his grip had relaxed slightly — the knuckles no longer white, the tendons no longer standing out like bridge cables beneath the skin. "All of them. Every blade, every firearm, every piece of ammunition. You're going to hand them over, and you're going to do it without complaint, and you're going to do it now."

The other Finch didn't argue. She didn't bristle or bare her teeth or reach for the knife that Finch could see strapped to her right calf beneath the torn fabric of her trousers. She simply nodded, reached down, and began to disassemble herself.

The knife came first. She drew it from its sheath with the careful precision of someone who understood weapons — the way you handle a thing you've held a thousand times — and laid it on the floor. Then the sheath itself, unbuckled from her leg and set beside the blade. From inside her jacket she produced a pistol, compact and well-maintained despite the years, and placed it face-down on the concrete with a deliberate *clack* that echoed through the lab. Two spare magazines. A belt of shotgun shells from her waistband. A short-barrelled shotgun from a holster on her back, the stock folded and locked. Another knife — this one smaller, a utility blade with a worn handle. A length of wire that could have been a garrotte or a tripwire or simply a piece of cord; with the other Finch, it was impossible to tell.

She laid them all out on the floor in a neat line, like offerings at an altar. Then she stepped back and held her hands out, palms up, empty.

"That's everything," she said.

Caleb studied the weapons with the eye of a man who had survived the apocalypse through vigilance and paranoia. He counted them. He picked up the pistol and checked the chamber — a round was chambered, and he ejected it, catching the bullet in his palm and pocketing it with practiced ease. He broke down the shotgun, checked the magazine, checked the barrels. He examined each blade, turning them over in his calloused hands, testing the edges with his thumb.

"All of it," he said again, as though the act of disarming her was not a single event but a continuous state of being.

"I understand," the other Finch said. There was no resentment in her voice. No defiance. Just a flat, steady acceptance that struck Finch as more remarkable than any display of emotion could have been. The tyrant Finch she had known — the Finch who had imprisoned her and Caleb, who had ruled Avarice through fear and violence — would never have submitted to this. She would have fought. She would have killed. She would have burned the lab to the ground rather than give up a single weapon.

But the girl standing before her now was not that girl. Or perhaps she was that girl, but different. Changed. The way a river is different after a flood — same water, same banks, but the course has shifted and something fundamental has been rearranged.

"I want to talk," the other Finch said, and she said it with such quiet earnestness that Finch felt something shift inside her chest, some wall she hadn't known she was building begin to crumble at its foundation. "I want to tell you things. About — about me. About what happened. About who I was and who I became. I know you have no reason to listen. I know you have every reason to put me down right now, right here, and never think about it again. But I'm asking you to listen. That's all. Just listen."

Caleb looked at Finch. The look was heavy with meaning — *This is your call. This is the girl who looks like you. This is your mirror, your shadow, your twin. You decide.*

"Talk," Finch said.

So the other Finch talked.

She talked about the years after Mara left. She talked about the way the world had closed around her like a fist, the way survival had become a kind of mania, a single note struck over and over until it drowned out everything else. She talked about the hunger — not the physical hunger, though that had been there too, the gnawing emptiness of scavenging for food in a world stripped bare, but the deeper hunger, the one that lived in the space behind her ribs and whispered *more, more, always more*. The hunger to be seen. To be known. To matter.

"I built Avarice because I was afraid," she said. "That's the truth of it. I was afraid, and fear made me cruel. I hurt people. I hurt people badly. I told myself it was necessary, that the world was cruel and I had to be crueler, but the truth is simpler than that. The truth is I was a child who had been given power, and I didn't know what to do with it except use it to keep people away. Because if they got close, they might see what I really was. And what I really was, underneath everything, was afraid."

She talked about Mara. About the woman who had saved her from the Hollows when she was six, who had carried her through the ash and the ruin and the screaming, who had held her through the nightmares and taught her to read and made her feel, for a brief and shining moment, that the world was not entirely made of teeth. She talked about the exile — the day Mara had told her she had to leave the Junkyard, that the other survivors would not tolerate her presence any longer, that she had to make her own way. She talked about the betrayal she had felt, the rage, the howling fury of a child abandoned by the only person who had ever loved her.

"I hated her," the other Finch said, and her voice was barely above a whisper. "For years. I hated her more than I hated the Hollows, more than I hated the world, more than I hated myself. Because she was the one who taught me what love felt like, and then she took it away. And I thought — I genuinely thought — that if I built something big enough, strong enough, cruel enough, I could make the world pay for what she did to me."

She paused. Her eye — the good one, the dark one, the one that wasn't hidden behind leather — found Mara's face, and there was something in that look that made Finch's throat tighten. Something raw and hungry and desperately young.

"I know now that I was wrong," the other Finch said. "I know now that she didn't abandon me. She was protecting me. Protecting the Junkyard. Protecting the children. She did the hardest thing a person can do — she chose the many over the one, and she let me hate her for it. And I spent ten years turning that hatred into a weapon, and I aimed it at everyone, and I aimed it at myself, and it nearly killed me."

She stopped. The lab was quiet. The device hummed. Finch could hear her own breathing, and the other Finch's breathing, and the faint, distant sound of wind moving through the skeletal remains of the building.

"I don't want to be alone any more," the other Finch said. She said it simply, without drama, without the weight of performance. She said it the way you say *I'm thirsty* or *I'm tired* — a statement of fact, plain and unadorned. "I don't want to build walls any more. I don't want to hurt people. I don't want to wake up every morning and reach for a weapon before I reach for anything else. I want to — I want to try. To be different. To be better. I don't know if I can. I don't know if people like me can change. But I want to try."

She looked at Caleb. "I'll help you with the device. I'll do whatever you need. I'll work, and I'll wait, and I'll prove myself through actions, not words. You can watch me. You can sleep with one eye open. You can keep the weapons and keep the wrench and keep whatever else you need to feel safe. I'm not asking for trust. I'm asking for a chance."

Caleb's jaw worked. His eyes — those hard, blue, practical eyes that had seen the worst the world had to offer and survived — moved from the other Finch's face to the weapons on the floor, to Finch, to Mara, and back to the other Finch. He was a man who had learned to measure people the way a carpenter measures wood: by grain and weight and the likelihood of splitting under pressure. He had measured the other Finch before and found her wanting. Now he was measuring again.

"If you try anything," he said, and his voice was flat and final as a closed door, "I won't hesitate. Do you understand?"

"I understand."

"And Mara watches you too. She knows you better than anyone. If she says you're a threat, you're done. No second chances."

"Fine."

"And you work under my supervision. At all times. You don't touch the device without my explicit instruction. You don't go near the controls. You don't go near the calibration arrays. You follow my directions, exactly as I give them, or you're out. Understood?"

"Understood."

Caleb studied her for a long moment. Then he turned away, walked to the workbench, and began to organize his tools. It was a dismissal and an acceptance rolled into one, and the other Finch seemed to understand this, because she stepped forward — slowly, carefully, the way you approach a wild animal — and stood beside him at the bench, waiting to be told what to do.

Finch watched them from across the lab, sitting on the edge of a desk with the hammer still in her hand, and tried to reconcile the girl she was seeing with the girl she had stabbed in the eye. The tyrant who had imprisoned her. The monster who had ruled Avarice through blood and fear. The dark mirror who had shown her everything she might become if she let the world hollow her out and fill the space with rage.

But the girl at the workbench was not that girl. Or she was that girl, but the girl was changing, and the changing was happening in real time, visible in the way she held herself — less coiled, less braced for impact — and in the way she followed Caleb's instructions with an attentiveness that was almost childlike, and in the small, tentative gestures that kept flickering across her face: a slight softening of the brow, a half-smile that appeared and vanished like a bird flashing through branches.

*I could have been her*, Finch thought, and the thought was not comfortable, but it was true. If the lottery numbers had been different. If Mara had never found her. If the world had been a little crueler and a little less kind. She could have been the girl with the eye patch and the scarred hands and the empire of fear.

She could have been the ghost.

---

## Chapter 23: The Work

There is a particular quality to the sound of two people working together in silence — a rhythm, a cadence, a language spoken not in words but in the placement of tools and the turn of wrenches and the quiet, unconscious choreography of hands moving in concert toward a shared goal. It is a sound that Finch had never heard before, not in any world, and it filled the lab with something that was almost warmth.

Caleb and the other Finch worked side by side at the device, and Finch watched them from her perch on the desk, the hammer laid across her knees like a sleeping animal. She had not put it down. She was not ready to put it down. But she watched, and what she saw surprised her.

The other Finch was good.

Not just competent — *good*. She moved through the work with a fluency that spoke of deep familiarity, of hands that had spent years taking things apart and putting them back together, of a mind that understood mechanical systems the way some people understood music: instinctively, intuitively, with a feel for the way components talked to each other and the way forces distributed themselves through a structure.

"She's gifted," Caleb said quietly, without looking up. He was hunched over the central housing, adjusting a series of micro-calibrators that Finch couldn't have named, let alone manipulated. His voice held a note of grudging respect, the kind of respect that a craftsman gives to another craftsman regardless of personal feeling. "I'll give her that. She built the motorcycle?"

"The motorcycle," the other Finch said, not looking up from the circuit board she was re-soldering, "was the first thing I ever built that didn't fall apart. I was twelve. It took me four months. I had to scavenge half the parts from a junkyard on the other side of the valley — actual junkyard, not the camp. Just a heap of rust and dead machines. I used a generator from an old washing machine for the power cell. Had to rewind the coils three times because I kept burning them out."

She said it casually, matter-of-factly, but Finch heard the pride in it — a small, bright thing buried beneath layers of armour and cynicism. The pride of a girl who had built something from nothing, who had taken the wreckage of the world and turned it into something that moved.

"Where did you learn?" Caleb asked, and there was genuine curiosity in his voice now, the barrier of suspicion thinning like ice in spring.

"Taught myself. Books, mostly. There were a few technical manuals in the ruins of the library in Bathurst. Engineering basics. Circuit theory. Materials science. I read them cover to cover, five or six times, until I knew them by heart. And then I started practising. Taking things apart. Putting them back together. Breaking things, mostly, at first. But you learn more from breaking things than from building them, because breaking shows you where the weak points are."

She paused, touched the soldering iron to a joint, watched the metal flow and settle. "Mara taught me to read. She didn't teach me to fix things. I had to figure that out on my own. But the reading — the reading was the key. Once I could read, the whole world opened up. Every book was a door. Every manual was a map. And I was so hungry for it. So hungry to understand how things worked, because if I understood how things worked, I could make them work for me. And if things worked for me, I didn't need people to."

The words hung in the air between them like smoke. Caleb didn't respond. He didn't need to. The honesty of the statement was its own response, and Finch saw something pass between the two of them — not trust, exactly, but the beginning of understanding. The recognition that competence, in a world that had tried to destroy them all, was its own form of currency.

The work deepened. Caleb moved to the power coupling — a cluster of thick cables that connected the device to the facility's generator, their insulation cracked and frayed from years of neglect. He stripped the damaged sections with a utility knife, his movements precise, his brow furrowed in the way it always got when he was solving a problem that required more intuition than instruction. The other Finch handed him replacement connectors without being asked — a small box of components she'd found in a drawer marked "SPARE PARTS — DISC INTERFACE," their surfaces still gleaming with the protective oil that had kept them from corroding.

"You knew where to find those," Caleb said, not quite a question.

"I looked through the manuals yesterday. While you were sleeping." The other Finch said it without looking up from the cable she was re-routing through a guide channel in the device's chassis. "There's a maintenance log in the filing cabinet. Previous technicians dated their entries. The last one was from six years before the outbreak. They'd been having trouble with the power coupling — intermittent failures, voltage drops during portal activation. They ordered replacement parts but never installed them. The parts are still in the drawer."

Caleb stared at her for a long moment. "You read the maintenance logs."

"I read everything. I'm — I like to know how things work. Before I touch them."

There was a silence that was not uncomfortable. It was the silence of two people who had discovered, against all expectation, that they spoke the same language. Caleb turned back to the power coupling, and the other Finch moved to the calibration interface, and for a while they worked without speaking, their hands moving in the quiet, practised dance of people who had found their rhythm.

Finch watched them, and she began to see something she hadn't expected to see. She had expected resentment — the other girl was encroaching on her father's territory, stepping into the role of assistant, taking up space that Finch herself had claimed by birthright. She had expected jealousy, or anger, or the dull ache of exclusion. But what she felt instead was something more complicated and more tender: a kind of wonder.

Her other self was trying. Genuinely, earnestly, with a focus and intensity that Finch recognised because she felt it herself — the determination to prove something, not to others but to oneself. The need to show that you were more than the worst thing you had ever done. The other Finch's hands moved with purpose, her brow furrowed in concentration, her lips pressed together in the thin line of someone who understood that every task completed was a step toward redemption, even if redemption was a word she would never have used.

*I know that look*, Finch thought. *I've seen it in the mirror.*

The work continued through the afternoon. Caleb and the other Finch dismantled the calibration interface — a complex array of circuit boards and sensors that sat at the heart of the device's targeting system — and laid its components across the workbench in neat rows. Each piece was examined, tested with a multimeter that still had charge, and either cleaned and replaced or set aside for replacement. The other Finch handled the smallest components with a jeweller's precision, her scarred fingers moving with a delicacy that seemed impossible given their history. She threaded wires through channels no wider than a pencil, soldered connections that were thinner than hair, and tested circuits with the patient, methodical thoroughness of someone who understood that in a system this complex, a single loose connection could mean the difference between a portal and an explosion.

"She knows what she's doing," Caleb said to Finch during a brief pause, when the other Finch had stepped away to find a specific tool. His voice was low, pitched for Finch's ears alone. "Better than I expected. Better than she should, honestly, given what she's had to work with."

"Is that good or bad?" Finch asked.

"It's both," Caleb said. "It's good because we need the help. It's bad because —" He paused, running a hand through his hair. "Because competence is the thing I trust least. People who are good at things are the most dangerous people in the world. They're the ones who can hurt you on purpose."

He looked at Finch. "But she's trying. I can see it. Can you?"

Finch nodded. She could see it. She could see it in the way the other Finch angled her body toward Caleb when he spoke, as though his words were a light she was drawn to. She could see it in the way she asked questions — not challenging questions, not competitive questions, but genuine questions, the questions of someone who wanted to understand rather than dominate. She could see it in the small, tentative gestures that kept flickering across her face: a slight softening of the brow, a half-smile that appeared and vanished like a bird flashing through branches.

The afternoon light faded. The fluorescent lights buzzed on, casting the lab in their harsh, clinical glow. The wind outside picked up, moaning through the broken windows with a sound that was almost human. Finch found herself drifting closer to the workbench, drawn by the quiet intensity of the work and the strange, almost domestic intimacy of watching two people build something together.

"Hand me the torque wrench," Caleb said to the other Finch, and she did, and he adjusted a bolt on the device's housing with the careful, measured pressure of someone who understood that too much force could crack the casing and too little could leave the seal inadequate. The other Finch watched his hands, memorising the pressure, and when Caleb moved to the next bolt, she picked up the wrench and repeated the motion with an accuracy that made Caleb raise an eyebrow.

"First time?" he asked.

"First time on this model," she said. "Not the first time I've used a torque wrench."

He grunted. It was the grunt of approval that Caleb reserved for people who surprised him, and Finch saw the other Finch's shoulders relax a fraction, the barest easing of tension, as though that grunt had given her something she hadn't known she was looking for.

The work continued into the evening. They ate cold rations from their packs — dried meat, hard bread, water that tasted of the steel bottle it had been carried in — and they ate while they worked, their hands moving between food and tools with the absent efficiency of people for whom meals were interruptions rather than events. The other Finch ate standing up, her plate balanced on the edge of the workbench, her eye fixed on the calibration readouts scrolling across the monitor. Caleb ate sitting down, his back against the device, his legs stretched out in front of him, his jaw working mechanically as he stared at the ceiling and thought through the next stage of the recalibration.

Mara watched from her chair. She had not spoken in hours, but she was not idle. Her eyes tracked the movement of the two workers with the alert, assessing gaze of a woman who had spent her life reading people and situations, and Finch noticed — noticed with a sharpness that surprised her — that Mara's expression had changed. The hardness was still there, the wariness, the permanent guard that Mara wore like armour. But beneath it, something had shifted. Something had thawed. She looked at the other Finch the way you look at a sapling growing in a place where you'd burned the forest — with a hope so cautious it barely dared to exist, but there nonetheless, stubborn and green and alive.

"Pass me the calibration key," Caleb said, and the other Finch passed it, and their fingers brushed in the exchange, and neither of them pulled away. It was a small thing — the briefest contact, the warmth of skin against skin — but Finch saw it, and she knew that both of them felt it, and she knew that in that moment something more than a device was being calibrated. Something between a man who had lost a daughter and a girl who had lost a father was being carefully, tentatively assembled, like a bridge being built from both sides of a river.

As night fell, the lab grew quieter. The wind outside dropped to a murmur. The device hummed its constant, low note, a sound that had become so familiar it was almost silence. Caleb and the other Finch worked through the night, their movements slowing but not stopping, their voices dropping to whispers as the hours deepened. Finch dozed at her desk, drifting in and out of a sleep that was more rest than restlessness, and in the half-dreams she heard their voices — Caleb explaining the theory of dimensional harmonics, the other Finch asking questions that were sharper and more perceptive than any she had heard from a sixteen-year-old, their dialogue weaving back and forth like thread on a loom, building something that neither of them could have built alone.

She woke at dawn to find them still at it. Caleb was hunched over the console, his eyes bloodshot, his hair standing up in the particular way it did when he'd been running his hands through it for hours. The other Finch was coiled on the floor beside the device, her back against its base, her head tilted at an angle that suggested she'd fallen asleep in that position and hadn't bothered to move. Her face was relaxed in sleep — the first time Finch had seen it without the permanent tension that lived in the muscles around her eye and jaw — and in that relaxation, she looked young. She looked like what she was: a girl, not a tyrant, not a ghost, not a mirror, but a girl who was tired and trying and slowly, painstakingly learning the language of another person's trust.

Caleb looked up as Finch stirred. His eyes were tired but his voice was steady when he spoke.

"We've made progress," he said. "Real progress. The power coupling is repaired. The calibration interface is cleaned and reassembled. The targeting sensors are online. But there's still a long way to go — the dimensional frequency modulators are corroded, the phase alignment is off by a significant margin, and there are at least three circuit boards that need to be replaced entirely. We're talking days, not hours."

"Days we don't have," Finch said.

"No," Caleb agreed. "But days we'll take. Because the alternative is staying here, and staying here is not an option."

He looked at the other Finch, still sleeping against the base of the device, and his expression was — complicated. It was the expression of a man who had started the day expecting to guard against an enemy and had ended it working beside a partner. It was the expression of surprise, and caution, and something that looked, in the grey light of dawn, very much like hope.

"She's good," he said again, and this time it was not a grudging admission but a statement of fact, spoken with the quiet respect of one worker for another. "Whatever she was — whatever she did — she's good with her hands. And she's —" He paused, searching for the word. "She's trying. That counts for something. In my book, that counts for a lot."

Finch nodded. She looked at the sleeping girl — her other self, her dark mirror, the tyrant who had become a worker — and she felt the shape of the world shift beneath her, just slightly, like the first tremor before a realignment.

*We're going to be okay*, she thought, and she didn't know if it was true, didn't know if any of them were going to be okay, but the thought was there, and the thought was green, and she held onto it the way you hold onto a light in a dark room.

The other Finch stirred. Her eye opened — the one good eye, dark and deep and startled by the morning. She saw Caleb and Finch watching her, and for a moment she froze, the way a deer freezes when it hears a sound in the woods. Then the tension drained from her face, slowly, like water from a basin, and she sat up and stretched and said, without preamble:

"I had a dream about the motorcycle. The one I built when I was twelve. It was sitting in a field of flowers, and the flowers were purple, and the motorcycle was running, and nobody was trying to kill me." She paused. "It was nice."

Caleb looked at her for a long moment. Then he said, "Purple flowers. Like lavender?"

The other Finch's eye met his. "Yeah," she said quietly. "Like lavender."

And the work continued.

---

## Chapter 24: Two of a Kind

It happened in the small hours, when Caleb had finally surrendered to exhaustion and fallen asleep on a pile of jackets in the corner of the lab, and Mara had closed her eyes in her chair, her breathing slow and even, and the device sat silent and half-assembled on its table like a sleeping animal.

The two Finches sat on opposite sides of the lab, facing each other across the humming dark. The fluorescent lights had dimmed to a low, amber glow — a power-saving mode that kicked in automatically when the device wasn't running, casting long shadows across the walls and turning everything the colour of old photographs. In this light, the other Finch's eye patch was a dark smudge against her pale skin, and her good eye caught the light and held it, a single point of brightness in the gloom.

They sat in silence for a long time. It was the other Finch who spoke first.

"When I was six," she said, "I used to dream about my mother. Not about her death — I was past that, or I thought I was. I dreamed about the way she smelled. Soap and something sweet, like vanilla, or honey. I'd wake up and I'd smell it, really smell it, like she was standing right beside me. And I'd reach out, and there'd be nothing there. Just air. Just the empty room and the sound of the city dying."

Finch didn't respond. She pulled her knees to her chest and rested her chin on them, making herself small, making herself a listener.

"I stopped dreaming about her when I was eight," the other Finch continued. "When Mara sent me away. After that, the dreams changed. I dreamed about Hollows. About being chased. About the sound of jaws. And sometimes I dreamed about Mara — about her walking away, about her back, about the way she didn't turn around when she left me. That was the worst dream. Not the monsters. The not-turning-around."

Finch felt something inside her tighten. She knew that dream. She had a version of it herself — not Mara walking away, but Caleb. Her father, vanishing into the night, taken by Robert Qwantum, leaving nothing behind but an empty chair at the kitchen table and a silence that had filled the house like water.

"I lost my dad," Finch said. The words came out smaller than she'd intended, softer, as though speaking them too loudly would make them more real. "When I was twelve. He was taken — pulled through to this world by a man named Robert Qwantum. I didn't know where he was. Didn't know if he was alive or dead. For four years, I lived in a house that felt like a tomb. My mum tried. She tried so hard. But she was grieving, and I was grieving, and we grieved in different ways, and eventually the grief became a wall between us that neither of us could climb."

The other Finch listened. She didn't interrupt, didn't offer platitudes, didn't try to fix the pain. She just listened, and her listening was a kind of gift that Finch hadn't known she needed.

"And then I woke up here," Finch continued. "In Earth Two. In the middle of the apocalypse. And the first thing I saw was Liam, and the first thing I felt was fear, and the second thing I felt was — relief. Because the world was destroyed, and the world was terrible, and everyone was dead or dying, but somewhere in the middle of all that, my dad was alive. He was here. He was real. And I would have walked through fire to find him. I would have walked through the Hollows and the ruins and the end of everything to get to him."

She paused. The other Finch's eye was bright in the darkness, watching her with an intensity that was almost physical.

"Did you?" the other Finch asked. "Walk through fire?"

"Through worse," Finch said. "Through the truth. Because the truth was that my dad wasn't the man I thought he was. The truth was that he'd been part of the experiments that created the Hollow virus. That he'd helped build the thing that destroyed the world. And I had to reconcile the father I loved with the man who'd done that, and I'm still not sure I've managed it."

The other Finch was quiet for a moment. Then she said: "That's the worst kind of loneliness, isn't it? Not being alone. Being with someone and still feeling alone. Being with someone and knowing that the distance between you isn't physical — it's built. It's made of things. Words and secrets and the things people can't bring themselves to say."

Finch nodded. She didn't trust her voice.

"I've been alone since I was eight," the other Finch said. "Really alone. Not the kind of alone where you have people around you but no one who understands. The kind of alone where there's no one. Where you go days without speaking to another human being. Where you start talking to yourself, just to hear a voice, and then you start answering yourself, and then you start having arguments with yourself, and then one day you realise you've been talking to yourself for so long that you've forgotten what another person's voice sounds like."

She touched her eye patch, a gesture that Finch was beginning to recognise as a kind of self-soothing, the way a child touches a bruise to remind themselves it's real.

"The worst part wasn't the Hollows," she said. "The worst part wasn't the hunger, or the cold, or the fear. The worst part was that no one came. For years, no one came. I kept waiting. I kept thinking, *She'll come back. Mara will come back. She'll find me. She'll take me home.* And she never did. And eventually I stopped waiting, and I started building, and the building became the waiting, and the waiting became the rage, and the rage became Avarice, and Avarice became everything."

She said it without self-pity, without drama, without the performative anguish of someone who wanted to be saved. She said it the way you describe a landscape — *this is what the terrain looks like, this is what you'll find if you walk through it* — and Finch heard the honesty in it, the raw, unflinching willingness to look at herself without flinching.

"I'm sorry," Finch said, and she meant it. Not the polite, reflexive sorry of social convention, but the deep, genuine sorry of one broken person recognising another. "I'm sorry for what happened to you. I'm sorry for what you had to become."

"Don't be sorry," the other Finch said. "Just — be here. That's enough. That's more than enough."

She shifted on the floor, drawing her knees up, wrapping her arms around them, making herself small in the way that Finch had just been small — the way of someone who had learned that taking up less space meant attracting less attention. The movement was so familiar, so precisely the way Finch herself folded into her own body when the world was too much, that it made her chest ache.

"Can I ask you something?" Finch said.

"Anything."

"The boy. In Avarice. The one who — the one who was like a brother to you."

The other Finch's face changed. It was subtle — a tightening around the eye, a flicker of something dark and quick that crossed her features like a shadow crossing the face of the moon — but Finch saw it, and she knew she had touched something that still bled.

"Reef," the other Finch said. "His name was Reef. He was —" She stopped. Started again. "He was the first person I saved. Not from Hollows. From himself. He was — broken. Starving. Half-dead when I found him. I was twelve, maybe thirteen. He was younger. Ten. He had these huge eyes that took up his whole face, and he looked at me like I was — like I was the answer to a question he'd been asking his whole life."

She paused. "I taught him to fight. Taught him to survive. Taught him to be hard, because hardness was the only currency that mattered in the world we lived in. And he learned. God, he learned. He became — he became what I needed him to be. A soldier. A soldier who would do anything I asked, because I was the one who had saved him, and saving someone is a kind of leash, isn't it? A kind of ownership. You saved them, so they belong to you. That's what I thought, anyway. That's what I told myself."

"And Mara took him away," Finch said.

The other Finch's jaw tightened. "Mara took them all away. Reef. Spike. Bo. Wren. Cass. She pulled them out of Avarice one by one, like pulling teeth. She told them I was toxic. She told them I was going to get them killed. She told them I was — she used the word *monster*. She told them I was a monster, and they believed her, because she was Mara, and Mara had always been the voice of authority, the voice of reason, the voice of the mother who knew best."

The words came out hard and flat, each one a stone dropped into the silence between them. Finch heard the old rage in them — the rage of a child who had been abandoned by the person she loved most — and she heard something else beneath it: the dawning, reluctant recognition that Mara might have been right.

"And you hated her for it," Finch said.

"I hated her for it. I hated her with everything I had. I hated her for saving me and then leaving me. I hated her for teaching me to be strong and then taking away the only people who made the strength worth having. I hated her for being right — because she was right, Finch. She was right. I was toxic. I was dangerous. I was going to get those kids killed if she hadn't taken them. And I knew it. I knew it the whole time, and I hated her anyway, because knowing the truth and accepting it are two very different things."

She laughed — a short, bitter sound that held no warmth. "I built Avarice on that hatred. Every wall, every weapon, every scream. It was all — fuel. Hatred is good fuel. It burns hot and it burns long and it keeps you warm when everything else has gone cold. But it's also toxic. It poisons everything it touches. It poisoned me. It poisoned the people around me. It turned me into the thing Mara always said I was."

The silence that followed was not empty. It was full — full of all the things they had both lost, all the people they had both been, all the roads they had walked and the roads they hadn't. Finch could feel the other girl's pain like a physical thing, radiating from her in waves, and she wanted to reach across the distance between them and take her hand, but she didn't know if she was allowed. She didn't know if the distance was bridgeable. She didn't know if some wounds were too deep for touch.

"Do you still hate her?" Finch asked.

The other Finch was quiet for a long time. When she spoke, her voice was different — smaller, rawer, stripped of the armour she usually wore around her words.

"No," she said. "I don't hate her. I haven't hated her for a long time. The hatred — it just ran out. Like fuel. Like a fire that burns until there's nothing left to burn, and then it's just — ash. And you're standing there in the cold, and the ash is everywhere, and you realise that the fire didn't keep you warm after all. It just made the cold feel colder when it was gone."

She touched her eye patch again. "I stopped hating her the night I stabbed you. The night I lost the eye. Because in that moment — in the moment when the blade went in and the world went white and I was lying on the ground, dying, I realised something. I realised that I had become exactly what I hated. I had become the thing that hurt people. I had become the violence. I had become the monster. And Mara — Mara had been trying to stop that from happening for twenty years. She had been trying to save me from myself. And I had spent those twenty years proving her wrong."

She paused. "Except she wasn't wrong. She was right. She was always right. And I was always — I was always the thing she was trying to prevent."

"I don't think that's true," Finch said, and the words came out before she could stop them, before she could weigh them and measure them and decide whether they were the right words to say. "I don't think you were ever — I don't think you were ever the monster. I think you were the girl. The girl who got lost. The girl who forgot how to find her way back."

The other Finch looked at her. In the amber light, with the eye patch and the scars and the raw, exposed face of someone who had been crying and had stopped and might start again, she looked — fragile. Not weak, not broken, but fragile in the way that glass is fragile: capable of extraordinary things, capable of catching the light and turning it into something beautiful, but capable of shattering if the blow is hard enough.

"I forgot how to cry," the other Finch said. "Did you know that? I forgot. It's been — I don't know. Ten years? Twelve? I cried when I was six, after my parents died. Mara held me and I cried until I couldn't breathe. And then I stopped. I stopped crying because crying was — weakness. And in the world we lived in, weakness was death. So I locked it away. I locked it behind a wall, and I built the wall higher and higher, and eventually the wall was so high that I couldn't even see over it. And I thought — I thought I'd lost it. I thought the ability to cry was just — gone. Amputated. Cut off and thrown away."

She looked at her hands. The scarred hands, the calloused hands, the hands that had built and destroyed in equal measure. "But it wasn't gone. It was just — waiting. Like a spring underground. Like a river that goes underground and comes back up somewhere else. It was there the whole time. I just needed —" She swallowed. "I just needed someone to be there when it came back."

"I'm here," Finch said, and the words were simple and true and they were the only thing she had to give.

The other Finch nodded. She pressed her palms against her eyes — her one good eye and the ruined place beneath the patch — and breathed. Finch could see the effort of it, the way her shoulders rose and fell, the way her jaw clenched and released, the way she was holding herself together through sheer force of will while the thing she had locked away pressed against its walls from the inside.

"I want to tell you about the first time I killed someone," the other Finch said, and her voice was flat now, clinical, the voice of someone describing something they had rehearsed many times in the privacy of their own mind. "Not a Hollow. A person. A survivor who tried to steal food from the compound when I was fourteen. He was — older. Maybe twenty. He had a knife. I had — nothing. I was unarmed. And he came at me, and I — I moved. I don't even know how. I moved, and the knife was in my hand, and he was on the ground, and there was blood everywhere, and I was standing over him with his knife in my hand and I was —"

She stopped. Her breath came in short, sharp gasps.

"I was calm," she said. "That's the terrible part. I was calm. I looked at him — at the blood, at the light going out of his eyes — and I was calm. And I thought: *This is what I'm for. This is what I'm good at. This is the thing I can do that no one else can do.* And I built on that. I built everything on that. The killing, the cruelty, the — the empire. It all came from that moment. From the moment I looked at a dead man and felt calm."

The silence that followed was the loudest silence Finch had ever heard. She could hear the hum of the device, the distant moan of the wind, the soft sound of Caleb breathing in his sleep, the creak of Mara's chair as she shifted in her rest. But beneath all of that, she could hear the other girl's pain, loud as a scream, sharp as a blade.

"I'm sorry," Finch said again, and she knew the words were inadequate, knew they were small and hollow against the weight of what she had heard, but she said them anyway because the alternative was silence, and silence was what had broken them both.

"Don't be sorry," the other Finch said, and her voice was different now — not flat, not clinical, but raw, scraped clean of all pretence. "Just — listen. That's all. Just listen."

"I'm listening."

"Then I'll tell you the rest. All of it. Every ugly, terrible thing I've ever done. Because I need someone to hear it. I need someone to know the whole truth and still be sitting here when I'm done. And you're the only person who can be that person. You know that, don't you? You're the only one who can hear this and understand it, because you're me. You're the me that didn't break. You're the me that got saved."

The words hung in the air like smoke. Finch felt them settle into her, felt their weight, felt the truth of them like a stone in her chest. She was the me that got saved. She was the version of herself that Mara had found and Caleb had protected and the world had not yet managed to destroy. She was the lucky one. The one who had been pulled back from the edge before the fall was complete.

And sitting here, in the amber glow of the lab, with the other Finch's raw, honest voice filling the space between them, she understood something that she had not understood before. She understood that survival was not a solo act. It was not a matter of strength or luck or will. It was a matter of connection — of having someone, somewhere, who would hold the rope while you climbed. And the other Finch had had no one. For twelve years, she had had no one. And the absence of that connection had hollowed her out and filled the hollow space with rage and cruelty and the desperate, destructive need to control everything because nothing had ever controlled her, nothing had ever caught her, nothing had ever held on.

"I understand," Finch said. And she did.

The other Finch's face crumpled. Not dramatically — not the theatrical collapse of a movie breakdown. Just a small, quiet crumpling, like a piece of paper being gently crushed. Her eye filled with tears, and she didn't wipe them away, and she didn't look away, and she sat there and let the tears fall and let Finch see them, and the act of letting someone see — of allowing the mask to come off, of choosing vulnerability over armour — was the bravest thing Finch had ever witnessed.

"I've been alone since I was eight," the other Finch said, and her voice was barely above a whisper now, a thread of sound in the humming dark. "Really alone. Not the kind of alone where you have people around you but no one who understands. The kind of alone where there's no one. Where you go days without speaking to another human being. Where you start talking to yourself, just to hear a voice, and then you start answering yourself, and then you start having arguments with yourself, and then one day you realise you've been talking to yourself for so long that you've forgotten what another person's voice sounds like."

She touched her eye patch, a gesture that Finch was beginning to recognise as a kind of self-soothing, the way a child touches a bruise to remind themselves it's real.

"The worst part wasn't the Hollows," she said. "The worst part wasn't the hunger, or the cold, or the fear. The worst part was that no one came. For years, no one came. I kept waiting. I kept thinking, *She'll come back. Mara will come back. She'll find me. She'll take me home.* And she never did. And eventually I stopped waiting, and I started building, and the building became the waiting, and the waiting became the rage, and the rage became Avarice, and Avarice became everything."

She said it without self-pity, without drama, without the performative anguish of someone who wanted to be saved. She said it the way you describe a landscape — *this is what the terrain looks like, this is what you'll find if you walk through it* — and Finch heard the honesty in it, the raw, unflinching willingness to look at herself without flinching.

"I'm sorry," Finch said, and she meant it. Not the polite, reflexive sorry of social convention, but the deep, genuine sorry of one broken person recognising another. "I'm sorry for what happened to you. I'm sorry for what you had to become."

"Don't be sorry," the other Finch said. "Just — be here. That's enough. That's more than enough."

They sat in silence for a while after that. The lab hummed. The shadows shifted. And then, without warning, without prelude, without any of the careful emotional scaffolding that people build around their most vulnerable moments, the other Finch began to cry.

It was not a dramatic weeping. It was not the heaving, gasping sobs of a breakdown or the silent, dignified tears of a movie heroine. It was the raw, ugly crying of someone who had forgotten how to cry and was remembering in real time — the kind of crying that involves the whole body, that shakes the shoulders and reddens the face and produces sounds that are closer to animal than human. It was the cry of a dam breaking, of a pressure valve releasing, of something that had been held back for so long that it had become part of the architecture of the self.

The other Finch cried, and Finch Adderwood did the only thing she could think to do. She crossed the lab, and she sat down beside her other self on the cold concrete floor, and she put her arm around the shaking shoulders, and she held on.

She held on while the other Finch sobbed into her shoulder, her tears hot and wet against Finch's neck. She held on while the crying turned to shuddering and the shuddering turned to silence. She held on while the other Finch's breathing slowly, slowly steadied, like a car engine cooling after a long drive.

"It's okay," Finch whispered, and she didn't know if it was true, didn't know if anything was okay or would ever be okay, but the words needed to be said, and so she said them. "It's okay. I'm here."

The other Finch pulled back. Her face was a mess — red, swollen, streaked with tears and snot, the eye patch slightly askew. She looked, in that moment, exactly like what she was: a sixteen-year-old girl who had been carrying far too much for far too long.

"Sorry," she said, and laughed, a wet, broken sound that was more honest than any laugh Finch had heard in either world. "I don't know what — I don't know what just happened."

"You cried," Finch said. "For the first time since you were six."

The other Finch stared at her. Then she laughed again, and this time the laugh was a little less broken, a little more whole, and Finch felt something inside her chest crack open like a seed.

"Yeah," the other Finch said. "Yeah. I guess I did."

They sat on the floor of the lab in the amber light, shoulder to shoulder, hip to hip, identical faces streaked with tears, and Finch thought: *This is what it feels like. This is what it feels like to look in a mirror and not be afraid of what looks back.*

---

## Chapter 25: The Swarm

The first sign was the silence.

Not the silence of the lab — that was a living silence, filled with the hum of the device and the soft breathing of sleeping people and the distant moan of wind through broken walls. This was a different silence. A silence that came from outside. A silence that fell over the world like a hand over a mouth.

Finch felt it before she heard it. A prickle at the base of her skull, a tightening in her stomach, the ancient mammalian instinct that says *something is wrong and it is coming closer*. She was on her feet before she was fully awake, the hammer in her hand, her heart hammering against her ribs like a fist against a locked door.

"Caleb," she said.

Her father was already moving. He was awake — had been awake, she realised, watching her and the other Finch through the small hours, keeping vigil the way he always kept vigil, with one eye closed and one eye open and his hand never far from a weapon. He crossed the lab in three strides and reached the window, pressing himself flat against the wall beside it, peering out through the shattered glass into the grey pre-dawn.

"How many?" he asked.

"Too many to count," Finch said. She was at the other window, the one that overlooked the approach road — a narrow, potholed track that wound through the scrubland toward the lab. And what she saw made her breath catch in her throat.

They came out of the mist like a tide. Hollows. Not a handful, not a dozen, not even fifty. Hundreds. A vast, shuffling, moaning mass of grey-skinned, empty-eyed things that moved with the terrible patience of water eroding stone. They filled the road and spilled over its edges, crushing through the scrub, knocking aside the rusted husks of abandoned vehicles, flowing around obstacles with the mindless, relentless persistence of a flood. The sound they made was the sound of a thousand whispers becoming a roar, a wet, guttural murmur that rose and fell like breathing, like the lab itself was inhaling and exhaling through the mouths of the dead.

"The device," Caleb said. "The power signatures. When we started the recalibration, it must have —"

"Generated enough energy to light up a beacon," the other Finch finished. She was on her feet too, standing beside Caleb at the window, and her face — the face that Finch knew, the face of the tyrant, the face that had once smiled while people suffered — was grim. "They're drawn to concentrated power. Everyone in Avarice knew that. You don't run high-output generators at night. You don't use electronics unless you have to. You keep your signal quiet, or the Hollows come."

"How long do we have?" Finch asked.

Caleb was already calculating. His eyes moved across the landscape with the practised efficiency of a man who had survived the end of the world by learning to read terrain the way a general reads a battlefield. "Five minutes. Maybe less. They're not running — they never run — but they don't stop either. And the ones at the front are already on the access road."

"There's only one way in," the other Finch said. "The main entrance. The back is sealed — concrete and rebar, half-collapsed. We hold the front, we hold the lab."

"With what?" Finch asked. She looked around the room, cataloguing their resources with the swift, desperate arithmetic of survival. Two firearms — the pistol and the shotgun that Caleb had taken from the other Finch. The wrench. The hammer. A length of pipe. A fire extinguisher. A box of tools. Not enough. Not nearly enough for what was coming.

"The device," the other Finch said, and there was something in her voice — a new note, a hardness that Finch recognised because she had heard it in her own voice, in moments when the only alternative to action was death. "If we can pulse it — send out a focused electromagnetic burst — it might disrupt their nervous systems. Hollows have a rudimentary electrical field. It's how they coordinate, how they move in swarms. Hit them with a strong enough pulse, and you scramble the field. Like an EMP, but targeted."

"Can it do that?" Caleb asked.

"I built the amplification array," the other Finch said. "I know what it can do. Give me two minutes."

Caleb looked at her. The look lasted a heartbeat — two heartbeats — and in that time, Finch saw something pass between them. Not trust. Not yet. But something adjacent to trust. The willingness to rely on someone's competence in the face of annihilation. The recognition that survival was not a solo act but a collaboration, and that collaboration required the suspension of doubt, even if only for as long as the crisis lasted.

"Two minutes," Caleb said. "Go."

The other Finch went. Her hands moved across the device with a speed and confidence that Finch could only watch in awe, flipping switches, rerouting circuits, adjusting amplifiers. She worked with the focused intensity of a musician performing a complex piece — no hesitation, no wasted movement, every gesture precise and purposeful.

Finch and Caleb fortified the entrance. They dragged desks across the doorway, piled equipment in front of them, created a makeshift barricade that wouldn't stop the Hollows for long but might slow them enough to buy time. The other weapons were distributed — the pistol to Finch, the shotgun to Caleb, the wrench and the hammer and the lengths of pipe distributed among them like talismans against the dark.

Mara sat in her chair, too weak to stand, too stubborn to close her eyes. She held the fire extinguisher in her lap, her thin arms wrapped around it, her face set in the hard, determined expression of a woman who had survived the apocalypse once and was prepared to do it again.

The first impacts came against the barricade with a sound like hands drumming on a coffin lid. The Hollows had found the entrance. They pressed against the desks, against the equipment, against the improvised wall, and the wall groaned and shifted, and Finch heard the crack of wood and the screech of metal under pressure.

"Ready," the other Finch called from the device. "On your mark."

Caleb looked at Finch. Finch looked at Caleb. And in that look was everything — four years of separation, the grief and the searching and the horror of the Junkyard and the bridge and the mountains and the prison and the lab. Everything they had survived. Everything they had lost. Everything they were still fighting for.

"Now," Finch said.

The other Finch threw the switch.

The device screamed. Not hummed, not buzzed, not vibrated — *screamed*, a sound so high and so intense that it felt less like hearing and more like being struck. The air in the lab compressed, thickened, became almost solid. Finch felt it in her teeth, in her bones, in the fluid of her eyes. The fluorescent lights flared to blinding white and then died, and in the sudden darkness, the device pulsed with a light that was not light — a frequency that existed somewhere beyond the visible spectrum, a wavelength that Finch felt rather than saw, a colour that had no name because it existed in no human language.

The pulse hit the Hollows like a wall of force. Finch felt it pass through her — a wave of pressure, a momentary distortion, as though reality itself had flexed like a sheet of metal — and then she heard the sound. A sound she had never heard before and would never forget. The sound of hundreds of Hollows stumbling, staggering, falling. The sound of coordination dissolving. The sound of a swarm becoming a scatter, a chaos, a thousand individual bodies suddenly unable to remember how to move as one.

The barricade buckled. Hollows spilled through, but they moved badly — stumbling, colliding, falling over each other, their movements jerky and uncoordinated, their empty eyes rolling in their skulls. They were still dangerous. They were always dangerous. But they were no longer a tide. They were a flood that had broken its banks, spreading in random directions, unable to find its course.

Finch fought. She fought with the hammer and with the pistol, with the desperate, efficient violence of someone who had learned to fight in a world that rewarded nothing else. She struck and shot and struck again, and the Hollows fell, and more came, and the air filled with the copper-sweet smell of blood and the wet, organic sound of impact.

Beside her, the other Finch fought too. She fought with a length of pipe in one hand and a fire axe she'd pulled from a wall bracket in the other, and she fought *well* — with a fluidity and a ferocity that spoke of years of practice, of a body trained for violence and redirected, now, toward protection. She positioned herself between Finch and a cluster of Hollows that had broken through the left flank, and she swung the axe in wide, devastating arcs, clearing space, buying time, creating a perimeter of safety around the girl she had once tried to kill.

There was a moment — a single, crystalline moment, suspended in the chaos like a drop of water in a storm — when Finch looked up from the body of a fallen Hollow and saw the other Finch standing over her, the axe raised, the eye patch dark against her pale face, her good eye burning with something that was not rage but its opposite. Protection. Fierce, furious, absolute protection. The look of someone who has decided that the person behind them will not die today, not on her watch, not while she still had breath in her body and strength in her arms.

And Finch understood. Understood what Mara had seen in the girl, all those years ago. Understood what the ghost — whoever or whatever the ghost had been — had seen in her. Understood that the tyrant and the protector were the same person, separated by a single choice, a single moment, a single act of will that said *I will not let this person fall*.

The battle lasted an hour. It felt like a lifetime. When the last Hollow fell and the silence returned — a real silence this time, the silence of aftermath — the lab was wrecked.
