The barricade was destroyed. The equipment was smashed. The walls were streaked with blood and ichor and the evidence of desperate, close-quarters violence.

But they were alive. All of them. Caleb, with a gash on his forehead and a look in his eyes that said he'd do it all again. Mara, still in her chair, the fire extinguisher dented but unused, her face grey with exhaustion and fierce with pride. The other Finch, breathing hard, leaning on the axe, her clothes torn and her hands bleeding and her eye — her one good eye — bright with something that looked, impossibly, like joy.

And Finch. Standing in the wreckage, the hammer in her hand, her body aching, her heart hammering, her mind clear. She looked at the other Finch, and the other Finch looked at her, and between them passed something that had no name — something older than words, deeper than understanding, more real than trust.

"You fought for me," Finch said.

"Of course I did," the other Finch said, as though it were the most obvious thing in the world. As though there had never been a question.

And in that moment, standing in the ruins of the lab with the dead scattered around them and the living huddled in the wreckage, Finch Adderwood felt something she had not felt since she'd woken up in Earth Two.

She felt safe.

---

## Chapter 26: Ready

The recalibration took three more days.

They worked in shifts — Caleb and the other Finch on the device, Finch and Mara on the defences, reinforcing the entrance, setting up warning systems, creating the kind of layered security that the Junkyard had once prided itself on. The Hollows had been repelled, but not destroyed, and the dead ones outside would attract scavengers — both human and otherwise. The lab needed to be a fortress as well as a laboratory.

The work was slow, methodical, and punctuated by the kind of quiet conversations that happen when people have decided to trust each other and are still learning how. Caleb and the other Finch talked while they worked — not about feelings, not about the past, but about the device. About the science. About the impossible mathematics of dimensional travel and the equally impossible engineering required to make it real. It was the language of two minds meeting on common ground, and Finch listened to their voices drifting across the lab and thought about how strange it was — how strange and how beautiful — that people who had been enemies could become something else simply by working side by side.

The recalibration itself was a painstaking process. Caleb and the other Finch had dismantled the device's control panel and laid its components across the workbench in neat rows — circuit boards, wiring harnesses, a central processing unit the size of a brick that hummed with a faint, residual warmth. The calibration interface was a screen that displayed columns of numbers — dimensional coordinates, frequency modulations, phase alignments — that meant nothing to Finch but clearly meant a great deal to the two people who were hunched over them, arguing in the low, intense tones of specialists who cared deeply about very specific things.

"The phase alignment is off by point-zero-three," the other Finch said, tapping the screen. "That's enough to shift us by — what? A full dimension?"

"Depends on the dimensional density of the target," Caleb replied. "On a dense multiverse, point-zero-three could mean Earth Three instead of Earth One. On a sparse one, it might not matter at all."

"Then we need to tighten it. We need point-zero-zero-one or better."

"With this equipment? We'd need a full quantum oscilloscope. Which we don't have."

"Then we build one."

They built one. From spare parts, from scavenged components, from the guts of a radio that had been sitting in a box of electronic refuse in the corner of the lab. It took them six hours and three arguments and one moment where the other Finch threw a soldering iron across the room and Caleb caught it without looking up from his work, which Finch found simultaneously impressive and concerning. But by the end of the six hours, they had a device that Caleb said was "close enough" and the other Finch said was "absolutely not close enough" and Caleb said "close enough for government work" and the other Finch said "which government" and they both laughed, which was the first time Finch had heard the other Finch laugh.

The final calibration involved standing at the device's control panel and entering a sequence of coordinates that Caleb read from a handwritten sheet of paper — the same coordinates he had been carrying since the Junkyard, written in his own careful hand, the numbers that represented Earth One's position in the dimensional spectrum. The other Finch entered them while Caleb watched, checking each digit against his notes, and when the last number was entered, the device emitted a sound that was different from its usual hum — a higher, clearer tone, like a bell being struck once, cleanly, in a room with good acoustics.

"That's the resonance," Caleb said. "That means it's locked. The coordinates are set."

"And if they're wrong?"

"Then we go somewhere that isn't home. But they're not wrong. I've checked them eleven times."

"Check them a twelfth."

He did. They were right.

That night, Finch sat in the lab while the others slept and stared at the device. It hummed softly in the darkness, a low, constant vibration that she could feel in her teeth and her bones and the soles of her feet. The disc at its centre was dark now, but she could see the faintest trace of blue light at its edge, like the memory of a flame, like the ghost of a fire that had been burning for years and would burn for years more. She thought about what it would feel like to step through it. She thought about what she would see on the other side.

Brookvale. The house on the street where she had grown up. Her mother's kitchen, the light through the doorway, the smell of pots and the hum of a song. The flannel jacket on the hook by the door. The boots by the back door. The wedding ring that had never been found.

She thought about what her mother would say. She thought about whether her mother would recognise her. She had been sixteen when she left — sixteen, with lavender hair and a colouring book and a world that made sense. She was still sixteen, but the lavender hair was the only thing that hadn't changed. Everything else — her face, her body, her eyes, the way she held herself, the way she moved through space — had been rewritten by months of fighting and running and surviving. She wondered if her mother would see the girl she had lost or the woman she had become, and she wondered which one her mother would prefer.

She wondered if her mother was still there. If the house was still standing. If the world on the other side had continued without her, the way worlds do, the way time does, carrying everything forward with the indifferent momentum of a river. Eight years had passed when she crossed over. How many had passed since? Was it still 2031 in Brookvale? Was it 2040? Was the house empty? Was the street empty? Was the whole world empty, the way this one was?

She didn't know. She couldn't know. And the not-knowing was the hardest part — harder than the fighting, harder than the fear, harder than the Hollows and the tyrant and the river and everything else. The not-knowing was a wound that wouldn't close, a question that wouldn't resolve, and she carried it in her chest like a stone as she sat in the humming dark and stared at the machine that might, if everything went according to plan, take her home.

Helen came on the second day. She arrived at dawn with a basket of bread and dried meat and a jar of honey that she'd been saving, she said, for a special occasion. She was a small woman, Helen, with a weathered face and hands that spoke of decades of hard work, and she looked at the wrecked lab and the bandaged fighters and the humming device with the calm, appraising eye of someone who had seen worse.

"You're leaving," she said to Caleb. It was not a question.

"We have to," Caleb said. "The device — it's our best chance. Our only chance. To get home."

Helen nodded. She set the basket on the workbench and unwrapped the food with careful, deliberate movements, as though the act of feeding people was a kind of prayer. "I understand," she said. "I've lived on this farm for thirty years. Before the world ended, after the world ended. It's the only home I've ever known. But I know what it means to need to go somewhere else. I know what it means to have a place that isn't here."

She looked at Mara, who sat in her chair with her hands folded in her lap, and something passed between the two women — a look of understanding, of shared experience, of the particular bond that forms between people who have survived the same catastrophe and come out the other side carrying different burdens.

"Take the food," Helen said. "Take all of it. And take this." She reached into her pocket and produced a small, flat stone, smooth and grey, with a single white line running through its centre like a vein of lightning. "It's a river stone. From the creek behind the farm. I've carried it for twenty years. It's lucky, I think. Or maybe it's just a stone, and I've been telling myself stories about it because stones are easier to carry than hope."

She pressed it into Caleb's hand, and Caleb closed his fingers around it, and Finch saw the old man's eyes go bright with something that he would never, in a thousand years, admit was tears.

"Thank you," he said. "For everything."

"Thank me by coming back," Helen said. "Someday. When the world makes sense again. Come back and tell me stories about where you've been."

Caleb nodded. He couldn't speak. He didn't need to.

Thomas came to say goodbye on the last evening. He appeared at the lab door with his hands in his pockets and his angular face set in the expression of a man who was working very hard to appear calm. He stood in the doorway for a moment, looking at the device, at the workbench, at the people who had become, over the past few weeks, something that resembled a family. Then he walked in and sat down on a crate and said nothing for a long time.

"I built a bookshelf once," he said finally. "In Wollongong. For a library. It was made of recycled timber — old fence posts, mostly — and it took me three weeks. When it was finished, the librarian stood in front of it and said, 'It's beautiful,' and I said, 'It's a bookshelf,' and she said, 'Those aren't mutually exclusive.'"

He looked at Caleb. "You're a bookshelf. You know that? You're a thing that holds other things up. And the fact that you think you're just a piece of timber is — well. It's the most bookshelf thing about you."

Caleb stared at him. "I have absolutely no idea what to do with that."

"Neither did I. That's the point." Thomas stood up, shook Caleb's hand, and walked out without looking back. In the doorway, he paused, just for a second, and Finch saw his jaw work, the way it did when he was holding something in. Then he was gone.

Lily came too, later, when the lab was quiet. She brought a book — a battered paperback copy of *The Hobbit*, its spine cracked, its pages yellowed — and pressed it into Finch's hands without ceremony.

"You'll need something to read wherever you're going," she said.

"Lily, I can't take—"

"You can and you will. I've read it four times. It's about someone who goes on a journey they didn't ask for and comes back different. It seemed appropriate."

Finch held the book and looked at Lily — at the braid that was coming undone, at the scar on her knee, at the face of a girl who had become her friend in a world where friendship was the rarest and most valuable currency. She wanted to say something adequate, something that captured the weight of what they were losing and the lightness of what they might find, but the words wouldn't come, and so she did the only thing she could think of. She hugged her.

Lily hugged her back, hard, the way you hug someone when you know you might not see them again.

"Don't die," Lily said into her shoulder.

"Working on it."

"Work harder."

The device was ready on the third day. Caleb ran the final diagnostics with the careful precision of a surgeon closing an incision, checking and rechecking each circuit, each connection, each calibration point. The other Finch assisted, handing him tools and readings with the quiet efficiency of someone who had found her place and was content to occupy it.

They tested it once — a small, contained test, opening the portal for a fraction of a second to verify the dimensional signature. The disc at the centre of the device lit up, a pale blue light that filled the lab with a quality of illumination that was not quite light and not quite something else, and for a heartbeat the air above the disc shimmered and parted, and Finch saw — through a gap in the fabric of reality — a flash of something green and familiar. Grass. Trees. A sky that was not grey. Then the portal closed, and the light faded, and the hum returned to its normal pitch, and Caleb let out a breath that he had been holding for longer than Finch had noticed.

"That was Earth One," he said. "For a fraction of a second. That was home."

"Are you sure?"

"I'd know that sky anywhere." He paused, his hand still hovering over the console, his eyes fixed on the spot where the portal had been. "Green grass. Real sunlight. Clouds. I saw clouds, Finch. Actual clouds. Not the grey smear we've been living under, but real, white, cumulus clouds, the kind that look like they've been put there by someone who understood what a sky was supposed to look like."

His voice cracked on the last word. Finch had never heard her father's voice crack before — not during the Hollow attacks, not during the prison break, not during any of the thousand terrible moments that had defined their time in Earth Two. But it cracked now, on the word *clouds*, and Finch understood why. Clouds were not a luxury. Clouds were not a wonder. Clouds were the baseline, the minimum, the thing you were supposed to be able to look up and see without having to fight for it. And her father had been denied that baseline for so long that seeing it, even for a fraction of a second, had broken something open inside him.

She reached out and put her hand on his arm. He looked at her, and his eyes were bright, and he nodded, once, and the moment passed. But Finch carried it with her — the image of her father, standing in a laboratory at the end of the world, weeping silently over the memory of clouds.

"I'd know that sky anywhere," he said again, and this time his voice was steady.

"It's as good as it's going to get," Caleb said, stepping back from the device and wiping his hands on a rag. The device hummed with a new quality now — a deeper, more resonant hum, as though it had found its voice. "There's always a margin of error with dimensional travel. The calibrations are precise, but the physics is — well. The physics is Qwantum's physics, and Qwantum's physics don't always behave the way you expect them to."

"What kind of margin of error?" Finch asked.

"The kind where we end up exactly where we want to be," Caleb said, "or the kind where we end up somewhere else entirely. The device is stable. The portal will open. The question is not whether we'll get through. The question is where we'll come out."

"Comforting," the other Finch said, and the dryness of her tone made Finch smile despite everything.

The device hummed. The portal pulsed. The light played across their faces, painting them in colours that shifted and changed like the surface of a kaleidoscope. Finch looked at the portal — really looked at it, letting her eyes relax, letting her focus soften — and she saw things she hadn't seen before. Patterns in the light. Structures in the shimmer. The faintest suggestion of geometry, of order, of a world on the other side that operated on principles that were different from this one but no less real. It was like looking at a painting through water — the shapes were there, the colours were there, the image was there, but it was distorted by the medium between her and it, twisted by the dimensional membrane that the portal was working so hard to tear.

She wondered what Mara was doing right now. She wondered if Mara was awake, or sleeping, or sitting in her chair by the window of the farmhouse, staring out at the grey Australian sky and thinking about the three people who were about to step through a machine and disappear from her world forever. She wondered if Mara knew, in the deep, instinctive way that Mara knew things, that this was happening now — that the moment of departure had arrived, and that the people she had fought for and bled for and loved were standing at the threshold of the unknown.

*I'll come back*, Finch thought. *If there's a way, I'll come back. I'll find a way across, and I'll come back, and I'll find you, and I'll tell you everything.* She didn't know if it was a promise or a prayer. She didn't know if the universe cared about either one. But she made it anyway, because some things needed to be said even if no one was listening.

They packed what they could carry. Food, water, tools, the few weapons that remained functional. Caleb took the calibration readouts, the data that would allow them to adjust the device on the other side — if there was an other side, if there was a device to adjust, if the margins of error didn't conspire to deposit them in the middle of the Pacific Ocean or the surface of the sun. Each person carried a pack — not heavy, because weight was a luxury they couldn't afford, but sufficient. A change of clothes. A water bottle. A knife. A torch. The basics of survival, compressed into a space between two shoulder straps.

The other Finch packed methodically, her movements precise and deliberate, the movements of someone who had packed for war and was now packing for something that was either the opposite of war or its most extreme expression. She checked each item twice — the water bottles sealed, the food wrapped tight, the weapons accessible but not exposed. She moved through the lab with the quiet efficiency of a soldier preparing for deployment, and Finch watched her and thought about how strange it was — how impossibly, beautifully strange — that this woman, this version of herself from another Earth, had become someone she could trust. Someone she could stand beside at the threshold of a machine that would tear reality apart and put it back together again, and know that they were both, in their own ways, ready for whatever came next.

Caleb also carried the river stone. Helen's stone, with its white line of lightning. He had wrapped it in a piece of cloth and tucked it into his breast pocket, close to his chest, and Finch suspected he would carry it there until they reached the other side, wherever and whenever that turned out to be. She wondered what Helen's stone would look like on the other side — whether it would still be grey, whether the white line would still run through it like a vein of lightning, or whether it would be something else entirely, a different stone, a different river, a different woman's luck. She wondered if luck worked across dimensions, or if it was as local and specific as everything else.

They said their final goodbyes on the steps of the farmhouse, in the grey light of early morning. Helen stood with her arms folded, her face set in the expression of someone who has already done her crying and is now doing the harder thing, which is standing upright while the people she loves walk away. Thomas lingered in the doorway, his hands in his pockets, his angular face unreadable. Lily stood on the porch with the *Hobbit* gone from her hands and a space in her arms that she was trying very hard not to acknowledge.

"Be safe," Helen said. "All of you."

"We will," Caleb said.

"No, you won't. You'll be reckless and foolish and brave, which is the same thing with better marketing. But come back anyway."

Finch stood at the window and looked out at the landscape she had come to know — the scrubland, the distant mountains, the grey sky that hung over Earth Two like a bruise. She had arrived here a stranger, lost and terrified and certain that she would die. She was leaving it something else. Not a survivor — she had always been a survivor. Something more. Someone who had looked into the darkest mirror she could find and seen not a monster but a person, and had chosen, against every instinct, to love what she saw.

"We're ready," she said.

Caleb nodded. Mara was silent in her chair, wrapped in the blanket, her face unreadable. The other Finch stood by the device, her hands at her sides, her eye fixed on the humming, glowing heart of the machine that would, if everything went according to plan, carry them away from this world and into the next.

It was time.

---

## Chapter 27: Mara's Choice

She waited until the last moment. She waited until the device was calibrated and the packs were loaded and the weapons were distributed and the goodbyes to Helen had been said and the only thing left was the act of leaving itself. She waited until Finch was standing at the threshold of the device, one foot on the platform, her hand reaching for the activation switch. And then she spoke.

"I'm staying."

The words fell into the lab like stones into still water. Finch turned. Caleb turned. The other Finch turned. And Mara sat in her chair with her hands folded in her lap and her eyes steady and her voice calm, and repeated the thing she had been carrying in her chest for three days, turning it over like a worry stone, polishing it smooth.

"I'm staying," she said again. "I'm going back to the Junkyard."

"Mara —" Caleb began.

"Don't." She held up a hand, and the authority in it — the old authority, the Mara authority, the voice that had once commanded a camp of survivors and kept children alive in a world that wanted them dead — silenced him mid-word. "Don't argue with me. Don't try to convince me. I've made my decision, and it's made, and it's not changing."

She shifted in her chair, wincing at the pain that still lived in her ribs and her spine and the places where Avarice had left its mark. But her eyes were clear, and her voice was steady, and when she spoke again, it was with the quiet, implacable certainty of someone who has walked through fire and come out the other side knowing exactly who she is.

"There are children at the Junkyard," she said. "Reef. Spike. Bo. Wren. Cass. They're alone. They've been alone since I was taken. They don't know if I'm alive or dead. They don't know if anyone is coming for them. And I am not going to leave them in that uncertainty. I am not going to step through a portal into another world while those children are waiting for me to come home."

She looked at Caleb, and the look was fierce and tender and uncompromising. "You have your daughter. You have each other. You have a world to go to, a home to return to. But this is my world, Caleb. This broken, ruined, impossible world. These are my children. This is my responsibility. And I am not running from it."

Caleb's face was a battleground of emotion. Finch could see the fight happening in real time — the desire to argue warring with the understanding that arguing would be futile, that Mara's mind was made and her will was iron and that the only respectful response was to accept what she had chosen.

"You're not well," he said, and the gentleness in his voice was almost worse than anger would have been.

"I'm well enough," Mara said. "I'll heal. Helen will help me. And the others — the Black Vultures, the stragglers, the people who scattered when Avarice fell — they'll come back. They always come back, when there's someone to come back to. I'll rebuild. It's what I do. It's what I've always done."

She turned to Finch, and the look on her face was the look that Finch had seen in the other Finch's eyes during the battle — the look of someone who has decided that the person in front of them will not be harmed, not while there is breath in their body. But it was more than that, too. It was the look of a mother. Of a teacher. Of a woman who has watched a girl grow into someone extraordinary and wants her to know it, needs her to know it, before the door closes and the worlds separate and the words can no longer be spoken.

"Finch," Mara said. "Come here."

Finch crossed the lab. She knelt beside Mara's chair, the way she had knelt beside her father's bed on the night before the world ended, the way she had knelt beside Liam's fire in the Junkyard, the way you kneel when something sacred is about to happen.

Mara reached into her pocket and produced a small, smooth, grey stone. Finch recognised it instantly. It was the stone that Wren had given her — the stone from the riverbank, the stone that had sat on her windowsill at the Junkyard, the stone she had left behind when she fled with Caleb and Mara into the mountains. She had thought it lost. She had grieved it, in the small, private way you grieve objects that carry the weight of memory.

"Helen found it," Mara said. "In the debris at the bridge collapse. She brought it to the farm with the other salvage. It's been sitting on her kitchen shelf for weeks, waiting for someone to claim it."

She placed the stone in Finch's palm and closed the girl's fingers around it. The stone was cool and smooth and fit perfectly in the curve of her hand, as though it had been made for her, as though it had been waiting for this moment.

"Wren will want it back," Mara said, and her voice cracked, just slightly, on the child's name. "When I find her. When I find all of them. I'll tell her I saw you. I'll tell her you're safe. I'll tell her you carried her stone across worlds."

Finch's eyes burned. She blinked, and the tears fell, and she didn't wipe them away because some griefs are too big for dignity.

"Mara," she said, and the name was a prayer and a thank-you and a goodbye all tangled together. "I —"

"I know," Mara said. "I know, sweetheart. I know." She reached out and took Finch's face in her hands — her thin, strong, scarred hands, the hands that had carried children through the apocalypse, the hands that had held Finch when she was small and lost and certain that the world was ending. "I'm proud of you," she said. "Do you hear me? I am so proud of you. You have become someone extraordinary. You have become someone your father should be proud of, and your mother should be proud of, and that girl —" she glanced at the other Finch, who stood very still by the device, her face pale and her eye bright, "— should be proud of. You are the best thing I have ever had the privilege of watching grow."

She leaned forward and pressed her lips to Finch's forehead, a kiss that was gentle and fierce and full of everything she had never been able to say in words. Then she pulled back, and her hands fell away, and her face was dry and her voice was steady and her eyes were the eyes of a woman who has made peace with the shape of her own story.

"Go," she said. "Go home. Go through the door. And when you get there, when you're standing in your world with your father beside you and the sun on your face, I want you to remember something. I want you to remember that the world is bigger than any one version of it. I want you to remember that the people you love are worth fighting for, even when the fighting seems impossible. And I want you to remember that there is a woman in a broken world who loves you, and who will rebuild, and who will be waiting if you ever find your way back."

Finch stood. She pressed the stone to her heart. She looked at Mara, and Mara looked at her, and between them passed everything — the years of searching, the grief, the discovery, the slow and painful work of learning to be a family in a world that had tried to tear them apart.

"Goodbye," Finch said.

"Goodbye, sweetheart," Mara said. "Take care of your father. He's a difficult man, but he's a good one. And take care of her, too." She glanced at the other Finch again, and this time the look on her face was complicated — love and grief and the particular, bittersweet ache of seeing a child you failed finally, finally finding her way home. "She's going to need you. More than she knows."

Finch nodded. She couldn't speak. She turned and walked to the device, and the other Finch looked at her, and in the other Finch's eye — that dark, deep, complicated eye — Finch saw something she had never expected to see.

Gratitude.

---

## Chapter 28: The Portal

The device activated with a sound like the world drawing breath.

Not the hum of the previous days, not the scream of the defensive pulse, but something deeper. Something foundational. A vibration that began in the base of the device and rose through its structure like sap through a tree, gathering speed and power and resonance until it filled the lab, filled the walls, filled Finch's chest and skull and the hollow spaces behind her eyes. It was a sound that she felt in her teeth, in the roots of her hair, in the fluid of her eyes. It was a sound that rewrote the air itself, replacing the familiar hum of the generator and the moan of the wind with something older and more fundamental — the sound of a machine remembering what it was built to do.

Finch watched the device come alive. She had seen it activated before — the defensive pulse, the test burst, the fraction-of-a-second glimpse of Earth One — but this was different. This was not a test. This was not a rehearsal. This was the real thing, the final thing, the thing that would tear a hole in the fabric of reality and hold it open long enough for three people to step through into the unknown.

She looked at Caleb. He was at the console, his hands moving across the controls with the practised ease of a man who had spent weeks learning every button, every dial, every calibration point. His face was set in the expression she knew best — the expression of concentration, of focus, of a mind that had narrowed itself to a single point and was processing information at a speed that his mouth couldn't match. His eyes moved across the readouts — power levels, dimensional frequencies, phase alignments — and his fingers adjusted, compensated, corrected, each movement precise and deliberate, the movements of a man who understood that in this moment, precision was the difference between survival and catastrophe.

The lights — the fluorescent strips that had buzzed and flickered through every hour of their time in the lab — went dark. All of them, simultaneously, as though someone had thrown a master switch. And in the darkness, the device began to glow.

It was not the harsh, clinical light of the pulse. It was something else entirely. A light that seemed to come from inside the device, from the spaces between its components, from the molecular gaps in the metal and the glass and the silicon. A light that was silver and gold and a colour that Finch had no name for — a colour that existed beyond the visible spectrum, that her eyes could see but her brain could not process, that left afterimages in her vision that pulsed and shifted like living things.

The air in the centre of the device began to change. It thickened, compressed, became visible — not as fog or mist or smoke, but as a distortion. A shimmering. A warping of light and space that reminded Finch of the way air looks above hot tarmac in summer, the way it bends and twists and refuses to hold still. But this was not heat. This was something far more fundamental. This was the fabric of reality itself beginning to fold.

A disc appeared. It hung in the air at the centre of the device, roughly a metre in diameter, and it was — Finch struggled for words, for comparisons, for any framework that might contain what she was seeing — it was like a window into somewhere else. Not a window in the traditional sense. Not a pane of glass or a frame of wood. A window in the way that a wound is a window: an opening in the surface of something, revealing what lies beneath.

The disc shimmered and pulsed. Through it, Finch could see — or thought she could see — shapes. Vague, indistinct, half-formed. Not a room. Not a landscape. Something else. Something that her brain kept trying to interpret and failing, the way a dreamer tries to read text in a dream and finds that the letters keep shifting and rearranging.

"It's working," Caleb said, and his voice was tight with awe and terror and the particular intensity of a man who has spent his life chasing an impossible dream and is now watching it materialise in front of him. "The portal is open. The dimensional breach is stable. The readings are —" He checked the monitor, frowned, checked again. "The readings are off."

"How off?" Finch asked.

"The calibrations specified Earth One. Our target. The home dimension. But the resonance signature coming through the portal doesn't match. Not exactly. There are deviations — subtle, but significant. The harmonic frequency is wrong. The spatial coordinates are wrong. The temporal signature is —" He paused. Swallowed. Looked at the portal with the expression of a man who has just realised that the road he's about to walk does not lead where he thought it did.

"Wrong," he said again. "It's not Earth One."

The lab was silent except for the thrum of the device and the distant moan of the portal. Finch looked at Caleb. Caleb looked at the portal. The other Finch looked at both of them, her eye moving between her father and her other self like a spectator at a tennis match.

"What's on the other side?" Finch asked.

"I don't know," Caleb said. "The device is drawing from a dimensional frequency that's adjacent to our target, but not identical to it. It's like — it's like tuning a radio and picking up a station one frequency off. The signal is clear. The signal is strong. But it's not the station you were looking for."

The other Finch took a step closer to the portal, her eye narrowing as she studied the shapes moving within it. She reached out — slowly, carefully, the way you reach for something that might burn — and held her hand above the disc. The light played across her scarred fingers, painting them in shifting colours that had no names.

"There's heat," she said. "Not much. But it's there. And there's — something else. A vibration. Like standing next to a generator." She pulled her hand back and flexed her fingers, as though the sensation had left a residue. "Whatever's on the other side, it's alive. I can feel it."

"Alive how?" Caleb asked.

"Alive as in — functioning. Active. Not dead, not abandoned. Whatever world that is, it's running. It's doing things." She paused. "That's either very good or very bad."

Caleb stared at the portal. The light played across his face, casting shadows that made him look older than he was, older than he had any right to be. "Not without shutting the device down. And once it's shut down, there's no guarantee I can restart it. The power cells are depleted. This is a one-shot deal, Finch. We go through now, or we don't go through at all."

The other Finch's jaw tightened. She looked at the portal — really looked at it, her single eye tracking the shapes that moved within the disc like fish in a luminous pond — and Finch saw the calculation happening behind her gaze. The tyrant was still in there, beneath the softening, beneath the tears, beneath the tentative trust they had built over days of shared work. The tyrant knew what it meant to weigh options and choose the least terrible one.

"What if it's worse?" the other Finch said. "What if we step through and it's — worse than here? Worse than Earth Two?"

"Then we deal with it," Caleb said. "The way we always deal with things. One step at a time."

"One step at a time," the other Finch repeated, and the words came out flat, stripped of hope, stripped of fear, reduced to their most basic function: the acknowledgement that the future was a country none of them had a map to.

Caleb moved to the console. His fingers hovered over the activation switch — a physical control, a heavy toggle set into the panel, its surface worn smooth by years of use. He looked at it the way a man looks at a door he knows he can't close once it's opened. His hand was steady. His jaw was set. But his eyes — those hard, blue, practical eyes that had seen the worst the world had to offer and survived — were bright with something that might have been terror and might have been hope and was probably both.

"Once I throw this switch," he said, more to himself than to anyone else, "the portal opens fully. The breach stabilises. We'll have —" He checked the monitor. "Maybe ninety seconds before the power cells burn out. Maybe less. We'll need to move fast."

"Ninety seconds," Finch said.

"Give or take."

"That's not a lot of time."

"It's all we've got."

The weight of the decision settled over the lab like a physical thing. Finch felt it on her shoulders, in her chest, in the pit of her stomach. Not Earth One. Not home. Something else. Somewhere else. A place they had never been, never planned for, never imagined.

But also: not Earth Two. Not the broken world. Not the world of Hollows and ruin and the ghosts of everything they had lost. Somewhere else. Somewhere new. Somewhere that might, despite the wrongness of the calibrations, despite the deviation in the readings, despite everything — be worth finding.

"We go through," Finch said.

Caleb looked at her. "You're sure?"

"No," Finch said. "But we go through anyway."

The other Finch nodded. She crossed the lab and stood beside Finch at the threshold of the device, and Finch felt the girl's presence beside her like a weight and a comfort and a promise.

"Together," the other Finch said. It was not a question.

"Together," Finch agreed.

They stood at the threshold and looked through the portal. The shapes within it had begun to resolve — slowly, painfully, the way a photograph develops in a darkroom, image emerging from chemical mist. Finch saw colour first. Not the grey and muted palette of Earth Two, but something vivid — a deep, saturated blue that could only be sky, and beneath it, green. Grass. Trees. Buildings, too, but buildings that were whole and intact and lit from within by warm light, the kind of light that came from windows and meant people were home.

"Is that —" she started.

"Earth One," Caleb said, and his voice was barely a whisper. "Or something like it. The spatial frequency is — close. Close enough. The atmosphere matches. The light spectrum matches. Whatever's on the other side, it's — it's a world that works. A world where things are intact."

The other Finch was staring at the portal with an expression Finch couldn't read. Not wonder, not fear — something older than both, something that lived in the space between longing and disbelief. She had never seen an intact world. She had never seen a city that wasn't ruined, a street that wasn't empty, a sky that wasn't grey. The green on the other side of the portal was the green of a world that had never ended, and it was the most beautiful thing she had ever seen.

"Is there —" the other Finch started, and then stopped. Her throat worked. Her eye was bright. "Is there a version of me there? On the other side? A version that didn't — that wasn't —"

She couldn't finish the sentence. She didn't need to. The question hung in the air like smoke: *Is there a version of me that got it right? A version that wasn't a monster?*

Caleb looked at her, and his face held something that Finch had never seen directed at the other Finch before — not suspicion, not wariness, not the guarded appraisal of a man measuring a threat, but compassion. Simple, uncomplicated compassion.

"I don't know," he said. "But we're going to find out."

"I can hear birds," the other Finch said, very quietly. And she could. Through the portal, carried on some invisible current, came the sound of birdsong — real birds, alive and singing, the sound of normalcy in a universe that had forgotten it.

The sound hit Finch like a wave. She closed her eyes and let it wash over her — the chirping, the trilling, the complex, layered music of creatures that had no idea that the world they lived in was a miracle, that the simple act of singing in a tree was a privilege that two of the three people standing at this portal had been denied for longer than they cared to remember. She thought of the birds at the farm — Helen's chickens, the wild parrots that sometimes visited the eucalyptus trees at the edge of the property — and she thought of the silence of Earth Two, where the only birds she had heard were the ones in her dreams.

"Listen to that," she whispered. "Just — listen."

They listened. For a long moment, nobody spoke. The three of them stood at the threshold of the device with the portal pulsing before them and the sound of birdsong drifting through the dimensional membrane like a gift, like a promise, like the first note of a song that they had been waiting their whole lives to hear. Caleb's hand tightened on Finch's. The other Finch's breathing was shallow, controlled, the breathing of someone who was trying very hard not to cry and was mostly succeeding.

Caleb checked the device one last time. The portal pulsed and shimmered, a disc of light hanging in the air like a second moon. Through it, the vague shapes continued to shift and dance, tantalising and unknowable. He took a breath. He took Finch's hand. He took the other Finch's hand. And the three of them — father and daughter and the daughter who was not a daughter but was something else, something new, something that had no name in any language — stood at the edge of the unknown and looked into the light.

The other Finch's hand was cold. Not the cold of fear — Finch had felt that cold before, in the prison cell, in the moments before the blade went in — but the cold of exertion, of a body that had been running on adrenaline and willpower for days and was now, at the threshold of the impossible, finally allowing itself to feel the weight of what it had endured. Her fingers were rough against Finch's, scarred and calloused, the hands of a girl who had built machines and broken bones in equal measure. But they held on. They held on with a grip that said, without words, *I am here. I am not letting go. Whatever happens on the other side of this light, we face it together.*

Caleb's hand was different. Warm and solid and steady, the hand of a man who had held a thousand steering wheels and a hundred wrenches and one daughter's face in the dark hours before the world ended. His grip was firm but not tight, the grip of someone who understood that holding on was not the same as holding back, that the people you loved were not possessions to be controlled but partners to be trusted. He squeezed Finch's hand once — a brief, private pressure that carried everything he couldn't say — and Finch squeezed back, and between them passed the whole silent language of a father and daughter who had been separated by dimensions and reunited by stubbornness and love.

"For Mara," Finch said.

"For Mara," the other Finch agreed.

"For home," Caleb said. "Wherever that turns out to be."

And they stepped through.

---

## Chapter 29: Through

There is no way to describe what dimensional travel feels like. Finch would try, later — would sit in the quiet moments of her new life and search for words, for metaphors, for frameworks that might contain the experience — and she would fail every time. Language is a tool designed for the world it was born in, and the space between dimensions is not a world. It is the absence of world. It is the gap between stories. It is the silence between notes.

The first sensation was light. Not light as Finch knew it — not the yellow-white of the sun or the blue-white of fluorescent tubes or the amber glow of the device. This was light stripped of colour, light before colour had been invented, light as a raw, undifferentiated force that pressed against her retinas and filled her skull and made her feel, for a single, terrifying moment, that she was made of light too. That her body had dissolved and she was nothing but photons, scattered across the void, meaningless and free.

The second sensation was sound. Or rather, the absence of sound, followed by a sound so vast and so deep that it bypassed her ears entirely and resonated in her bones, in her blood, in the fluid of her eyes. It was the sound of everything happening at once — the birth and death of stars, the folding of time, the slow, patient grinding of tectonic plates on a billion worlds. It was the sound of the universe clearing its throat.

The third sensation was the feeling of being unmade.

This is the closest she can come. Being unmade. As though every atom of her body had been separated from every other atom, and the atoms themselves had been separated from their meaning, and the meaning had been separated from the form, and she was left standing in the void with nothing but the memory of herself, the knowledge that she had once been a girl named Finch Adderwood, and even that knowledge was fading, dissolving, becoming less certain with every passing moment.

She held on. She held on to the memory. She held on to the name. She held on to the feel of her father's hand in her left hand and the other Finch's hand in her right, and she held on to those sensations like a drowning person holds to a piece of wreckage, and she said her name, silently, in the space where her voice should have been: *Finch. Finch. Finch.*

She felt — or imagined she felt — other presences in the void. Not people, not exactly, but *shapes*. Echoes of other travellers who had passed through this space before her, their passage still rippling through the dimensional fabric like the wake of a ship. She thought she saw faces — not her face, not Caleb's, not the other Finch's, but faces that were somehow familiar, as though she had seen them in dreams she couldn't remember. The void was not empty. It was full of the ghosts of every crossing, every traveller who had pushed between worlds, and the ghosts were watching her pass with expressions she couldn't read and wouldn't have understood.

Time did not exist here. Finch had no sense of how long the passage took — seconds, minutes, hours, years. The void operated on a clock that had no hands, that measured time in something other than seconds, in units that had no human name. She could have been falling for an instant or for an eternity, and the distinction would have meant nothing, because in this place, instant and eternity were the same thing, separated by nothing more than the direction you were looking.

She thought of Mara, left behind in Earth Two. She thought of Helen, standing on the farmhouse steps with her arms folded. She thought of Lily and Thomas and the children at the Junkyard — Reef, Spike, Bo, Wren, Cass — and she felt a grief so sharp and so sudden that it cut through the void like a blade. She was leaving them. All of them. Every person who had sheltered her, fed her, taught her, fought beside her. She was leaving them in a broken world, and she might never see them again, and the not-seeing was a wound that the void could not heal.

*Remember them*, she told herself. *Remember all of them. Carry them with you. They're part of you now, and wherever you land, they'll be there, inside you, in the shape of who you've become.*

And then there was something else — something that cut through the void like a blade through smoke. A flash of colour. Not the colourless light of the void, but actual colour: green, and blue, and the warm gold of sunlight on grass. It was there and gone, there and gone, flickering through the void like images through a broken projector, and Finch realised she was seeing the other side. Not clearly, not coherently, but in fragments, in shards, in pieces of a world that were pushing through the dimensional membrane like flowers through cracks in concrete.

She saw a tree. A real tree, alive and green, its leaves catching the light of a sun that was not the grey smear of Earth Two. She saw a building — not a ruin, not a shell, but a building that was intact and occupied, with windows that glowed with warm light and a door that was closed against the weather. She saw people — distant, indistinct, but real — moving through a street that was whole and paved and lined with things that looked, impossibly, like shops. The world on the other side of the portal was not just alive. It was *thriving*. It was a world where things had worked out, where the apocalypse had never come, where people lived and worked and argued and laughed and went about the small, precious business of being human in a world that let them.

The sight of it hit Finch like a physical blow. She gasped — or tried to gasp, but there was no air in the void to breathe, no lungs to breathe it with, only the memory of lungs, the ghost of breathing, the idea of air. The tears came then, hot and sudden and impossible to stop, streaming down her face in the zero-gravity of the space between worlds, floating away from her like tiny, luminous pearls.

She was going home. Or somewhere close to home. Somewhere that looked like home. And the looking was almost enough.

And then, as suddenly as it had begun, it ended.

The light collapsed. The sound compressed into a single, sharp note — a note that Finch felt in every cell of her body — and then released, expanding outward like a shockwave, and she was falling. Not falling through air, but falling through *space*, falling through the gap between one world and the next, and the falling was not down but in, a movement not of gravity but of dimensional pressure, as though the universe itself were pushing her through a keyhole and the keyhole was the portal and the portal was the disc of light and the disc of light was everywhere and nowhere and she was inside it, part of it, indistinguishable from it.

She felt the other Finch's hand tighten around hers. Felt Caleb's hand tighten on the other side. Felt the three of them connected, a chain of human warmth and human will, holding on to each other as the void tried to separate them, as the space between dimensions tried to scatter them like seeds in the wind.

And then she was out.

The sensation of emerging was the opposite of the sensation of entering. Not unmaking but remaking. Not dissolution but reconstruction. She felt herself come back together — atom by atom, cell by cell, the slow, patient assembly of a human body from the raw materials of the void. She felt her lungs fill with air that was not the air of Earth Two — thicker, richer, tinged with something metallic and sweet. She felt her feet find a surface that was not the concrete of the lab but something harder, smoother, vibrating with a frequency that hummed up through her legs and into her spine.

She opened her eyes.

The world slammed into her like a wave. Not the grey, muted world of Earth Two — this was something else entirely, something that her brain tried to process and failed, the way it fails when you step from a dark room into bright sunlight. For several seconds, Finch could see nothing but light — a vast, enveloping, impossible light that came from every direction at once — and then her vision adjusted, and the world resolved itself around her, detail by detail, like a photograph developing in slow motion.

She was standing on a rooftop. A flat, expansive surface of dark composite material that stretched in all directions, marked with painted lines and embedded with lights that pulsed in a slow, rhythmic pattern, like the heartbeat of a sleeping animal. The surface beneath her boots was warm — not the cold concrete of the Qwantum Lab, but a material that radiated a gentle, steady heat, as though it were alive, as though the building itself were breathing. Above her, the sky was not a sky. It was a canopy of light — not sunlight, not starlight, but something else, a diffuse, ambient glow that seemed to come from everywhere at once, as though the atmosphere itself were luminous. The colour was wrong. Too blue. Too vivid. Too saturated. It was the colour of a sky that had been designed rather than born.

She turned in a slow circle. The rooftop was vast — larger than a football field, its surface marked with geometric patterns that glowed faintly in the ambient light. At its edges, low walls of the same dark composite material rose to waist height, and beyond those walls, the city fell away in every direction, a cascade of light and structure and movement that made Finch's head spin. She could see the tops of buildings — not the ruined, skeletal buildings of Earth Two, but buildings that were whole and alive, their surfaces covered in lights and screens and the shifting, organic textures of materials she had no name for. She could see bridges — not bridges of steel and concrete, but bridges of light, of energy, of something that hung in the air between buildings like threads of spun glass. She could see movement — people, vehicles, things that were neither, all of them moving through the city with the purposeful, choreographed efficiency of a system that had been optimised beyond anything Finch had ever imagined.

The air hit her next. It was thick and clean and carried scents she had never encountered — a faint sweetness, like ozone after a storm, mixed with something organic and warm, like woodsmoke from a fire that burned clean fuel. There was a taste to it, too, a mineral sharpness that sat on her tongue and made her teeth ache. It was the taste of a world that had been manufactured rather than grown, a world where even the atmosphere was a product of design rather than accident.

Caleb stood beside her, his hand still gripping hers, his face white with shock and wonder. He released her hand slowly, as though unsure that the ground beneath his feet was real, and looked up at the sky with an expression that Finch had never seen on his face before. It was the expression of a man who had spent his life studying the impossible and had finally, finally been confronted with something that exceeded his capacity to categorise. His mouth worked silently. His fingers twitched at his sides, reaching for tools that weren't there, for instruments that could measure this, for frameworks that could contain it.

The other Finch stood on Finch's other side, her eye wide, her mouth slightly open, her face caught in an expression that Finch had never seen on it before and might never see again: pure, unguarded astonishment. The tyrant mask — the hard jaw, the narrowed eye, the permanent scowl of a woman who had learned to meet the world with hostility — was gone. In its place was the face of a girl who was seeing something beautiful for the first time in years. Her single eye moved across the sky, the light playing across the surface of her iris, and Finch saw tears in it — not the tears of grief or relief, but the tears of someone who had forgotten that the world could contain beauty and had just been reminded.

"Jesus Christ," the other Finch whispered. The words came out cracked and small, stripped of their usual edge, reduced to their most basic function: the expression of a human being overwhelmed by something she could not understand.

They stood in silence for a long moment, the three of them, breathing air that tasted like electricity and possibility, feeling a world that was not their own pressing in on them from every direction. The portal behind them pulsed once, twice, three times, and then collapsed with a sound like a sigh, and the disc of light vanished, and they were alone.

On a rooftop.

In a city.

A city unlike anything Finch had ever seen.

She looked to her left and saw towers — not buildings, *towers* — rising from the ground in sleek, impossible curves, their surfaces covered in lights that shifted and changed like the scales of a living creature. She looked to her right and saw more towers, and between them, suspended in the air like threads of light, structures that connected the buildings at heights that made her stomach lurch. She looked down and saw — nothing. The edge of the rooftop. A drop that fell away into a canyon of light and movement and the faint, distant sound of music she couldn't identify.

The other Finch was the first to speak. She stood at the edge of the rooftop with her hands gripping the railing, her knuckles white, her single eye fixed on the city below with an expression that Finch could not read. Not fear. Not wonder. Something else. Something that lived in the space between the two, in the territory where amazement meets dread and neither one wins.

"This isn't Earth One," the other Finch said. It was not a question.

"No," Caleb said. His voice was barely above a whisper. "No, it's not."

"Then where are we?"

Caleb looked up at the sky — the wrong sky, the designed sky, the luminous blue canopy that pulsed with light from sources Finch couldn't identify. He looked at the towers, at the lights, at the structures that hung in the air like miracles of engineering that had no name in any language he spoke. He looked at his hands — scarred, calloused, the hands of a truck driver and a mechanic and a man who had survived the end of the world — and then he looked at the city, and the city looked back at him with ten thousand eyes of light.

"I don't know," he said. "But whatever this is — wherever this is — we're here. And we're alive. And that's enough. For now, that's enough."

Finch stood beside them, the wind from the city rising around her, carrying with it the sounds and smells and vibrations of a world that was not her own. She felt the stone in her pocket — Helen's river stone, with its white line of lightning — and she held onto it, feeling its cool, smooth weight against her palm, and she thought: *This is where the story starts. Not at the beginning. Not at the end. Somewhere in the middle, the way all the best stories start. In the middle of everything, with nothing but the clothes on your back and the people beside you and the faint, stubborn hope that the next step will lead somewhere worth going.*

She looked at her father. She looked at her other self. And she said, quietly, to no one in particular, to the city and the sky and the vast, indifferent universe that had dropped them here:

"Okay."

It was not a word of surrender. It was not a word of defeat. It was the word you say when you've been thrown into the deep end and you've stopped flailing and started swimming. It was the word you say when the old life is gone and the new one hasn't begun and you're standing in the gap between them with nothing but your breath and your heartbeat and the people you love.

"Okay," she said again. "Let's see where we are."

---

## Chapter 30: Wrong Turn

It was not Earth One.

Finch knew this before she knew anything else, before she processed the sky or the air or the quality of the light. She knew it in her bones, in the way you know the temperature of a room before you check the thermostat, in the way you know the taste of water before it touches your tongue. This was not home. This was not the world she had been trying to reach. This was something else entirely.

She stood on the edge of the rooftop and looked out over the city, and the city looked back at her with ten thousand eyes of light.

It sprawled in every direction, vast and vertical and impossible, a forest of towers that rose from the ground like the fingers of a buried god reaching for a sky that was not a sky. The buildings were immense — not just tall but *massive*, their facades covered in screens and lights and moving images that shifted and changed with a fluidity that made them look alive. Holographic advertisements flickered in the air between the towers, their colours so vivid they hurt to look at: electric blue, neon pink, acid green, the deep, pulsing red of a heartbeat. The advertisements showed faces — beautiful, symmetrical, inhuman faces — and words in a language Finch could almost read but couldn't, the letters rearranging themselves before her eyes like a code she couldn't crack.

Between the towers, the streets were rivers of light. Vehicles — if they were vehicles, if the word even applied — moved through the air in precise, choreographed patterns, their headlights cutting through the perpetual twilight like searchlights. They were not cars. They were not planes. They were something in between: sleek, aerodynamic shapes that hovered and banked and accelerated with a grace that made the concept of wheels seem barbaric. They moved in lanes, Finch realised. Lanes in the sky. Traffic patterns in three dimensions. The infrastructure of a civilisation that had solved the problem of ground-level congestion by simply abandoning the ground.

Below the airborne traffic, at street level, the city pulsed with a different kind of energy. Finch could see it even from this height — the movement of people, the glow of storefronts, the flicker of screens and signs and holographic displays that turned the streets into canyons of light. The buildings at ground level were older than the towers, or at least designed to look older: facades of stone and brick and weathered metal that spoke of a different era, a different aesthetic, a time before the sky had been colonised and the air had become a highway. But even the older buildings were suffused with technology — screens embedded in brickwork, lights that responded to the movement of passersby, surfaces that shifted and changed colour with the slow, hypnotic rhythm of a living thing.

And the noise. Even from this height, even through the composite walls of the rooftop, Finch could hear it: the hum and roar of a city that never slept, never quieted, never stopped. Not the silence of Earth Two, not the howl of wind through empty buildings, but a constant, omnipresent sound that was part music and part machinery and part something else — a frequency that existed below the threshold of hearing, a vibration that she felt in her teeth and her bones and the fluid of her eyes.

"What is this place?" Caleb whispered. His voice was small against the scale of the city, a human sound in an inhuman landscape. "Where are we?"

The other Finch didn't answer. She stood at the edge of the rooftop with her eye wide and her hands gripping the railing, and she stared out at the city with an expression that Finch could not read. Not fear. Not wonder. Something else. Something that lived in the space between the two, in the territory where amazement meets dread and neither one wins.

Finch looked up. The sky — the wrong sky, the designed sky, the luminous blue canopy — was crisscrossed with the trails of airborne vehicles, their lights leaving fading streaks across the firmament like the brushstrokes of an invisible painter. And beyond the trails, beyond the light pollution and the haze, she could see stars. But they were wrong too. The constellations were unfamiliar. The patterns were all rearranged. The sky was a sky she had never seen, a sky that belonged to a world that had been shaped by different hands, different choices, different histories.

"Earth Three," Caleb said, and his voice had the flat, measured quality of a man who has spent his life studying the impossible and is now standing in the middle of it. "The calibrations. The deviations in the readings. They weren't errors. They were — they were pointing to a different dimension. A third dimension. A world that exists parallel to ours but follows a different developmental trajectory. Where Earth One remained — relatively — unchanged. Where Earth Two was destroyed by the Hollow virus. Here, here something else happened. Something different. Something that led to this."

He gestured at the city, at the towers, at the airborne vehicles, at the holographic advertisements, at the vast, glittering, terrifying expanse of a civilisation that had leapt so far ahead of Finch's own world that the gap between them was not merely technological but conceptual. This was not a world that had evolved differently. This was a world that had evolved *faster*, driven by different pressures, different discoveries, different inventions, along a path that had led it to a place Finch could not have imagined even in her most elaborate dreams.

"We're on a rooftop," Finch said, because it was the only concrete thing she could hold on to. "We're on a rooftop in a city we don't know, in a world we don't understand, with no way back." She looked at Caleb. "The portal is gone."

"I know."

"Can you rebuild it?"

Caleb looked at her. His face was drawn, tired, older than it had been an hour ago. But his eyes — those hard, blue, practical eyes — were clear. "Not here. Not now. Maybe not ever, without the original device. But —" He paused. Straightened. Squared his shoulders in the way that Finch recognised, the way he stood when he was about to do something difficult, the way he had stood on the morning he left for the lab that would change the world. "We're alive. We're together. And we're in a world that has technology beyond anything we've ever seen. If there's a way to get home, we'll find it. We always do."

The other Finch was still silent. She stood at the railing, her knuckles white, her eye fixed on the city. And then she spoke, and her voice was quiet and strange and full of something that might have been wonder and might have been grief and was probably both.

"It's beautiful," she said.

And it was. Despite everything — despite the wrongness, despite the fear, despite the vast, overwhelming alien-ness of a world that operated on principles Finch could not fathom — it was beautiful. The light, the movement, the sheer, audacious scale of human ambition made visible in steel and glass and light. It was the most beautiful thing Finch had ever seen, and the most terrifying, and the two qualities were inseparable, two sides of the same coin, two faces of the same impossible truth.

They stood on the rooftop — three strangers in a strange land, a father and a daughter and the daughter who was not a daughter — and they looked out over the city, and the city looked back at them with its ten thousand eyes of light, and the future — unknown, unknowable, shimmering like a portal that had already closed — waited for them in the neon dark.

---

*End of Part One: The World That Wasn't*

---

**CHAPTER 31: "Descent"**

The portal was gone. Not fading, not dimming — gone, as though it had never existed at all. One moment there had been a shimmer of displaced air behind them, a disc-shaped ghost humming with the memory of the device that had opened it. The next, there was only a patch of rooftop concrete, slightly warmer than the surrounding surface, and the faint smell of ozone dissolving into the night air. Finch pressed her hand against the spot where the portal had been. The concrete was smooth beneath her palm, unremarkable. She could feel nothing — no vibration, no residual energy, no whisper of the passage between worlds. The door had closed, and the key was eight thousand miles away in another dimension.

"Can you open it again?" she asked, though she already knew the answer.

Caleb was already on his knees, examining the ground with the focused intensity he brought to everything — the same intensity that had once driven him to spend eleven hours calibrating the disc in the lab, the same focus that had seen them through bridge collapses and Hollow attacks and the tyrant's fortress. He ran his fingers along the concrete, pressed his ear to the ground, closed his eyes. When he opened them, his face held the particular expression Finch had learned to dread: the one that meant the answer was no, and the one that meant he wished it were otherwise.

"The device on the other end is gone," he said. "Disconnected, destroyed, or simply shut off. I can't tell from here. But the connection is severed. We're not going back through there."

The tyrant Finch — the other Finch, the one with the eye patch and the scar tissue and the muscle-carved hands — said nothing. She stood at the rooftop's edge, looking down at the city below, and Finch couldn't tell if the expression on her face was fear or fascination or some mixture of both that had no name. The wind caught the tyrant's hair, shorter than Finch's and darker, without the lavender, and blew it across the patch that covered her ruined eye.

"Then we're stuck," the tyrant said. Not a question.

"For now," Caleb said, and the way he said it — the careful, measured way, as though the words themselves were a bridge that might collapse — told Finch that he had no idea how to make the "for now" into "for a little while" and eventually into "not at all."

Finch stood up. The rooftop was large, a flat expanse of grey concrete bordered by low walls and the rusted remnants of what might have been antenna arrays. They were on the top of a building that was clearly tall — Finch could feel the height in her inner ear, in the way the air pressure sat differently on her skin — but from up here, surrounded by the city's skyline, their building seemed almost modest. Other structures rose around them in every direction, their facades lit from within or without, casting the night in shades of blue and violet and green. Some were so tall that their upper floors disappeared into the haze of light pollution. Others pulsed and shifted, their surfaces moving as though they were alive, their walls rippling like the surface of a pond disturbed by a stone.

"We need to get off this roof," Finch said.

She didn't say it because it was an insight. She said it because nobody else was saying it, and because standing on a rooftop in a world they didn't understand, with no way back, felt like the kind of thing that could become permanent if you let it.

They found the stairwell door on the far side of the roof — a rusted metal thing with a handle that turned only after Caleb put his shoulder into it. The stairwell was dark. Not the dark of a power outage or a broken bulb, but the dark of abandonment, of disuse, of a space that had been forgotten by the people who maintained the building. Dust lay on the steps in thick, undisturbed layers. Finch's boots left prints in it, as did the tyrant's heavier ones, and Caleb's in between. They descended twelve flights, the sound of their footsteps echoing off concrete walls that were stained with moisture and age. The building was old — not old the way buildings in Earth Two were old, scarred by apocalypse and neglect, but old the way things got when they'd been built to last and then simply outlived their purpose. The architecture was strange, angular, with doorways that were slightly too tall and corridors that turned at angles that didn't feel entirely right. Finch had the sense, as they descended, that the building had been designed for people who were not quite the same shape as the three of them.

On the sixth floor, they found offices — open-plan spaces with desks arranged in rows, the desks covered in a fine layer of grey dust. Screens sat on every desk, dark and blank, their surfaces curved and seamless, without the buttons or ports Finch expected. Caleb touched one, and it flickered — a brief flash of light, a momentary glow, then darkness again. He pulled his hand back as though burned, though Finch saw no mark.

"Dead," he said. "Or sleeping. I'm not sure which."

They kept descending. The dust thickened on the lower floors, and the air grew stale, carrying the particular smell of enclosed spaces that hadn't been ventilated in years — a dry, mineral smell, like chalk, with an undercurrent of something chemical that Finch couldn't identify. The stairwell walls were covered in places with marks that might have been graffiti, though the symbols were unlike any writing Finch had seen. They reached the ground floor, and Caleb pushed through another door — this one newer, its surface a smooth, dark material that gave way more easily — and they emerged into a lobby.

The lobby was large, with high ceilings and a floor of polished stone that was cracked in several places. Through the building's front entrance — a broad archway where glass doors had once hung — Finch could see the street beyond. The doors were gone, leaving only the metal tracks they'd slid along, and the archway yawned open to the night like a mouth.

They stepped through.

The street was empty. Not empty the way a street was empty at three in the morning in a town where everyone was asleep, but empty in a way that suggested this was a place people passed through rather than inhabited. The buildings on either side were tall and narrow, their surfaces a patchwork of dark metal and glass, many of them dark, their windows showing nothing. The street itself was wide — wide enough for vehicles, though Finch saw none — and paved with a smooth, dark material that absorbed the light rather than reflecting it. Overhead, the sky was invisible, hidden behind the canopy of the city: a lattice of bridges and walkways and suspended structures that connected the upper floors of the buildings, creating a second street level far above their heads. Between these structures, the neon light of the city filtered down in shafts and beams, painting everything in shades of blue and magenta.

Finch breathed in. The air tasted of metal and ozone and something sweet, something almost floral, but wrong — the way a flower might smell if it were made of electronics. There was a sound beneath everything, a low, constant hum that Finch felt as much as heard, the vibration of it coming up through the soles of her boots and into her bones. It was the sound of a city that was alive, that was functioning, that was doing a thousand things she couldn't see and couldn't understand.

"There," Caleb said, pointing down the street to their left. The buildings there were shorter, the light less intense, and beyond them Finch could see what might have been open space — an absence of structures that suggested the edge of this district, a place where the city thinned out.

They walked. The street was long, and their footsteps made sounds that felt too loud in the silence — the scuff of boots on smooth pavement, the rustle of clothing, the quiet catch of breath. Finch found herself walking close to the tyrant, though she wasn't sure why. Perhaps it was because they moved the same way — the same stride length, the same unconscious rhythm, the same habit of scanning left and right with every few steps. Perhaps it was something else. The tyrant walked with her hand resting on the handle of a knife that Finch knew she no longer had — a habitual gesture, a muscle memory from a world where danger was always close enough to touch. Without the knife, the gesture looked vulnerable. Finch found that she didn't want to think about what vulnerability meant on the face of someone who looked like her.

They passed through the district Caleb had pointed toward, and the buildings did indeed grow shorter, the streets narrower, the light dimmer. The technology was still present — Finch could see the hum of it in the walls, the glow of conduits buried in the pavement, the occasional flicker of a screen in a window — but it felt less maintained here, less attended to. Some of the buildings showed signs of damage: cracked facades, shattered windows that had been patched with dark material, doors that hung open on broken hinges. This was the edge, Finch thought. The place where the city's attention had wandered, where its systems had moved on to other priorities. It was not abandoned — she could see lights in some windows, hear the murmur of voices from behind walls — but it was neglected, a place that existed in the shadow of the city's grander ambitions.

They emerged onto a broader street at the district's edge, and here Finch stopped. Before them, the ground dropped away — a gentle slope that led down toward a vast, open space. And beyond that space, the city. The real city. The one they'd seen from the rooftop but hadn't truly comprehended, because from the rooftop it had been abstract, a pattern of lights and shapes. But from here, from the ground, with the full scope of it spread out before them, Finch could see what Neo-Sydney actually was.

It was enormous. It was a city that had swallowed the horizon, that had consumed the landscape and replaced it with itself. Buildings of every size and shape rose from the ground in every direction, some slender and crystalline, some broad and organic, some so tall they seemed to lean against the sky. The light was everywhere — not just in the windows and the signs but in the air itself, a luminous haze that made the night feel like a perpetually overcast day. Vehicles moved through the air between the buildings, silent and swift, their lights tracing bright lines across the darkness. And beneath it all, beneath the towers and the vehicles and the light, the hum — that constant, living vibration, the sound of a civilization operating at a level Finch's mind could not fully grasp.

"Christ," the tyrant said, very quietly.

It was the first time Finch had heard her use a word like that without anger in it.

Caleb was already moving down the slope, drawn toward the city the way a moth was drawn toward light — not with the mindless compulsion of instinct but with the deliberate, focused hunger of a scientist who had just been shown a mystery worthy of his attention. Finch followed, and the tyrant followed Finch, and the three of them walked down the slope and into the streets of Neo-Sydney, into the neon and the hum and the vast, terrifying beauty of a world they did not belong to.

The street at the bottom of the slope was different from the ones they'd been walking on. It was wider, smoother, and lit from within — the pavement itself glowed with a soft, blue-white light that seemed to pulse in time with the hum that permeated everything. Along either side of the street, the buildings were shorter here than in the district behind them, but more intact, their surfaces clean and dark, their windows lit with the cold, even glow of screens. There were people on the street — not many, but some — and Finch's first thought was that they were wrong. Not wrong in any way she could immediately articulate, but wrong the way a word was wrong when it was misspelled — the same components, arranged differently, creating something that was almost familiar but not quite.

They walked past a woman who was leaning against a wall, her eyes closed, a thin line of light tracing the curve of her ear and disappearing into her hair. She didn't open her eyes as they passed, but Finch saw her mouth move, and the air in front of her face shimmered for a moment, as though she were speaking to someone who wasn't there. They passed a man sitting on a low wall, his hands moving in the air above a surface that Finch couldn't see — gestures, deliberate and practiced, that produced no visible effect but which clearly meant something to the man making them. They passed a pair of teenagers walking side by side, their faces lit from below by the glow of something in their palms, their eyes focused on a point in midair that existed for them and for no one else.

The city was not asleep. The city was awake, and it was doing things, and most of those things were invisible to three people who had arrived from other worlds and other times. Finch pulled the collar of her jacket up — Caleb's jacket, the one he'd given her, too large across the shoulders — and walked faster, and the hum of the city rose around them like a tide.

---

**CHAPTER 32: "Neo-Sydney"**
