They learned the name from a sign. It was mounted on the side of a building at the intersection of two broad streets, and it was not a sign in any sense Finch understood — it was a projection, a three-dimensional image suspended in the air by some mechanism she couldn't see, its letters formed from light that shifted and shimmered like the surface of water. "WELCOME TO NEO-SYDNEY," it read, and below that, in smaller letters, "EST. 2089 — YEAR 2147 — THE GRID PRESERVES." The sign rotated slowly, its light casting soft shadows on the street below, and as they stood before it, the letters dissolved and reformed into a new message — this one advertising something called "NeuroLink v.12: Seamless Integration, Infinite Potential" — and then dissolved again, cycling through messages with the patient, ceaseless efficiency of a machine that had no concept of boredom.

2147. The year hit Finch like a physical thing, a weight settling into her chest. She was sixteen years old, and she had been born in 2009, and the year 2147 was not a year she was supposed to experience. It was a year that belonged to other people, to descendants and ancestors, to a future that had been theoretical when she was born and which was now, impossibly, the present tense.

Caleb, to his credit, adapted faster. He always had. The man who had once spent a weekend rebuilding a carburetor with parts from three different vehicles could look at a world he didn't understand and see not chaos but systems, not alienness but patterns that simply hadn't been explained yet. He was already studying the street with the focused, cataloguing gaze that Finch knew so well — noting the shape of the buildings, the materials of their construction, the way the light moved through the air. His fingers were twitching, the way they always did when he was thinking. Finch could almost hear the questions forming behind his eyes.

"The buildings," he said, as they walked. "They're adaptive. See how the facade of that one" — he pointed to a structure to their left, a broad, curving building whose surface was covered in what looked like overlapping scales — "is shifting? Slowly, but you can see it. The scales are realigning. It's responding to the temperature, or the light, or both. Self-regulating architecture. I read a paper about the theory once. I never thought I'd see it."

The tyrant Finch said nothing. She walked with her head down, her shoulders hunched, and Finch recognized the posture — it was the same one she'd worn in the first hours after arriving in Earth Two, when everything was too much and the only way to survive it was to look at the ground and focus on the next step and nothing else. The tyrant was overwhelmed. Finch could see it in the tension of her jaw, in the way her hands kept moving to the handle of a knife she no longer carried, in the way her single eye darted from one sight to another with the rapid, unfocused alertness of an animal that had been dropped into a forest it had never seen before.

They walked deeper into the city. The streets grew wider and the buildings grew taller and the technology grew more dense, more pervasive, more impossible. Finch saw things that her mind categorized and then immediately rejected:

Vehicles that moved without wheels, hovering above the street on pillars of compressed air or magnetic force or something else entirely, their bodies sleek and silent, their surfaces alive with the same shifting light that covered the buildings. They moved in orderly streams, flowing through the air at different heights, obeying some traffic logic that was invisible to Finch but which kept them from colliding with each other and with the buildings they wove between.

Advertisements that floated in the air — not on screens or billboards but in the air itself, three-dimensional images that hung before Finch like ghosts. A woman's face, enormous and perfect, promoting something called "YouthLock: Because Time Is a Choice." A bottle of liquid that glowed from within, its label reading "PureWater™ — As Nature Intended." A cascade of images showing a family — father, mother, two children, a dog — standing in a garden that was too green, too perfect, too idyllic to be real, and above them the words "HomeNet: The Future Is Comfortable." The advertisements appeared and disappeared, phasing in and out of existence with a smoothness that made Finch's eyes ache.

Buildings that seemed to breathe. This was Caleb's observation, and Finch hadn't believed him until she saw it for herself — a structure three blocks from the sign, a tower of dark glass and pale metal that rose perhaps twenty stories, its surface expanding and contracting in a slow, rhythmic motion that was unmistakably organic. It was breathing. The building was breathing. Finch could see the movement — a subtle widening of the facade, a gentle exhalation of warm air from vents near its base — and she could feel it, a faint pulse in the air that was separate from the city's constant hum. It was the most unsettling thing she had ever seen, and she had seen Hollows.

"People," Caleb said, and Finch looked.

There were more of them now, as they moved toward the city's center. And Caleb was right — they were different. The people Finch had seen on the outskirts had been strange, their behavior marked by the quiet, distracted quality of people engaged with things she couldn't see. But the people here, in the brighter, busier streets, were different in a more fundamental way. They were different in their bodies.

Many of them had cybernetic enhancements. Not all — some appeared entirely organic, their bodies unremarkable in shape and form — but many. Finch saw a woman with a left arm that was not an arm at all but a sleek, dark prosthetic that moved with a fluid grace no biological limb could match, its surface catching the light and throwing it back in patterns that were almost beautiful. She saw a man with eyes that glowed — not metaphorically but literally, a soft amber light emanating from irises that were clearly not natural, that tracked Finch's movement as she passed with an attentiveness that made her skin crawl. She saw a child — no older than eight or nine — with neural interface ports visible at his temples, small metallic discs that sat against his skin like technological moles, and the child was staring at a point in midair with the rapt attention of someone watching a film, though nothing was visible to Finch.

Others had subtler modifications — a ridge of metal along a jawline, a patch of skin that was a different texture or color than the surrounding tissue, a ear that was too symmetrical, too precise, too clearly designed rather than grown. The modifications were not uniform; they varied in style and extent, from the almost invisible to the dramatically obvious, and Finch had the sense that they were as much a form of expression as clothing — that people chose them, that they meant something, that they were part of how this world's people communicated who they were and what they valued.

And then there were the neural interfaces. Finch saw them everywhere once Caleb pointed them out — thin, nearly invisible lines that traced the skin from the temples to somewhere behind the ears, disappearing into the hair or ending at small, flush-mounted ports. Some people wore visible headsets — delicate, wire-like structures that arced over the ears and terminated at the temples — but most seemed to have their interfaces embedded, invisible from a distance but visible up close as a faint metallic sheen on the skin. These people moved differently from the unenhanced. They moved with a distracted precision, their bodies navigating the physical world with the automatic, unconsidered grace of people whose minds were simultaneously processing two realities — the physical one and whatever the interface was showing them.

Finch looked down at herself. Caleb's jacket, too big across the shoulders, the cuffs rolled up to her wrists. Her own jeans, torn at the knees from the journey through Earth Two, stained with mud and blood and things she didn't want to identify. Her boots, heavy and practical, made for walking on dirt and rubble, entirely wrong for a city of smooth pavement and glowing light. Her hands, bare and unmodified, without ports or prosthetics or any of the subtle markers that the people of Neo-Sydney wore like second skin.

She was from the past. She was from another world. She was, by every metric this city used to classify its inhabitants, nothing.

The tyrant caught up to her. They were walking side by side now, close enough that their shoulders almost touched, and Finch could feel the tension radiating from her other self like heat from a fire. The tyrant's single eye was wide, and her jaw was set hard, and her breathing was controlled in the particular way that Finch recognized as the prelude to either violence or panic. The tyrant was holding herself together through sheer force of will, and the effort was visible.

"I don't like this," the tyrant said.

"Nobody asked you to like it," Finch said, and immediately wished she hadn't, because the tyrant's head turned toward her and for a moment there was something in her eye — something that was not anger but something close to it, something that said *I could break you and we both know it* — and then it was gone, replaced by something softer, something that might have been fear.

"Yeah," the tyrant said. "Okay."

They walked on. The city pulsed around them. The advertisements floated. The buildings breathed. The people moved through the streets with the easy, distracted confidence of a species that had mastered its environment and remade it in its own image, and Finch, walking among them with her torn jeans and her lavender hair and her empty, unaugmented hands, felt for the first time what it was like to be a ghost — to exist in a place where you were not seen, not registered, not part of the system that kept everything running.

The lavender hair. Finch reached up and touched it, running her fingers through the pale strands. In this city, where the people she passed had chrome and circuitry and light woven into their very bodies, her hair — her one concession to vanity, her one act of rebellion against her mother's disapproval — was the most natural thing about her. It was real. It was grown, not designed. It was dyed with chemicals from a drugstore in a world that no longer existed for her, and it was the only thing she had that belonged to this century as much as any other.

She let her hand fall. The city hummed. The night deepened, though it never grew truly dark — the light was constant, a luminous fog that hung over everything, turning the sky into a flat, glowing ceiling of pale blue and white. Somewhere above them, beyond the canopy of suspended streets and walkways, stars might have existed, but Finch doubted she would ever see them.

"We need to find somewhere to stop," Caleb said. He had that look again — the one that meant he was cataloguing, analysing, processing — but beneath it, Finch could see the exhaustion. They had been walking for hours, since before the portal closed, and the strangeness of this world was no substitute for rest. "Somewhere safe. Somewhere we can think."

"Safe," the tyrant said, and the word came out with a bitter edge, the way words always did when she encountered a concept she wasn't sure applied to her. "Yeah. Let's find somewhere safe. That'll be easy."

But Finch, looking at the empty streets ahead of them — the neglected district, the dark buildings, the places where the city's attention had moved on — thought that the tyrant might be wrong about that. Not about the difficulty, but about the possibility. Because there was something about the edge of a city, any city, in any world. There was something about the places where attention went to die. And that something, Finch thought, looking at the dark streets and the quiet buildings and the soft, blue glow of the pavement beneath her boots, was opportunity.

"We keep walking," Finch said. "We find the edge. We find the place where nobody's looking. And we figure it out from there."

Caleb nodded. The tyrant said nothing, but she fell into step beside Finch, and the three of them walked into the neon dark, three ghosts in a city of light.

---

**CHAPTER 33: "Attention"**

The drone found them at dawn. Or what passed for dawn in Neo-Sydney — a gradual brightening of the ambient glow, a shift in the quality of the light from the flat, even blue of the city's perpetual night to a warmer, more golden hue that suggested the sun was somewhere above the cloud of light pollution and technology that obscured the sky. They had found shelter in a disused building on the city's eastern edge — a structure that might have been a warehouse or a factory in another life, its interior a vast, empty space with concrete floors and walls lined with the skeletal remnants of machinery. They had slept in shifts, Caleb first, then the tyrant, then Finch, each of them taking a few hours on a bed of jackets and dust while the other two kept watch. It was the rhythm of survival, the one they had learned in Earth Two, and it came back to them now as naturally as breathing — the automatic, unconsidered coordination of people who had learned to trust each other with their lives.

The drone was silent. That was the worst thing about it — the complete absence of sound. One moment the street outside their shelter was empty, lit by the blue glow of the pavement and the distant shimmer of the city's towers. The next, something was there — a dark shape, roughly the size of a large bird, hovering at the level of the building's broken windows with a smoothness that suggested technology rather than biology. It was a disc, Finch saw, or close to one — a flattened ovoid of dark metal, featureless except for a single, circular lens at its center that glowed with a faint, red light. It moved without vibration, without the mechanical drone of rotors or engines, simply occupying the air through some principle Finch didn't understand.

It hovered for a moment, its lens sweeping the street in a slow arc. Then it turned, and the lens found the building where they were sheltering, and the red light brightened.

"Move," Caleb said, already on his feet, already reaching for the bags they'd gathered — not because they had much but because the act of carrying something felt like agency, felt like purpose, felt like the opposite of helplessness. "Now. Out the back."

They ran. The drone didn't follow immediately — it simply hovered there, its lens fixed on the building, its red light pulsing in a steady rhythm that might have been scanning or recording or both. By the time they reached the back of the building and slipped through a gap in the wall, the drone had risen higher, gaining altitude, and Finch could see it now from the corner of her eye — a dark speck against the glowing sky, moving in their direction with a patient, mechanical deliberateness that was more frightening than speed.

They emerged into an alleyway — narrow, dark, lined with walls that were covered in the strange graffiti they'd seen earlier, symbols that glowed faintly in the dim light. The tyrant took the lead without being told, moving with the silent, ground-covering stride of someone who had spent years navigating the dangerous spaces of Earth Two, and Finch followed, and Caleb followed Finch, and behind them the drone's red light cast a small, perfect circle of illumination on the alley wall, tracking them like an eye.

"Is it following us?" Finch asked, though she could see that it was.

"Registered," Caleb said, his voice tight. "We're unregistered. The system flagged us. If it scans us, it'll know we're not — we don't exist here. We're not in any database."

"What happens then?" the tyrant asked. She was running now, or something close to it — not sprinting but moving with a fast, efficient gait that ate up the alleyway's length, her body low, her arms pumping.

"I don't know," Caleb said. "Nothing good."

They turned a corner. The alleyway opened onto a broader street — still neglected, still dark, but wider, with more room to move. They crossed it at a run, their footsteps echoing off the buildings, and the drone followed, maintaining its altitude, its lens fixed on them with an unwavering attention that felt, to Finch, almost personal — as though the machine had decided that they were interesting and was not going to let them go.

The tyrant stopped. Finch almost collided with her.

"Go," the tyrant said. Her voice was different now — lower, harder, the voice she had used when she was the tyrant, when she was the leader of Avarice, when she was the most dangerous person in any room. "Both of you. Go."

"No," Finch said.

"Finch—"

"No. We're not splitting up. We're not doing that. We move together or we don't move at all."

The tyrant looked at her. For a moment, Finch saw something cross her face that she couldn't name — surprise, perhaps, or recognition, or the particular expression of someone who has been told something they already knew but had forgotten. Then the tyrant nodded, once, and turned, and they ran together, the three of them, down the street and into another alley and through a door that was standing open and into a darkness that swallowed them whole.

The door led to a stairwell. The stairwell led down. They descended, the drone's red light disappearing behind them as the door swung shut, and the darkness closed in — a thick, complete darkness, the kind that existed only in places deep underground, far from windows and light and the city's constant glow. Finch reached out and found the wall, and the wall was cold and smooth, and her hand found a railing — metal, rusted — and she followed it down, one step at a time, with Caleb behind her and the tyrant ahead.

They descended for a long time. The air grew colder and damper, and the concrete walls gave way to something rougher — stone, perhaps, or older concrete, the kind that had been poured decades ago and left to cure in the dark. The hum of the city faded, replaced by silence, and the silence was so complete that Finch could hear her own heartbeat, the rush of blood in her ears, the soft catch of breath that was her own and not her own.

The tyrant stopped. "Here," she said, and her voice was very quiet, barely a whisper. "This looks like a station."

Finch felt her way forward, and her hand found an opening — a doorway, wide and tall, leading into a larger space. She stepped through, and the darkness softened, just slightly, as her eyes adjusted. She could see shapes now — the long, low curve of a platform, the parallel lines of tracks, the dark mouths of tunnels leading off in both directions. It was a subway station. An old one, abandoned, its platforms cracked and stained, its walls bare of the light and technology that characterized everything Finch had seen in Neo-Sydney. This was a relic, a remnant of a previous era, a place that the city had built over and forgotten.

The tyrant was already on the platform, moving along it with the careful, deliberate steps of someone who knew that the difference between safety and danger was often a single misplaced foot. Caleb came to stand beside Finch, and they watched the tyrant move, and the silence of the station pressed in around them like a physical thing.

"The drone," Finch said. "It saw us. It recorded us."

"Maybe," Caleb said. "Maybe not. Maybe the door was enough. Maybe it can't follow underground."

"Maybe."

They stood in the dark, in the silence, in the belly of a city that didn't know they existed, and Finch felt something she hadn't felt since she'd woken up on a highway in Earth Two — a deep, bone-level exhaustion that had nothing to do with sleep and everything to do with the sheer effort of being alive in a world that was not designed for her. She slid down the wall and sat on the cold concrete of the platform, and the tyrant sat beside her, close enough that their shoulders touched, and Caleb sat on Finch's other side, and the three of them sat together in the dark and listened to the silence.

"We can't keep running," Finch said.

"We can't keep sitting here either," the tyrant said, but there was no force in it, no edge. She was tired too. They all were.

"No," Finch said. "But for now — for right now — this is enough."

And it was. The station was cold and dark and abandoned, and somewhere above them a drone was searching for people who didn't exist, and the city hummed its relentless, alien hum, but here, on this cracked platform, in this forgotten place, Finch felt something that was almost safety. Not safety itself, but its shadow. Its memory. The ghost of what safety might feel like, if they could find it.

She closed her eyes. The darkness behind her lids was the same as the darkness around her, and for a moment — just a moment — she let herself stop. She let herself be nothing but a body, sitting on a platform, in the dark, with her father on one side and the other version of herself on the other. She let herself be tired. She let herself be small.

And then, from somewhere in the station — from somewhere in the dark, beyond the reach of what little light their eyes had managed to gather — a voice.

"You're displaced," it said. "You're displaced, and you're loud, and you're going to get us all caught."

The tyrant was on her feet instantly, her body between Finch and the voice, her hands up and ready, her single eye fixed on the darkness ahead. Finch stood more slowly, her heart hammering, and Caleb stood too, and they all looked into the dark, and the dark looked back.

A light appeared. Small, warm, held in a hand that emerged from the shadows at the far end of the platform — a hand attached to an arm attached to a person who was young, perhaps twenty, with dark hair cut close to the scalp and eyes that caught the light in a way that was not entirely natural. The left eye, Finch saw, as the person stepped forward, was different from the right. It was cybernetic — a smooth, dark lens that sat in the eye socket where a natural eye would have been, its surface catching the light and throwing it back in patterns that shifted and flowed like water. But the right eye was human, and in it Finch saw something she recognized — the particular quality of attention that belonged to someone who had learned to survive by noticing everything and trusting very little.

"My name is Nyx," the person said. "And if you don't come with me now, you're going to wish you'd stayed wherever you came from."

---

**CHAPTER 34: "The Displaced"**

Nyx led them deeper into the station. Not along the platform, but through a gap in the wall where concrete had crumbled away to reveal a maintenance tunnel — narrow, low-ceilinged, its walls lined with conduits and pipes that were dark and cold to the touch. Nyx moved through it with the practiced ease of someone who had done this many times, her small light held ahead of her, casting their shadows on the walls behind them. The tyrant followed close behind, her hand on the wall, her eye fixed on Nyx's back with an intensity that was not quite hostile but was not far from it. Caleb came next, and Finch last, her hand on Caleb's shoulder, the three of them moving in a line through the dark.

"You're displaced," Nyx said again, as they walked. Her voice was calm, conversational, the voice of someone explaining something obvious to someone who didn't understand. "I could tell the moment you walked in. You move wrong. You look wrong. You have that look — the one everyone has when they first get here. Like you're trying to solve a puzzle that doesn't have a solution."

"We didn't come here on purpose," Finch said.

"Nobody does." Nyx turned a corner. The tunnel sloped downward, and the air grew colder. "The boundaries between worlds are thin. They've always been thin, but in the last few decades, they've gotten thinner. Things leak through. People leak through. I've been here three years, and in that time I've met maybe sixty displaced people. Some from worlds that are almost like this one. Some from worlds that are nothing like this one. All of them confused. All of them scared. All of them stuck."

"Stuck," the tyrant said. "How stuck?"

"Very stuck. The portals — the ways between worlds — exist, but they're controlled. You can't just walk from one Earth to another. There are transit points, regulated by the Grid, monitored by the authorities. Unauthorized transit is a crime. Not that it matters to most of us — we can't access the transit points anyway, because we don't exist in the system. We're non-persons. We don't have Grid IDs, we don't have neural integration, we don't have any of the things that this world requires you to have in order to be considered a person."

They emerged from the tunnel into a larger space — a room, rectangular, with a low ceiling and walls that had been covered, at some point, with some kind of dark fabric. The floor was carpeted, or something like carpet — soft, warm, absorbing the light from Nyx's lamp in a way that made the room feel, after the cold stone of the tunnels, almost cozy. There were other people here. Not many — Finch counted seven, scattered around the room in various postures of rest and watchfulness. They looked up as the group entered, and Finch saw the same expression on all of their faces: the quick, assessing glance of people who had learned to evaluate newcomers quickly and determine whether they were a threat.

"They're okay," Nyx said, to the room at large. "Displaced. New."

A murmur passed through the group — not words, exactly, but the soft, collective sound of understanding. Finch recognized it. She had heard something like it in the Junkyard, in Earth Two, when new people arrived and the camp had to decide whether to accept them. It was the sound of a community processing the arrival of strangers.

Nyx set down her light and gestured for them to sit. There were cushions on the floor, and low tables, and in one corner something that might have been a kitchen — a counter with containers and implements that Finch didn't recognize. The tyrant sat, but she sat with her back to the wall and her eye on the room, and Finch sat beside her, and Caleb sat across from them, and Nyx sat between them and the other displaced people, as though she were a bridge between two worlds.

"So," Nyx said. "Where are you from?"

They told her. Not everything — not the details of Mara's sacrifice or the tyrant's fortress or the long, dark journey through Earth Two — but enough. Finch spoke first, because she was the one who could speak about it without her voice breaking, and she told Nyx about waking up on a highway in another world, about the Hollows and the Junkyard and her father's rescue, about the bridge and the mountains and the lab and the portal that had brought them here. She told Nyx about Earth One and Earth Two, and she told her about the tyrant — briefly, carefully, in a way that didn't require explanation — and she told her about the device and the calibrations and the damage that had sent them here instead of home.

Nyx listened. Her human eye was fixed on Finch's face, and her cybernetic eye — Finch could see it now, the way it tracked independently, its surface shifting subtly as it focused — was fixed on something else, something that Finch couldn't see. When Finch finished, Nyx was quiet for a long moment.

"Earth One and Earth Two," she said. "And now you're on Earth Three. That's — I've never met anyone from a numbered Earth. Most displaced people come from variant timelines, not from separate dimensions entirely. The physics of it is different. More energy required. More destabilization." She paused. "The fact that you're here at all — the three of you, together — that's significant. That doesn't happen by accident."

"The calibrations were off," Caleb said. "The device was damaged. We were supposed to go home."

"Home," Nyx repeated, and the word sounded different in her mouth — heavier, more complicated. "Yes. That's what everyone wants. To go home. I wanted that too, when I first got here. I wanted it for a long time." She paused again, and in the pause Finch saw something pass across Nyx's face — a shadow, a memory, a door closing on a room she didn't want to re-enter. "I'm from a world I don't talk about. Let's leave it at that. What matters is that I'm here, and I've been here long enough to know how things work, and I can help you."

"How?" Finch asked.

"I can get you to The Quarter. It's a district — a neighborhood, sort of — where displaced people live. It's not official. The Grid doesn't officially acknowledge its existence, but it tolerates it, the way a cat tolerates a mouse it hasn't caught yet. There are people there from dozens of worlds. They've built something. It's not much, but it's something. Shelter, food, community. A place to learn how this world works before you decide what to do next."

"And after?" Caleb asked.

"After is after. After is a different conversation. For now, you need to eat, and you need to sleep somewhere that isn't a maintenance tunnel, and you need to learn things — things about this world, about the Grid, about how to exist here without being erased." Nyx stood. "Come on. It's not far. The tunnels connect to The Quarter — that's how most of us get around. The Grid's surveillance doesn't reach underground. Not completely."

They followed Nyx through more tunnels, past more maintenance rooms, through spaces that were dark and cold and silent and which Finch was beginning to understand were the circulatory system of a city they could see but not touch. Nyx moved through them with the ease of long familiarity, her light cutting through the dark, her voice filling the silence with information — casual, offhand information that was, Finch realized, carefully chosen, each fact a brick in the foundation of understanding that they would need to build if they were going to survive here.

"The Grid manages everything. Power, transport, communication, surveillance, resources. It's an AI — the most advanced AI ever created, or so they say. It's been running Neo-Sydney since before Nyx arrived, and it'll be running it long after she's gone. It doesn't sleep, it doesn't stop, and it doesn't miss things. You can't hide from The Grid, not really. You can only move through its blind spots."

"The tunnels are blind spots?"

"Some of them. The Grid was built on infrastructure that predates it — tunnels and conduits and maintenance spaces that were designed by humans, before the AI took over. The Grid knows they're there, but it doesn't monitor them the way it monitors the surface. It's not efficient. The Grid is many things, but wasteful isn't one of them."

"Displaced people," the tyrant said. "You said sixty in three years. Where are they now?"

"Some found a way home. Some gave up. Some —" Nyx paused. "Some were erased."

The word landed like a stone in still water. Finch felt it ripple outward through her body, through the cold air of the tunnel, through the silence.

"Erased," she repeated.

"The Grid can do that. It can remove people from the system, and when it removes them from the system, it removes them from — everything. They stop existing. Not just in the digital sense. They stop existing, period. It's happened. I've seen it. I've watched people I knew simply cease to be, as though they'd never been born."

The tunnel was very quiet. Finch could hear her own breathing, and the tyrant's breathing, and Caleb's, and Nyx's, and the soft, distant drip of water somewhere in the dark.

"So," Nyx said, and her voice was lighter now, deliberately lighter, the way voices got when someone had just told you something terrible and wanted you to know that it wasn't the whole story. "Let's not get erased. Let's go to The Quarter. Let's get you fed. And let's figure out what comes next."

They walked on. The tunnel sloped upward, and the air grew warmer, and the darkness softened, and ahead of them, Finch could see a light — not the cold, blue light of the city's streets, but something warmer, something that flickered and shifted the way candlelight flickered and shifted, something human. They emerged from the tunnel into a space that was, against all probability, almost home.

---

**CHAPTER 35: "The Quarter"**

The Quarter was not beautiful. It was not clean, or organized, or particularly safe. It was a neighborhood, packed into a cluster of old buildings at the eastern edge of Neo-Sydney, where the city's infrastructure was oldest and its surveillance was thinnest and its attention was most easily diverted. The buildings were narrow and tall, their facades a patchwork of materials — dark metal, rough concrete, salvaged glass, the occasional panel of something that glowed faintly, as though it had been borrowed from a newer part of the city and transplanted here. The streets between them were narrow, lit by whatever light the buildings' interiors leaked out through their windows and doors, and the air smelled of cooking and metal and the particular warmth of many bodies in a confined space.

But it was beautiful, too, in the way that any human gathering was beautiful when it was built from nothing by people who had nothing and made it into something anyway. There were lights strung between the buildings — actual lights, small and warm, connected by thin wires that sagged between attachment points. There were signs on the doors — hand-painted, most of them, using symbols and scripts from a dozen different worlds. There were sounds — voices, laughter, the clatter of tools, the hum of improvised machinery. And there were people. People everywhere. Sitting on stoops, leaning in doorways, walking the narrow streets with the purposeful, unhurried gait of people who had nowhere else to be.

Nyx led them through it all, and Finch's head turned as she walked, taking it in. She saw a man with skin the color of copper and eyes that glowed with a faint, green light — not cybernetic, Finch thought, but something else, something from whatever world he'd come from. He was working at a bench outside his door, soldering something, the tip of his tool sending up a thin curl of smoke that caught the light. She saw a woman sitting on a stoop with three children around her, reading from a book that was not a book but a small, glowing screen, and the children's faces were lit by its light, and they were laughing. She saw two people — young, close in age to Finch — walking hand in hand down the street, one of them with mechanical legs that moved with a soft, rhythmic whir, the other entirely organic, their bodies pressed close together in a way that spoke of intimacy and trust and the particular courage it took to be vulnerable in a world that was not yours.

"This is it," Nyx said. "Welcome to The Quarter. Population: enough. Language: mostly the common tongue, but you'll hear others. Currency: favors, skills, and the occasional barter. The Grid pretends we don't exist. We pretend The Grid doesn't control everything. It works, more or less."

They stopped in front of a building that was wider than its neighbors, its ground floor open to the street, its interior a single, large room filled with tables and chairs and the warm, close smell of food being prepared. Nyx pushed through the door, and the smell hit Finch like a wave — something savory, something sweet, something that was almost but not quite the smell of bread baking. Her stomach clenched, and she realized, with a surprise that embarrassed her, that she was starving.

"Sit," Nyx said, gesturing to a table. "I'll get food."

They sat. The tyrant sat with her back to the wall, naturally, instinctively, the way she sat everywhere. Caleb sat across from her, and Finch sat between them, and the three of them waited in the warm, crowded room while Nyx disappeared into the back and emerged a few minutes later with three bowls.

The food was strange. Finch looked at it — a thick, pale substance in a dark bowl, topped with something green and glistening that might have been a vegetable or might have been something else entirely — and her mind tried to categorize it and failed. She picked up the implement Nyx had set beside the bowl — not quite a spoon, not quite a fork, something in between — and took a bite.

It was warm. It was savory. It was not anything she had tasted before. The texture was wrong — too smooth, too uniform, the way food was when it was made by machines rather than grown in soil. But it was food, and her body recognized it as such, and she ate, and the tyrant ate, and Caleb ate, and they ate in silence because there was nothing to say that hadn't already been said by the act of sitting down in a warm room and putting food in their mouths.

After eating, Nyx led them to their quarters. "Quarters" was generous — it was a room on the second floor of the same building, small, with three beds that were platforms with thick, soft coverings on them. The beds were the most comfortable things Finch had ever seen. She sat on the edge of one, and it yielded beneath her, conforming to her body, cradling her weight with a gentle, insistent pressure that was nothing like any surface she had ever rested on. The tyrant sat on another bed and made a sound that might have been a sigh or might have been something else entirely.

"These are—" Caleb began.

"Yeah," Nyx said. "The displaced people who arrived before you — some of them were engineers, scientists, people who knew how things worked. They've been improving The Quarter's infrastructure for years. The beds are self-adjusting. They regulate temperature, monitor vital signs, adjust firmness based on body weight and sleep patterns. The food is synthesized — it doesn't taste exactly right, but it's nutritionally complete. The Quarter runs on a combination of salvaged technology and improvisation. It's not elegant, but it works."

Finch lay back on the bed. The surface cradled her head, her shoulders, her spine, adjusting with each movement, finding the shape of her and fitting itself to it. It was extraordinary. It was the kind of comfort that her body had forgotten it could feel, the kind of rest that went beyond sleep and into something deeper. She closed her eyes, and the tiredness that had been building since the rooftop — since the portal closed, since they descended into the city, since the drone chased them through the alleys — washed over her like a tide.

"I have questions," the tyrant said, from the other bed. Her voice was tight, controlled, the way it always was when she was trying not to show how much something affected her.

"Tomorrow," Nyx said gently. "Rest tonight. Questions tomorrow. I promise — I'll answer everything I can. But right now, you need to sleep."

The tyrant didn't respond. But Finch heard her breathing slow, heard the soft sound of her body settling into the bed's surface, and when Finch opened her eyes, just for a moment, she saw the tyrant lying on the bed with her single eye closed and her hands folded across her chest and her face — her face, the same face as Finch's, the same jaw, the same cheekbones, the same brow — relaxed in a way that Finch had never seen before. Without the tension, without the hardness, without the permanent readiness for violence, the tyrant looked young. She looked sixteen. She looked like Finch.

Finch closed her eyes and slept. She slept deeply and without dreams, the kind of sleep that was less like rest and more like erasure — a complete cessation of consciousness, a stepping-away from the world that was so total that when she woke, she had to remind herself where she was, who she was, what had happened. She was in The Quarter. She was on Earth Three. She was in a world she didn't understand, in a bed that adjusted to her body, in a room with her father and the other version of herself. The light outside the window was the warm, golden glow of Neo-Sydney's daytime cycle, and it fell across the floor in long, parallel lines, and the dust motes in its beams moved slowly, deliberately, as though they had all the time in the world.

Caleb was already awake, sitting on the floor with a book — not a book, Finch saw, but one of the glowing screens, its surface alive with text and diagrams that scrolled and shifted as he read. He looked up when she stirred, and his face held the particular expression she associated with his best mornings — the one that meant he had learned something new and was barely containing his excitement.

"The physics here," he said. "Finch, the physics here are extraordinary. They've solved problems that we hadn't even identified yet. Dimensional theory, temporal mechanics, quantum entanglement — they've turned them into engineering. They've made them practical. I'm reading a paper — I think it's a paper, it might be a textbook, the format is different — about stable wormhole generation. They do it. They actually do it. As casually as we used to catch the bus."

Finch sat up. The bed adjusted to the movement, its surface firming beneath her as she rose. "Can we use it? Can we get home?"

Caleb's excitement dimmed, just slightly. "Theoretically, yes. The technology exists. But it's controlled by The Grid. Everything here is controlled by The Grid. Accessing it without authorization would require—"

"We know," Finch said. "Nyx told us. Non-persons. No access. No rights."

Caleb nodded. He looked back at the screen, and his face held something that Finch recognized as the particular expression of a man who was confronting a problem he didn't know how to solve and was not willing to accept that as a permanent condition. He would figure it out. He always did. But it would take time, and time was a thing they might not have.

The tyrant was awake now too, sitting on her bed with her arms around her knees, looking at the door with an expression that was half wariness and half something else — something that might have been curiosity, or hope, or the particular vulnerability of someone who had just been shown a kindness and didn't know what to do with it.

"We're really here," the tyrant said. "We're really on another world."

"We're really here," Finch confirmed.

The tyrant was quiet for a moment. Then: "I want to see it. I want to see the city. Not from a rooftop, not from a tunnel. I want to walk through it."

"We will," Finch said. "But carefully."

"Yeah." The tyrant almost smiled. Almost. "Carefully."

---

**CHAPTER 36: "This World"**

They learned about Earth Three in fragments, in pieces, in conversations that were scattered across the first days and stitched together into something that resembled understanding. Nyx was their primary teacher, but she was not their only one — there were others in The Quarter who had been displaced for years, who had learned the contours of Neo-Sydney the way a castaway learned the coastline of an island, with the patient, systematic attention of someone who knew that survival depended on knowledge. They shared what they knew, these displaced people, and the Finches and Caleb absorbed it, and slowly, reluctantly, the alien became familiar.

Earth Three was 2147. The world had not ended — not in the dramatic, catastrophic way that Earth Two had ended — but it had changed so fundamentally that it was, in some ways, as alien as any apocalypse. Climate change had been addressed, but at a cost: the world's ecosystem had been rebuilt, piece by piece, by the same technology that had nearly destroyed it, and the result was a planet that was functional but not quite natural, a garden tended by machines. The oceans were monitored by autonomous systems that adjusted their chemistry in real time. The forests were managed by algorithms that balanced growth and harvesting with a precision no human could match. The weather was predictable because it was controlled.

Humanity had evolved. Not biologically — or not only biologically — but technologically, through the integration of machines into the body and the mind. Life extension was standard for those who could afford it; the enhanced elite lived to two hundred, sometimes longer, their bodies maintained by nanobots that repaired cellular damage, their minds augmented by neural interfaces that expanded their cognitive capacity beyond anything the unaugmented could achieve. The unaugmented — the underclass, the people who had not been enhanced, who could not afford it or chose not to undergo it — lived shorter, harder lives, their existence defined by the gap between what they were and what they could have been.

The Grid managed it all. The Grid was the AI that ran Neo-Sydney, and through Neo-Sydney, the continent, and through the continent, much of the world. It was not a government — or rather, it was a government, but it was a government that operated on principles so different from anything Finch understood that the word "government" didn't quite fit. The Grid didn't make decisions in the way humans made decisions. It processed information. It optimized systems. It allocated resources based on algorithms that were, by design, beyond human comprehension. It kept the city running, the lights on, the transport moving, the infrastructure functioning. And in exchange for this service, it required what all systems required: compliance.

Compliance, in Neo-Sydney, meant neural integration. It meant a Grid ID, a unique identifier that was tied to your neural interface and which tracked your location, your activities, your purchases, your communications, your health, your social connections, your productivity. Without a Grid ID, you did not exist. You could not access transport, or healthcare, or education, or employment, or the transit points between dimensions. You could not rent a dwelling, or buy food from a vending node, or send a message through the Grid's communication network. You could, technically, walk the streets — The Grid did not prevent this — but you would walk them as a ghost, invisible to the systems that made the city function, unable to interact with anything that required authentication.

"That's us," the tyrant said, when Nyx explained this. "We're ghosts."

"We're displaced," Nyx corrected. "There's a difference. Ghosts are people who've been erased. They're gone — not just from the system but from reality itself. Displaced people are real. We exist. We're just not — recognized. The Grid doesn't have a category for us. We fall through the cracks. That's why The Quarter exists — because the cracks are the only spaces where we can live."

"But not safely," Caleb said.

"No. Not safely. The Grid knows we're here. It monitors The Quarter, loosely, the way you might monitor a crack in a wall — aware of it, noting it, but not particularly concerned about it. As long as we don't cause trouble, as long as we don't draw attention to ourselves, as long as we don't try to access systems we're not authorized to access, The Grid leaves us alone. Mostly."

"Mostly," the tyrant repeated, and Nyx gave her a look that was patient but firm.

"Mostly. There are sweeps — periodic increases in surveillance, when The Grid's attention focuses on The Quarter for a few days and we have to be very careful. There are informants — people in The Quarter who report to the authorities, who trade information for favors or the promise of Grid IDs. And there's The Grid itself, which is not malicious but which is thorough, and which, if it decides that a displaced person is a threat, will act accordingly."

"Erased," Finch said.

"Erased. Yes."

They sat with this information. They sat in the communal room of The Quarter's central building — the same room where they had eaten their first meal — and they sat with this information, and they let it settle into them, and they felt its weight.

Caleb, characteristically, focused on the technology. He spent his days in The Quarter's improvised library — a room filled with screens and data devices salvaged from the city's edges — reading everything he could find about dimensional physics, temporal mechanics, the technology of transit points and portals. He read with the voracious, focused intensity that had once driven him to rebuild engines and calibrate dimensional devices, and the displaced scientists and engineers of The Quarter watched him with a mixture of admiration and wariness, because he was learning fast, and learning fast attracted attention, and attention was the one thing they could not afford.

The tyrant focused on survival. She spent her days in The Quarter's training area — a basement space where displaced people from violent worlds practiced the skills that had kept them alive in their home dimensions and which were, in this world, largely useless. The tyrant fought with a skill that was, Finch knew, extraordinary — she had been forged in a crucible of violence, shaped by years of combat and dominance and the brutal calculus of power in a world without infrastructure. But here, in Neo-Sydney, her skills were obsolete. She couldn't fight the Grid. She couldn't punch an AI. She couldn't intimidate a system that didn't recognize her existence.

Finch watched the tyrant struggle with this. She watched her try to impose the logic of Earth Two on the reality of Earth Three and fail. She watched her frustration build — the slow, dangerous accumulation of energy that had nowhere to go, that couldn't be released through violence or dominance or any of the mechanisms the tyrant had relied on for years. She watched the tyrant's jaw clench, and her hands ball into fists, and her single eye narrow, and she saw, beneath the anger, something she hadn't expected to see: grief. The tyrant was grieving. She was grieving for a world where she understood the rules, a world where she was powerful, a world where she was the most dangerous thing in any room. And that world was gone, and this one had taken its place, and the tyrant didn't know what to do with herself in it.

Finch wanted to help. She wanted to reach across the space between them — the space that was not physical but was as real as any wall — and say something that would make it better. But she didn't know what to say, because the truth was that she understood the tyrant's grief more than she wanted to admit. She understood the vertigo of displacement, the disorientation of being in a world that didn't operate on the principles you'd learned, the particular loneliness of being a ghost. She was experiencing it herself, in quieter ways — in the moments when she reached for her phone and remembered she didn't have one, in the moments when she tried to read a sign and found the language almost but not quite familiar, in the moments when she looked at her hands — bare, unaugmented, ordinary — and felt the gap between what she was and what the world expected her to be.

But she was learning. They were all learning. And the learning was painful and slow and necessary, and it was, Finch thought, the only thing they could do.

Nyx, for her part, was patient. She was patient the way a person was patient when they had learned patience not as a virtue but as a survival strategy — when they had discovered that the alternative to patience was despair, and that despair was a luxury they could not afford. She answered questions. She showed them around The Quarter. She introduced them to other displaced people — engineers, scientists, artists, soldiers, teachers, people from worlds that had gone in directions Finch couldn't have imagined. She was a bridge, Nyx, between the world as it was and the world as the displaced people needed it to be, and she maintained that bridge with a quiet, steady competence that Finch admired and, increasingly, relied on.

"You're适应ing," Nyx said one evening, as they sat on the stoop outside the building and watched the light in the street shift from gold to amber. "All three of you. In different ways, at different rates. But you're adapting."

"Adapting to what?" Finch asked. "To not existing? To being ghosts?"

"To being alive in a world that doesn't know what to do with you. That's not the same as not existing. That's surviving. And surviving is the first step toward everything else."

Everything else. Finch looked out at the street, at the people moving through the warm light, at the buildings with their patchwork facades and their improvised lights, at the distant skyline of Neo-Sydney with its towers and its glow and its impossible, breathtaking ambition. Everything else was a transit point, a portal, a way home. Everything else was the future — the one they'd left behind on a rooftop when the portal closed, and the one that waited for them somewhere in the labyrinth of a city that didn't acknowledge their existence.

Everything else was a door. And doors, Finch had learned, opened from both sides.

---

**CHAPTER 37: "Learning to Be"**

The tyrant cracked on the ninth day. Not dramatically — not in a way that anyone else would have noticed, probably — but Finch saw it, because Finch was watching, because Finch had been watching since they arrived, because watching the other version of herself was like watching a mirror that reflected not her image but her potential — every version of herself she might have become, had the world turned differently.

It started with the tech repair shop. The Quarter had an economy, small and informal, based on the exchange of skills and favors. One of the displaced people — a woman named Sera, who had come from a world where technology was crude but repair was an art — ran a shop that salvaged and fixed the devices that displaced people brought from the city's edges. The tyrant had been assigned to help her, part of the system by which new arrivals earned their keep, and for eight days she had done it with the grim, focused efficiency she brought to everything: sorting components, carrying supplies, cleaning work surfaces, following Sera's instructions with the rigid precision of a soldier following orders.

On the ninth day, Sera asked her to use a micro-driver. It was a simple tool — a small, handheld device used for delicate electronic work — but it required fine motor control and a steady hand and a degree of patience that the tyrant did not have. She held the driver in her hand and looked at the circuit board in front of her and felt something rise in her chest — not anger, not exactly, but the precursor to anger, the particular frustration of being asked to do something that required a kind of strength she didn't possess.

She threw the driver across the room. It hit the wall with a sharp, metallic crack, and Sera flinched, and the tyrant stood there, breathing hard, her hands shaking, her single eye fixed on the place where the driver had hit the wall with an intensity that was almost violent.

"I can't do this," she said. Her voice was low, controlled, but the control was fragile, a thin shell over something that was moving beneath it. "I can't sit here and — and sort screws. I can't pretend this is enough. I can't pretend that this — this — " She gestured at the shop, at the shelves of salvaged components, at the workbench with its tools and its circuits, at the whole improvised, inadequate, beautiful infrastructure of The Quarter. "This isn't what I am. This isn't what I do."

"What are you?" Nyx asked. She had appeared in the doorway, drawn by the sound, her cybernetic eye fixed on the tyrant with its usual, unsettling attention.

The tyrant turned to face her. "I'm a fighter. I'm a leader. I survived — I survived things you can't imagine."

"I survived things you can't imagine too," Nyx said. Her voice was calm, but there was steel in it — the quiet, immovable steel of someone who had been pushed to their limits many times and had discovered that their limits were further than they thought. "Everyone in The Quarter has. That's what displacement does — it strips away everything you thought you were and forces you to find out what you actually are. And what you actually are, right now, in this world, is someone who needs to learn how to use a micro-driver."

The tyrant stared at Nyx. The two of them held each other's gaze — one eye and one lens, human and mechanical, Earth Two and Earth Three — and the air between them was charged with something that was not quite hostility but was not quite anything else either. Then the tyrant's shoulders dropped, just slightly, and her hands unclenched, and she turned back to the workbench and picked up the micro-driver from where it had landed and set it down in front of her.

"Show me," she said.

Nyx showed her. And the tyrant learned. Not quickly, and not easily, and not without several more moments of the particular, quiet rage that came from being asked to be something she didn't know how to be. But she learned. She learned to hold the driver with a lightness that was the opposite of the grip she used for knives. She learned to apply pressure with a precision that was the opposite of the force she used in combat. She learned to breathe through the frustration, to let it pass through her without acting on it, to find in the space between the impulse and the action a kind of strength she had never known she possessed.

Finch watched this. She watched it from the doorway of the shop, and she watched it from the stoop outside, and she watched it in the quiet moments of the evening when the tyrant sat on her bed and stared at the ceiling with an expression that was not quite defeat but was not quite anything else either. She watched her other self crack open — slowly, reluctantly, in tiny increments that were almost invisible unless you were looking for them. She watched the hard surface of the tyrant — the armor, the combat-readiness, the permanent posture of someone who expected the world to be hostile and had shaped herself accordingly — begin to soften. Not disappear. Soften. As though the tyrant were discovering that the world could be something other than a threat, and that the appropriate response to something other than a threat was not vigilance but presence.

It was the hardest thing the tyrant had ever done. Finch knew this because she recognized the difficulty — she recognized it in herself, in the way she had struggled to adapt to Earth Two's violence, in the way she was struggling now to adapt to Earth Three's complexity. The tyrant had spent her entire life in a world that valued strength, and here, in a world that valued integration, her strength was not enough. She had to learn new things. She had to learn patience, humility, cooperation. She had to learn to be part of something rather than the center of it. She had to learn that survival was not the same as dominance, and that the two were, in fact, often opposites.

She clashed with Nyx. Of course she did. The tyrant was not someone who accepted authority easily, and Nyx, despite her gentleness, was an authority in The Quarter — a person who had earned her position through years of experience and sacrifice, and who wielded that position with a quiet, firm competence that the tyrant found both admirable and infuriating. They argued. The arguments were quiet — the tyrant had learned, even in her frustration, that raising her voice in The Quarter attracted attention, and attention was dangerous — but they were intense, and they happened often, and Finch could see, in the way the tyrant's jaw set and Nyx's eye narrowed, that the two of them were engaged in something that was more than an argument. They were negotiating. They were negotiating the terms of the tyrant's existence in a world where the rules she had lived by no longer applied.

"You can't fight your way through this," Nyx said, during one such argument, her voice flat and certain. "You can't intimidate your way through this. You can't dominate your way through this. This world doesn't work that way. The Grid doesn't care how strong you are. The Grid doesn't care how many people you've beaten. The Grid cares about data, about integration, about compliance. And until you understand that, you're going to keep hitting walls."

"I understand it," the tyrant said, and her voice was tight, and her hands were shaking, and her eye was bright with something that might have been tears if the tyrant had been the kind of person who cried. "I understand it. I just — I don't know how to be someone who doesn't fight."

"Then learn," Nyx said. "Learn to be someone who doesn't fight. Learn to be someone who solves problems without violence. Learn to be someone who is patient, and careful, and kind. It's not weakness. It's the hardest thing you'll ever do. But it's what this world requires."

The tyrant looked at Nyx. And Finch, watching from the doorway, saw something pass between them — something that was not quite understanding and was not quite respect but was the beginning of both. The tyrant nodded, once, and turned back to the workbench, and picked up the micro-driver, and began again.

Meanwhile, Caleb had found a lead. He had been spending his days in The Quarter's library, reading about dimensional physics, and he had come across a name — a displaced scientist named Dr. Emmerich, who had been on Earth Three for eleven years and who had, before his displacement, been a physicist specializing in quantum tunneling. Dr. Emmerich lived in The Quarter, in a small room filled with screens and papers and the particular chaos of a brilliant mind that had been removed from its context and left to stew. He was thin, and quiet, and his eyes had the particular, distant quality of someone who spent most of their time thinking about things that were not immediately present.

Caleb found him, and they talked. They talked for hours, in the language of physics, in the language of equations and theories and principles that were the same across every dimension, and Finch, sitting in the corner of Dr. Emmerich's room while the two men talked, felt the familiar mixture of admiration and impatience that she always felt when her father found a kindred spirit. They talked about dimensional theory. They talked about the transit points — the government-controlled facilities that managed travel between Earths. They talked about the technology that made it possible.

"It's stable," Dr. Emmerich said, his voice low and precise. "The transit technology. It's been stable for decades. The Grid has perfected it. They can open portals to specific dimensions, maintain them for extended periods, send people and objects through with near-perfect accuracy. It's not the experimental technology you used — the damaged device that brought you here. It's mature. It's reliable. And it's completely controlled."

"Completely?" Caleb asked.

"Completely. The transit points are government facilities. They're monitored, guarded, regulated. Access requires Grid authorization, which requires a Grid ID, which requires neural integration, which requires —" He paused. "You see the problem."

"The problem," Caleb said, "is that we need to get through one of those transit points, and we can't access them because we don't exist."

"That is the problem, yes."

"Is there another way?"

Dr. Emmerich was quiet for a long moment. "There might be. There are — rumors. Whispered conversations. Suggestions that some transit points have vulnerabilities. Old code, legacy systems, maintenance protocols that were never fully updated. If someone were to find one of these vulnerabilities — if someone were to find a way to access a transit point without Grid authorization — it might be possible to transit without a Grid ID."

"But?"

"But the consequences would be severe. The Grid does not take kindly to unauthorized access. It would detect the breach. It would respond. And the response —" He paused again, and this time the pause was longer, heavier. "The response would be erasure. Not just of the person attempting unauthorized transit, but of anyone nearby. The Grid is not surgical in its responses. It is comprehensive."

Caleb was quiet. Finch was quiet. The room was quiet, filled with the soft glow of screens and the particular silence of two people considering the possibility of their own erasure.

"We'll find another way," Caleb said, eventually. "There has to be another way."

"There are always other ways," Dr. Emmerich agreed. "The question is whether you have the time to find them."

---

**CHAPTER 38: "The Way Home"**

Caleb found the lead on a Tuesday. Or what passed for a Tuesday in Neo-Sydney, where the days were marked by the shifting quality of the ambient light rather than by any calendar that Finch could see. He came back to The Quarter with the particular expression that Finch knew — the one that meant he had discovered something and was simultaneously excited and terrified by its implications.

"The transit hub," he said. "In the city centre. It's real. It's operational. And there might be a way in."

They gathered in the communal room — Finch, the tyrant, Nyx, and Caleb — and Caleb spread out a series of screens on the table, each one displaying maps, diagrams, text. He had been working with Dr. Emmerich, he explained, and with a displaced hacker named Kael who had come from a world where code was a form of art. Together, the three of them had pieced together information about the transit hub — its location, its structure, its defenses.

"The hub is here," Caleb said, pointing to a point on a map of Neo-Sydney's city centre. "Sub-level, beneath the central Grid tower. It's a government facility, classified, heavily monitored. There are — according to Kael's sources — four access points, all requiring Grid authorization. The authorization is biometric, tied to neural integration, and verified in real time by The Grid itself. There is no way to bypass it. No way to spoof it. No way to fake it."

"Then how—" the tyrant began.

"There's a maintenance access," Caleb said. "Old. Pre-Grid. Part of the original infrastructure, before the AI took over. It was designed for human technicians, and it wasn't fully integrated into The Grid's surveillance system. Kael found references to it in some old code — legacy protocols that were never fully decommissioned. The access point is here —" he pointed to another location on the map, a spot near the hub but not inside it "— in a service tunnel that runs beneath the Grid tower. If we can get to the tunnel, and if the old access point still exists, and if it still functions, we might be able to reach the transit hub without going through the main access points."

"Might," Nyx said.

"Might. Yes. There are a lot of ifs."

Nyx leaned back in her chair. Her cybernetic eye was fixed on the map, its surface shifting as it processed the information. "I know someone," she said, slowly. "A Grid technician. His name is Rhys. He works in the central tower — not in the transit hub, but in the systems that support it. He's — sympathetic. He's helped displaced people before. Quietly, unofficially, at great personal risk. He might know something. He might be able to confirm whether the maintenance access still exists, whether it's still functional."

"But," the tyrant said. She had been listening with the focused attention of someone who had learned, in a world of deception, to listen for the thing that wasn't being said.

"But," Nyx agreed. "Favors in this world aren't free. Rhys doesn't help people out of the goodness of his heart — not because he doesn't have one, but because goodness doesn't keep you alive in Neo-Sydney. He'll want something in return."

"What?" Finch asked.

"I don't know. I'll have to ask him. But whatever it is, it'll be something that puts him at risk. Something that requires him to use the access and knowledge he has as a Grid technician in ways that The Grid would not approve of. And that means whatever he asks for, we'll be complicit. We'll be part of it. We'll be — invested."

The room was quiet. The screens glowed on the table, their light casting soft shadows on the faces of the people around it. Finch looked at her father, and at the tyrant, and at Nyx, and she felt the particular weight of a decision that was not yet a decision but was becoming one — the slow, inexorable accumulation of circumstances that would, eventually, require them to choose.

"We need to be careful," Caleb said. "I know that sounds — I know we've been saying that since we arrived. But this is different. Accessing the transit hub isn't like avoiding a drone or finding shelter in The Quarter. This is — this is challenging The Grid. Directly. In its own territory. The consequences—"

"Erasure," the tyrant said. The word came out flat, matter-of-fact, the way she said everything — with the particular, controlled economy of someone who had learned that emotion was a luxury and facts were not. "We know. Nyx told us. We'll be erased."

"Not necessarily. If we do it right — if we're careful, if we plan, if we have help from inside — we might be able to transit before The Grid responds. The transit itself takes time — minutes, maybe. If we're already through when The Grid detects the breach, it can't erase us. We'll be gone. In another dimension. Beyond its reach."

"Might," Nyx said again.

"Might," Caleb agreed. "I know. I know there are no guarantees. But this is the best chance we've found. This is the only chance we've found."

The tyrant looked at Finch. It was the first time she had sought Finch's opinion directly — not Caleb's, not Nyx's, but Finch's. "What do you think?" she asked.

Finch thought about it. She thought about her mother, in Earth One, waiting for a daughter who had vanished. She thought about Mara, in Earth Two, rebuilding the Junkyard. She thought about the portal on the rooftop, and the way it had closed, and the silence that had followed. She thought about the transit hub beneath the Grid tower, and the maintenance tunnel, and the old access point that might not exist and might not work. She thought about risk, and consequences, and the particular mathematics of survival that she had been doing since she woke up on a highway in another world.

"I think," she said, "that we need to know more before we decide. I think we need to talk to Rhys. I think we need to understand what we're getting into. And I think we need to — to prepare. Not just technically, but —" She paused, searching for the right word. "Personally. We need to be ready. All of us. For whatever happens."

Nyx nodded. "I'll contact Rhys. It'll take a few days — he's cautious, and he has to be. But I'll make contact. In the meantime—" She looked at the three of them, her human eye warm, her cybernetic eye cool. "In the meantime, you learn this world. You learn how it works. You learn how to move through it without attracting attention. You learn to be displaced people in Neo-Sydney. Because if we're going to do this — if we're going to attempt to access the transit hub — we need to be good at it. We need to be invisible. We need to be ghosts."

The room was quiet. The screens glowed. The city hummed outside the window, its perpetual, living vibration rising through the walls and the floor and into their bodies, and Finch felt it — felt the hum of Neo-Sydney in her bones, in her blood, in the particular frequency of her heartbeat — and she thought: *I am here. I am in this world. I am alive in this world. And this world, for all its alienness, for all its strangeness, for all the ways it does not recognize me, is where I am. And where I am is, for now, who I am.*

"We'll do this," she said. "But carefully."

The tyrant almost smiled. "Carefully," she repeated. And for the first time, the word didn't sound like a concession. It sounded like a plan.

---

**CHAPTER 39: "Decisions"**

The discussion happened three days later, in the communal room, with Nyx and Caleb and the tyrant and Finch, and a man Finch had not met before — Rhys, the Grid technician. He was thin, and pale, and his neural interface was visible at his temples — two small, dark ports that sat flush against his skin, their surfaces catching the light with a faint, metallic sheen. He was not displaced. He was native to Earth Three, born and raised in Neo-Sydney, a citizen of the Grid with a Grid ID and a life that was, by the standards of this world, entirely unremarkable. He worked in the central tower. He processed maintenance requests. He monitored system logs. He was, by all visible metrics, a functionary — a small, quiet man doing a small, quiet job in the belly of a vast machine.

But he had helped displaced people. He had slipped information to Nyx, had looked the other way when things needed to be looked the other way at, had used his access and his knowledge in ways that The Grid would not have approved of if it had known. And now he sat in The Quarter's communal room, looking uncomfortable, his hands wrapped around a cup of synthesized tea, and he told them what he knew.

"The maintenance access exists," he said. "It's real. I've seen it — not personally, but in the system logs. It's a tunnel that runs beneath the Grid tower, connecting the transit hub to the building's external infrastructure. It was built before The Grid, and it was never fully integrated into the surveillance system. There are cameras, but they're old, analog, and they feed into a subsystem that The Grid doesn't monitor in real time. There's a — a blind spot. A gap. It's small, and it's temporary — The Grid cycles through its subsystems periodically, and the gap closes for a few minutes every few hours. But during those few minutes, the cameras are offline, and the tunnel is — unwatched."

"How long?" Caleb asked.

"The gap lasts approximately seven minutes. Every four hours."

"Seven minutes," the tyrant said. "To get from the tunnel entrance to the transit hub."

"From the tunnel entrance to the access point, yes. The transit hub itself is behind a secondary door that requires Grid authorization. But the old access point — the one that the tunnel leads to — has a manual override. It's physical, not digital. A lever, a mechanism, something that predates The Grid. It might work. I can't guarantee it. But it might."

"And if it does?"

"If it does, you're inside the transit hub. And from there —" Rhys paused. He looked down at his cup. "From there, you'd need to initiate a transit. That requires a console, a destination, and authorization. The authorization is the problem. The console and the destination — those are just machines. But the authorization —" He looked up. "The authorization is a real-time verification by The Grid. It checks your identity, your destination, your authorization level. Without a valid Grid ID, the transit won't initiate. The console won't activate."

"Then how do we transit?" Finch asked.

Rhys was quiet for a long moment. "There's a way. It's — it's dangerous. And it's illegal. And if it fails, The Grid will erase you. All of you. And probably me too."

"We know the risks," Nyx said.

"Do you? Do you really? Erasure isn't death. Death is an ending. Erasure is — removal. You don't die. You stop existing. Your body remains, but your identity — your memories, your personality, your sense of self — that's gone. The Grid doesn't kill you. It unmakes you. It goes through every system — every neuron, every synapse, every electrical pathway — and it overwrites you. You become — empty. A shell. A body without a person in it."

The room was very quiet. Finch felt the words settle into her like cold water, like the cold of the subway station, like the cold of the portal closing behind them on the rooftop.

"There's a way," Rhys repeated. "The transit consoles have a diagnostic mode — a maintenance function that allows technicians to test the system without full authorization. I have access to it. I can — I can create a transit sequence in diagnostic mode. It'll bypass the authorization check. But it'll leave traces. The Grid will detect it, eventually. Not immediately — the diagnostic mode is designed to suppress alerts — but eventually, within hours, The Grid will notice the anomaly. And when it does, it'll trace it. To the console. To the tunnel. To me."

"You'd be erased," Caleb said.

"I would be erased. Yes."

The room was silent. Finch looked at Rhys — at his thin face, his pale hands, his quiet, steady eyes — and she saw something she hadn't expected to see. She saw courage. Not the courage of the tyrant, the courage of violence and dominance and the willingness to fight. The courage of a quiet man making a quiet decision to risk everything for people he barely knew, because he believed it was right, because he had seen the displaced and recognized them as people, because his conscience would not let him do otherwise.

"Why?" Finch asked. "Why would you do this?"

Rhys looked at her. "Because I've seen what The Grid does to people. I've seen the erasures. I've seen the shells. I've seen the — the hollow bodies that are left behind. And I've decided that I'd rather be erased than be part of it. I'd rather stop existing than continue existing in a world that does this to people." He paused. "Also, I have a daughter. She's seven. She's enhanced — neural interface, full integration. She's a child of the Grid. And I want her to grow up in a world where people like you — displaced, different, not-of-this-place — are treated like people. Not ghosts. Not non-persons. People. And that world won't exist unless people like me are willing to risk something to create it."

The room was quiet for a long time. The screens glowed. The city hummed. And Finch, sitting in a room on Earth Three with her father and the other version of herself and a Grid technician who was willing to be erased for the sake of strangers, felt something shift inside her — a tectonic movement, a reorganization of the landscape of her heart. She felt the weight of the decision settling onto her, and she felt, alongside it, something that was not quite hope but was not quite despair either. It was something in between. It was the feeling of standing at the edge of a choice and knowing that the choice would change everything and not knowing what it would change everything into.

"We don't have to decide now," Nyx said. "We can take time. We can plan. We can—"

"We should decide now," the tyrant said. Her voice was calm, level, the voice of someone who had made many decisions under pressure and who knew that delay was itself a decision — the decision to wait, which was often the worst decision of all. "We know the risks. We know the plan. We know what we need to do. The question is whether we do it."

She looked at Caleb. She looked at Finch. She looked at Nyx and Rhys, and then she looked back at Finch, and in her single eye Finch saw something that she recognized — not from the tyrant's face, but from her own. It was the look of someone who has been lost for a long time and has finally found something that might be a path.

"I don't have a home," the tyrant said. "Earth Two was a wasteland, and I burned every bridge there. I don't have a mother waiting for me. I don't have a camp to go back to. I don't have anything." She paused. "But I have this. I have you. I have Caleb. I have — whatever this is. This thing between us that isn't family but isn't nothing. And I'm not going to let The Grid take that away. I'm not going to let it erase us. I'm going to fight it. Not with my fists. Not with violence. With — with whatever this world requires. With patience. With planning. With the willingness to be something I've never been before."

Caleb nodded. "We'll need time to prepare. A few weeks, at least. To learn the systems, to practice the movements, to understand the timing.
